transition systems
with
underlying graphs

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}=(V,E,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Source}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Target}},I)
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}^{\prime}=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Source}}^{\prime},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Target}}^{\prime},I^{\prime})
, and
acceptance conditions

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}=(\gamma,\Gamma,\mathbb{W})
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}^{\prime}=(\gamma^{\prime},\Gamma^{\prime},\mathbb{W}^{\prime})
.

3.1
Morphisms of transition systems

Definition 3.1
.

A
morphism of graphs
from
G
to
G^{\prime}
is a pair of mappings
\varphi=(\varphi_{V}:V\rightarrow V^{\prime},\varphi_{E}:E\rightarrow E^{\prime})
preserving edges, that is:

•

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Source}}^{\prime}(\varphi_{E}(e))=\varphi_{V}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Source}}(e))
for every
e\in E
,

•

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Target}}^{\prime}(\varphi_{E}(e))=\varphi_{V}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Target}}(e))
for every
e\in E
.

We say that
\varphi
is a
morphism of pointed graphs
if, moreover, it preserves
initial vertices
:

•

\varphi_{V}(v_{0})\in I^{\prime}
for every
v_{0}\in I
.

If
(G,(l_{V},L_{V}),(l_{E},L_{E}))
and
(G,(l^{\prime}_{V},L^{\prime}_{V}),(l^{\prime}_{E},L^{\prime}_{E}))
are
labelled graphs
, we say that
\varphi
is a
morphism of labelled graphs
if, in addition,
L_{V}\subseteq L_{V}^{\prime}
,
L_{E}\subseteq L_{E}^{\prime}
and
\varphi
preserves labels:

•

l_{V}^{\prime}(\varphi_{V}(v))=l_{V}(v)
for every
v\in V
,

•

l_{E}^{\prime}(\varphi_{E}(e))=l_{E}(e)
for every
e\in V
.

We will write
\varphi\colon G\to G^{\prime}
to denote a
morphism

\varphi
. We will drop the subscript in
\varphi_{V}
and
\varphi_{E}
whenever it can be deduced from its use.
We say that
\varphi
is
surjective
(resp. injective) if
\varphi_{V}
is.

Note that the mapping
\varphi_{V}
does not completely determine a
morphism

\varphi
, as multiple edges might exist between two given vertices. However, if
G
has no isolated vertices, the mapping
\varphi_{E}
does determine it. It will be convenient nonetheless to also keep the notation for
\varphi_{V}
.

We remark that the image of a
run
in
G
by a
morphism of pointed graphs
is a
run
in
G^{\prime}
. Therefore, a
morphism of pointed graphs

\varphi\colon G\to G^{\prime}
induces a mapping

\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\varphi}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Run}^{\infty}(G)}\to{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Run}^{\infty}(G^{\prime})}.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
and
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
be two (
labelled
)
transition systems
. A
weak morphism of (labelled) transition systems

\varphi\colon\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}\to\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
is a
morphism of (labelled) pointed graphs
between their
underlying graphs
,
\varphi:G\to G^{\prime}
.
We say that it is a
morphism of (labelled) transition systems
if it
preserves the acceptance
of
runs
, that is:

•

for every
infinite run

\rho\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Run}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS})}
,
\gamma(\rho)\in\mathbb{W}\;\iff\;\gamma^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\varphi}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}(\rho))\in\mathbb{W}^{\prime}
.

A
morphism of labelled TS
between
automata
(resp. between
games
) will be called a
morphism of automata
(resp.
morphism of games
).

We say that a
morphism of TS

\varphi\colon\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}\to\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
is an
isomorphism
if
\varphi_{V}
and
\varphi_{E}
are bijective and
\varphi^{-1}=(\varphi_{V}^{-1},\varphi_{E}^{-1})
is a
morphism
from
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
. In that case, we say that
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
and
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
are
isomorphic
.

3.2
Local properties of morphisms

Definition 3.3
.

A
morphism of pointed graphs

\varphi:G\rightarrow G^{\prime}
is called:

•

Locally surjective
if it verifies:

1.

For every
v_{0}^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}
there exists
v_{0}\in I
such that
\varphi(v_{0})=v_{0}^{\prime}
.

2.

For every
v\in V
and every
e^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Out}}(\varphi(v))

there exists
e\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Out}}(v)
such that
\varphi(e)=e^{\prime}
.

•

Locally injective
if it verifies:

1.

For every
v_{0}^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}
, there is at most one
v_{0}\in I
such that
\varphi(v_{0})=v_{0}^{\prime}
.

2.

For every
v\in V
and every couple
e_{1},e_{2}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Out}}(v)
,
\varphi(e_{1})=\varphi(e_{2})
implies
e_{1}=e_{2}
.

•

Locally bijective
if it is both
locally surjective
and
locally injective
.

Equivalently, a
morphism of pointed graphs

\varphi
is
locally surjective
(resp.
locally injective
) if for every
v\in V
the restriction of
\varphi_{E}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Out}}(v)
is a surjection onto
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Out}}(\varphi(v))
(resp. an injection into
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Out}}(\varphi(v))
), and the restriction of
\varphi_{V}
to
I
is a surjection onto
I^{\prime}
(resp. an injection into
I^{\prime}
).

Let
\varphi:\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}\rightarrow\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
be a
(weak) morphism
, and let
\rho^{\prime}=v_{0}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{e_{0}^{\prime}}v_{1}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{e_{1}^{\prime}}\dots
be a
run
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
. If
\varphi
is
locally surjective
, we can pick an initial vertex
v_{0}
in
\varphi^{-1}(v_{0}^{\prime})
and build step-by-step a run
\rho
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
from
v_{0}
that is sent to
\rho^{\prime}
under
\varphi
. If
\varphi
is moreover
locally bijective
, the choices of the
initial vertex
and the edges at each step are unique, so
runs
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
can be simulated in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
via
\varphi
in a unique way.
Said differently, if
\varphi:\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}\rightarrow\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
is a
locally bijective morphism
, we can see
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
as an
automaton
that processes
runs
of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
in a deterministic fashion (this idea is formalised in Section
5.4.3
).
This property will allow us to show that a
locally bijective

morphism
witnesses the semantic equivalence of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
and
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
(see Section
3.4
).

We note that the notion of
locally bijective

morphisms of transition systems
almost coincide with the usual concept of bisimulation. The main difference is that
locally bijective morphisms
treat the
acceptance
of a run as a whole; we do not impose the
output colour
of an edge
\gamma(e)
to coincide with the
colour

\gamma^{\prime}(\varphi(e))
. This allows us to compare
transition systems
using different types of
acceptance conditions
.

Remark 3.4
.

Let
\varphi
be a
morphism of pointed graphs
.

1.

If
\varphi
is
locally surjective
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\varphi}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}
is surjective.

2.

If
\varphi
is
locally injective
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\varphi}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}
is injective.

3.

If
\varphi
is
locally bijective
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\varphi}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}
is bijective.

In the following, the
weak morphisms
under consideration will be
locally surjective
. The next lemma ensures that we can assume that they are
surjective
without loss of generality.

Lemma 3.5
.

If
\varphi\colon\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}\to\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
is a
locally surjective

weak morphism
, it is onto the
accessible part
of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
. That is, for every
accessible
state
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
, there exists some state
v\in\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
such that
\varphi_{V}(v)=v^{\prime}
. In particular, if every state of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
is
accessible
,
\varphi
is
surjective
.

Proof 3.6
.

Let
v^{\prime}
be an accessible state of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
. By definition, there exists a
finite run

\rho^{\prime}
from an initial vertex of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
to
v^{\prime}
. By surjectivity of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\varphi}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}
, there is a finite
run

\rho\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Run}^{\mathsf{fin}}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS})}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\varphi}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}(\rho)=\rho^{\prime}
. As
\varphi
is a
morphism of graphs
, we have that
\varphi({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Target}}(\rho))=v^{\prime}
.

Example 3.7
.

In Figure
6
we provide an example of a
locally bijective morphism
between the two rightmost
transition systems
from Figure
3
(we have removed
input letters
for simplicity).
We recall that the
acceptance set
of the rightmost
transition system
is the
Muller language associated to

\mathcal{F}=\{\{\alpha\},\{\beta\}\}
.
The morphism is given by
\varphi_{V}(v_{1})=\varphi_{V}(v_{2})=v^{\prime}
and
\varphi_{V}(v_{2})=v_{2}^{\prime}
. In this case, the mapping
\varphi_{V}
determines a unique
morphism
; the (uniquely determined) mapping
\varphi_{E}
is represented by the colours of the edges in the figure.
It is easy to check that this mapping
preserves the acceptance
of
runs
and that it is
locally bijective
.

Figure 6
:
A
locally bijective morphism
from a
parity

TS
to a
Muller

TS
with
acceptance set
given by
\mathcal{F}=\{\{\alpha\},\{\beta\}\}
. We use dashed arrows to represent the images of vertices, and colours to represent the image of edges (that can be inferred from
\varphi_{V}
).

3.3
History-deterministic mappings

Locally bijective morphisms
are a natural generalisation of the
composition
of a
transition system
with a
deterministic

automaton
. They guarantee the semantic equivalence of the two involved
transition systems
, but at the cost of the use of some strong hypothesis, as the outgoing edges of a vertex
v
must exactly correspond to the outgoing edges of its image
\varphi(v)
.
We can imagine correct transformations that do not satisfy this requirement.
Notably,
history-deterministic

automata
have been introduced as a method to bypass this restriction, with the hope of outperforming transformations that are witnessed by
locally bijective morphisms
.
In general, if
\mathcal{A}
is an
HD

automaton

recognising
the
acceptance set
ot
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
, the
composition

\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\ltimes}}\mathcal{A}
does not admit a
locally bijective morphism
to
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
, although it shares most semantic properties with it (Proposition
2.7
).

We introduce next
HD mappings
, which are
weak morphisms
with the minimal set of hypothesis ensuring that, if
\varphi\colon\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}\to\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
is an
HD mapping
, we can
simulate

runs
of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
via
\varphi
while preserving their
acceptance
. This will allow us to show that
\varphi
witnesses the semantic equivalence of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
and
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
(Section
3.4
).

Figure 7
:
Different types of morphisms and the relations between them. The fact that
locally surjective morphisms
are
HD mappings
is given by Lemma
3.14
. Note that
HD mappings
are also
locally surjective

weak morphisms
(Remark
3.8
).

History-deterministic mappings.

Let
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
and
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
be
transition systems
and
\varphi:\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}\to\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
a
weak morphism
between them.
A
resolver simulating
\varphi
consists in a pair of functions
\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}r_{\mathsf{Init}}}\colon I^{\prime}\to I
and
r\colon E^{*}\times E^{\prime}\to E
such that:

1.

\varphi({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}}(v_{0}^{\prime}))=v_{0}^{\prime}
for all
v_{0}^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}
,

2.

\varphi(r(\rho,e^{\prime}))=e^{\prime}
, for all
\rho\in E^{*}
and
e^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
,

3.

if
e_{0}^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Out}}(I^{\prime})
,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Source}}(r(\varepsilon,e_{0}^{\prime}))={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Source}}(e_{0}^{\prime}))
, and

4.

if
\rho
is a finite run in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
ending in
v
and
e^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Out}}(\varphi(v))
, then
r(\rho,e^{\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Out}}(v)
.

Given a
run

\rho^{\prime}=e_{0}^{\prime}e_{1}^{\prime}\dots\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Run}^{\infty}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime})}
starting in some
v_{0}^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}
, the
run induced by

r
is the sequence
\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}r}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}(\rho^{\prime})=e_{0}e_{1}e_{2}\dots\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Run}^{\infty}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS})}
defined by
e_{i}=r(e_{0}\dots e_{i-1},e_{i}^{\prime})
, which is indeed a
run
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
.
We say that the
resolver
is
sound
if for every
accepting run

\rho^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Run}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime})}
, the
run

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}(\rho^{\prime})
is
accepting
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
.
Note that we do not impose
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}(\rho^{\prime})
to be
rejecting
if
\rho^{\prime}
is.

Remark 3.8
.

Provided that all states of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
are
accessible
, a
resolver simulating
\varphi
can only exist if
\varphi
is a
locally surjective

weak morphism
.

Said differently, a
sound

resolver simulating

\varphi
is a winning strategy for the player Duplicator in the following game:

•

In round
0
, Spoiler picks an
initial vertex

v_{0}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
. Duplicator responds by picking an
initial vertex

v_{0}
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
such that
\varphi(v_{0})=v_{0}^{\prime}
.

•

In round
n>0
, Spoiler picks an edge
e_{n}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
, and Duplicator responds by picking an edge
e_{n}
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
such that
\varphi(e_{n})=e_{n}^{\prime}
.

•

Duplicator wins if either
e_{1}e_{2}\dots
is an
accepting run
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
from
v_{0}
or
e_{1}^{\prime}e_{2}^{\prime}\dots
is not an
accepting run
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
from
v_{0}^{\prime}
(it is either not a
run from
v_{0}
or not
accepting
). Spoiler wins otherwise.

Definition 3.9
.

Let
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
and
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
be (
labelled
)
transition systems
.
A
history-deterministic mapping
(HD mapping) of transition systems from
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
to
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
is a pair of mappings
\varphi=(\varphi_{V}:V\rightarrow V^{\prime},\varphi_{E}:E\rightarrow E^{\prime})
such that:

•

\varphi
is a
weak morphism
,

•

\varphi

preserves accepting runs
:
\rho\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Run}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS})}
and
\gamma(\rho)\in\mathbb{W}\;\implies\;\gamma^{\prime}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\varphi}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}(\rho))\in\mathbb{W}^{\prime}
, and

•

there exists a
sound

resolver simulating

\varphi
.

Even if a
history-deterministic mapping
is not necessarily
locally bijective
(and not even a
morphism of transition systems
), the existence of a
sound

resolver
allows us to define a right inverse to
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\varphi}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}
preserving the
acceptance
of
runs
.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
\varphi\colon\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}\to\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
be an
HD mapping
and let
({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}},r)
be a
sound

resolver simulating
it. The following holds:

•

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\varphi}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}\circ{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathit{Id}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Run}^{\infty}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime})}}}
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}
preserves the
acceptance
of
runs
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
, that is, for every
run

\rho^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Run}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime})}
,
\rho^{\prime}
is
accepting
if and only if
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}(\rho^{\prime})
is
accepting
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
.

Proof 3.11
.

The first item follows from the fact that
\varphi(r(\rho,e^{\prime}))=e^{\prime}
for every
\rho\in E^{*}
and
e^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
.

For the second item, the definition of a
sound resolver
imposes that if
\rho^{\prime}
is
accepting
, so is
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}(\rho^{\prime})
. For the other direction, if
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}(\rho^{\prime})
is
accepting
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\varphi}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}(\rho^{\prime}))=\rho^{\prime}
has to be
accepting
, as an
HD mapping

preserves accepting runs
.

Example 3.12
.

In Figure
8
we give an example of a
weak morphism

\varphi\colon\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}\to\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
that is a
history-deterministic mapping
, but which is neither a
morphism
, nor
locally bijective
.

Transition system

\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
, on the left of the figure, is a
parity

TS
(more precisely, a
coBüchi

TS
).

Transition system

\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
, depicted on the right of the figure, is a
Muller

TS
using as
acceptance set
the
Muller language associated to

\mathcal{F}=\{\{\alpha\},\{\alpha,\beta\},\{\alpha,\lambda\}\}
; that is, a
run
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
is
accepting
if and only if it eventually avoids either transition
e^{\prime}
or transition
f^{\prime}
.
The
weak morphism
we propose is given by:
\varphi(v_{0})=\varphi(v_{1})=\varphi(v_{2})=v^{\prime}
, and
\varphi(u_{1})=\varphi(u_{2})=u^{\prime}
. The image of most edges is uniquely determined, and we use colours to represent them. We have named the only edges whose image is not uniquely determined, and we define
\varphi(e_{1})=\varphi(e_{2})=e^{\prime}
and
\varphi(f_{1})=\varphi(f_{2})=f^{\prime}
.

Figure 8
:
A
history-deterministic mapping
from a
parity

TS
to a
Muller

TS
with
acceptance set
given by
\mathcal{F}=\{\{\alpha\},\{\alpha,\beta\},\{\alpha,\lambda\}\}
. We use dashed arrows to represent the images of vertices, and colours to represent the image of edges.

We remark that
\varphi
does not
preserve rejecting runs
. Indeed, a run in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
alternating between
v_{0}
and
u_{1}
, taking transition
f_{1}
infinitely often, is
rejecting
, but its image is
accepting
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
.
However,
\varphi

preserves accepting runs
: a
run
is
accepting
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
if and only if it eventually stays in
\{v_{1},u_{1}\}
or in
\{v_{2},u_{2}\}
. In the first case, the image under
\varphi
avoids transition
f^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
, and in the second case, its image avoids transition
e^{\prime}
.

Finally, we describe a
sound

resolver simulating
\varphi
. When simulating a run from
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
, we have a choice to make only when we are in state
v_{0}
. If the previous transition in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
was
e^{\prime}
, we will go up, that is,
v^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\alpha}u^{\prime}
is simulated by
v_{0}\xrightarrow{1}u_{1}
and
v^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\alpha}v^{\prime}
is simulated by
v_{0}\xrightarrow{1}v_{1}
.
If the previous transition in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
was
f^{\prime}
, we will go down symmetrically.
In this way, if transition
f^{\prime}
is eventually not visited by the run in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
, we ensure to stay in
\{v_{1},u_{1}\}
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
(and symmetrically, we ensure to stay in
\{v_{2},u_{2}\}
if
e^{\prime}
is avoided in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
).

History-deterministic-for-games mappings.

In the case of
games
, we need to slightly strengthen the definition of
HD mappings
to guarantee that, if there is a suitable mapping
\varphi\colon\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, then
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
have the same
winner
.
In order to show that if
Eve
wins
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
then she
wins

\mathcal{G}
, we need a method to transfer
strategies
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{G}
. A regular
resolver simulating
\varphi
does not suffice to do this, as it does not take into account the partition into
Eve
and
Adam
vertices.
We need to be able to simulate a
play
of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{G}
in a two-players-game fashion,
Adam’s
moves will be simulated by
Adam
, and
Eve’s
moves by
Eve
. This idea leads to the notion of
HD-for-games mapping
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
be two
games
, and
\varphi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
be a
weak morphism
between them admitting a
resolver

({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}},r)

simulating

\varphi
.
Given
runs

\rho^{\prime}=e_{0}^{\prime}e_{1}^{\prime}\dots\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Run}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})}
and
\rho=e_{0}e_{1}\dots\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Run}(\mathcal{G})}
, we say that
\rho
is
consistent with

({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}},r)
over
\rho^{\prime}
if:

1.

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Source}}(e_{0})={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Source}}(e_{0}^{\prime})
,

2.

\varphi(e_{i})=e_{i}^{\prime}
, and

3.

for every finite prefix
e_{0}e_{1}\dots e_{n-1}\rho
ending in a vertex
controlled by Eve
, the next edge in
\rho
is
e_{n}=r(e_{0}\dots e_{n-1},e_{n}^{\prime})
.

We remark that there exists at least one run
consistent with

({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}},r)
over
\rho^{\prime}
, namely
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}(\rho^{\prime})
.
We say that
({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}},r)
is
sound for
\mathcal{G}
if it verifies that for any
accepting run

\rho^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Run}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})}
, all runs
consistent with

({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}},r)
over
\rho^{\prime}
are
accepting
in
\mathcal{G}
.

Said differently, a
resolver sound for
\mathcal{G}
is a winning strategy for Duplicator in the following game:

•

In round
0
, Spoiler picks an
initial vertex

v_{0}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. Duplicator responds by picking an
initial vertex

v_{0}
in
\mathcal{G}
such that
\varphi(v_{0})=v_{0}^{\prime}
.

•

In round
n>0
, Spoiler picks an edge
e_{n}^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. If
v_{n-1}
is controlled by
Adam
, Spoiler chooses an edge
e_{n}=v_{n-1}\xrightarrow{}v_{n}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Out}}(v_{n-1})
such that
\varphi(e_{n})=e_{n}^{\prime}
. If
v_{n}
is controlled by
Eve
, it is Duplicator who chooses one such
e_{n}
.

•

Duplicator wins if either
e_{1}e_{2}\dots
is an
accepting run
in
\mathcal{G}
from
v_{0}
or
e_{1}^{\prime}e_{2}^{\prime}\dots
is not an
accepting run
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
from
v_{0}^{\prime}
. Spoiler wins otherwise.

Definition 3.13
.

An
HD mapping

of games

\varphi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is called
history-deterministic-for-games
if it admits a
resolver

sound for
\mathcal{G}
.

Whenever we apply the term
HD-for-games
to a map
\varphi:\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}\to\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
, it will implicitly imply that
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
and
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
are
games
(that is, they have a fixed
vertex-labelling

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}l}_{\mathsf{Players}}\colon V\to\{\mathrm{Eve},\mathrm{Adam}\}
), and that
\varphi
preserves those
vertex-labellings
).

In the next lemma, we prove that
HD
and
HD-for-games
mappings are a strict generalisation of
locally surjective morphisms
(and therefore, also of
locally bijective
ones). On the other hand, we remark that
HD mappings
must be
locally surjective
, but they are not necessarily
morphisms
(they might not
preserve rejecting runs
).

Lemma 3.14
.

If
\varphi:\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}\to\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
is a
locally surjective morphism
, it is also an
HD mapping
. If
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
and
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
are
games
,
\varphi
is moreover
HD-for-games
.

Proof 3.15
.

We need to define a
sound

resolver simulating

\varphi
. Let
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}}\colon I^{\prime}\to I
be any function choosing initial vertices satisfying that
\varphi\circ{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathit{Id}_{I^{\prime}}}
(which exists by
local surjectivity
of
\varphi
). For each
v\in V
and edge
e^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Out}}(\varphi(v))
we choose one edge
f(v,e^{\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Out}}(v)
such that
\varphi(f(v,e^{\prime}))=e^{\prime}
(which exists by
local surjectivity
), and we let
r
be the
resolver
induced by these choices. Formally, we define
r\colon E^{*}\times E^{\prime}\to E
recursively. For the base case, if
e_{0}^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Out}}(v^{\prime})
, with
v^{\prime}\in I^{\prime}
, we define
r(\varepsilon,e_{0}^{\prime})=f({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}}(v^{\prime}),e_{0}^{\prime})
. Assume that
r
has been defined for
runs
of length
\leq n
, and let
\rho\in E^{*}
be of length
n+1
and
e^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
. If
\rho
is not a
run
or
e^{\prime}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Out}}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Target}}(\varphi(\rho)))
, we let
r(\rho,e^{\prime})
be any edge in
\varphi^{-1}(e^{\prime})
. If not, let
v={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Target}}(\rho)
and define
r(\rho,e^{\prime})
to be the edge
f(v,e^{\prime})
.

It is straightforward to check that
({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}},r)
is indeed a
resolver
(for every
run

\rho^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Run}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime})}
, the sequence
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}(\rho^{\prime})
is a
run
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
and
\rho^{\prime}
is its image under
\varphi
). Finally, since
\varphi
is a
morphism
, for every
\rho^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Run}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime})}
and every
\rho\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Run}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS})}

consistent with

({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}},r)
over
\rho^{\prime}
,
\rho
is
accepting
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
if and only if
\rho^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\varphi}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}(\rho)
is
accepting
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
. We conclude that
({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}},r)
is a
sound resolver
(resp.
sound for
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
) and therefore
\varphi
is an
HD mapping
(resp.
HD-for-games mapping
).

Restrictions and extensions of initial sets.

The following simple lemma states that reducing the number of
initial vertices
preserves the
history-determinism
of mappings.

Lemma 3.16
.

Let
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
and
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
be two
TS
such that there is an
HD
(resp.
HD-for-games
) mapping
\varphi\colon\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}\to\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
. For any non-empty subset
\tilde{I}\subseteq I^{\prime}
,
\varphi
is also an
HD
(resp.
HD-for-games
) mapping between the
transition systems

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}}(\tilde{I})}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}_{\tilde{I}}}
; that is, the
transitions systems
obtained by setting
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}}(\tilde{I})
and
\tilde{I}
as
initial vertices
, respectively.

For arbitrary
acceptance conditions
, enlarging the set of
initial vertices
does not preserve
history-determinism
. However, for
transition systems
using the
acceptance conditions
considered in this work, we can enlarge the set of
initial vertices
without loss of generality. The proof can be found in Appendix
C
.

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
and
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
be two
TS
such that all their states are
accessible
, and let
\varphi\colon\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}\to\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
be an
HD
(resp.
HD-for-games
) mapping between them. If
\mathbb{W}
and
\mathbb{W}^{\prime}
are
prefix-independent
, the mapping
\varphi
is also
HD
(resp.
HD-for-games
) when considered between the
transition systems

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}_{V}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}_{V^{\prime}}}
, consisting of the
transition systems

\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
and
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
where all the states are set to be
initial
.

3.4
Preservation of semantic properties of automata and games

We start this section by showing that
locally bijective morphisms
and
HD mappings
are a strict generalisation of
compositions
by
deterministic
and
history-deterministic

automata
, respectively (Proposition
3.18
).
Then, we prove that these mappings witness the semantic equivalence of the
transition systems
under consideration. That is, (1) if
\varphi\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is an
HD mapping

of automata
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})}
, and if
\varphi
is
locally bijective
,
\mathcal{A}
is
deterministic
(or
unambiguous
) if and only if
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is (Proposition
3.20
);
5
5

5

The results in this section do not directly imply that if
\mathcal{A}
is an
automaton
recognising the
acceptance set
of another automaton
\mathcal{B}
, then
\mathcal{B}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\ltimes}}\mathcal{A}

recognises
the same language as
\mathcal{B}
, if
\mathcal{A}
is not
history-deterministic
(Proposition
2.6
). In that case, the equality
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\ltimes}}\mathcal{A})}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{B})}
follows from the idea that
runs
in
\mathcal{B}
can be simulated in
\mathcal{B}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\ltimes}}\mathcal{A}
“guided by the non-deterministic choices of
\mathcal{A}
”, which we do not formalise in this work.
and (2) if
\varphi\colon\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is an
HD-for-games mapping
,
\mathcal{G}
and
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
have the same
winner
(Proposition
3.24
and Corollary
3.26
).

Morphisms generalise composition by an automaton.

Proposition 3.18
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
complete

automaton

accepting
the language
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}=\mathbb{W}\subseteq\Sigma^{\omega}
, and let
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
be a
(labelled) TS
with
acceptance set

\mathbb{W}
.
Then, there exists a
locally surjective

weak morphism of (labelled) TS

\varphi\colon\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\ltimes}}\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
that
preserves accepting runs
. Moreover:

1.

If
\mathcal{A}
is
deterministic
,
\varphi
can be chosen to be a
locally bijective morphism
.

2.

If
\mathcal{A}
is
HD
, then
\varphi
can be chosen to be an
HD mapping
.

3.

If
\mathcal{A}
is
HD
and
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
is a
game

suitable for transformations
, then
\varphi
can be chosen to be an
HD-for-games mapping
.

Proof 3.19
.

We recall that the set of states of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\ltimes}}\mathcal{A}
is
V\times Q
and its set of transitions
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}E}^{\ltimes}
is a subset of
(E\times\Delta)\sqcup E
, where
V
and
Q
(resp.
E
and
\Delta
) are the states (resp. transitions) of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
and
\mathcal{A}
, respectively.
We let
\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq\Gamma^{\omega}
be the
acceptance set
of
\mathcal{A}
.
We define
\varphi_{V}(v,q)=v
and
\varphi_{E}(e_{1},e_{2})=e_{1}
for
(e_{1},e_{2})\in E\times\Delta
and
\varphi_{E}(e_{1})=e_{1}
for
e_{1}\in E
.
It is immediate to check that
\varphi
is a
weak morphism
.

Given a
run

\rho=(v_{0},q_{0})\xrightarrow{c_{0}}(v_{1},q_{1})\xrightarrow{c_{1}}\dots
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\ltimes}}\mathcal{A}
, we can consider its projection over
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\varphi}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}(\rho)=v_{0}\xrightarrow{a_{0}}v_{1}\xrightarrow{a_{1}}\dots
. We note that there must exist a unique
run
in
\mathcal{A}
of the form

\varphi_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho)=q_{0}\xrightarrow{a_{0}:c_{0}}q_{1}\xrightarrow{a_{1}:c_{1}}\dots.

(Formally, some letters
a_{i}
might equal
\varepsilon
, and in this case
q_{i}\xrightarrow{a_{i}:c_{i}}q_{i+1}
does not appear in the
run

\varphi_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho)
).

We show that
\varphi

preserves accepting runs
. Let
\rho
be an
accepting run
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\ltimes}}\mathcal{A}
. In that case,
c_{0}c_{1}c_{2}\dots\in\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{A}}
, and therefore
\varphi_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho)
is an
accepting run
in
\mathcal{A}
over
a_{0}a_{1}a_{2}\dots
, so we conclude that
a_{0}a_{1}a_{2}\dots\in\mathbb{W}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\varphi}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}(\rho)
is an
accepting run
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
.

We prove next the
local surjectivity
of
\varphi
. Clearly,
\varphi
induces a surjection between the
initial vertices
of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\ltimes}}\mathcal{A}
(which are
I_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}\times I_{\mathcal{A}}
) and those of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
.
Let
(v,q)\in V\times Q
and
e_{1}=v\xrightarrow{a}v^{\prime}\in E
. If
a=\varepsilon
, the edge
e_{1}
belongs to
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}E}^{\ltimes}
and
\varphi(e_{1})=e_{1}
. If
a\neq\varepsilon
, since
\mathcal{A}
is
complete
there is a transition
e_{2}=q\xrightarrow{a}q^{\prime}\in\Delta
and
\varphi(e_{1},e_{2})=e_{1}
, so
\varphi
is
locally surjective
.

1.

Since
\mathcal{A}
has a single
initial state

q_{0}
,
\varphi
induces a bijection between the
initial vertices
of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\ltimes}}\mathcal{A}
(which are
I_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}\times\{q_{0}\}
) and those of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
.
Let
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}E}^{\ltimes}_{\mathrm{col}}\subseteq E\times\Delta
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}E}^{\ltimes}_{\varepsilon}\subseteq E
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}E}^{\ltimes}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}E}^{\ltimes}_{\mathrm{col}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}E}^{\ltimes}_{\varepsilon}
. We remark that
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\varphi\mathchoice{\vphantom{\big|}}{}{}{}\right|_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}E}^{\ltimes}_{\varepsilon}}}
is the identity function (so injective) and that
\varphi({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}E}^{\ltimes}_{\mathrm{col}})\cap\varphi({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}E}^{\ltimes}_{\varepsilon})=\emptyset
because
\varphi({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}E}^{\ltimes}_{\mathrm{col}})
are exactly
coloured
transitions of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
. Finally, let
(e_{1},e_{2})
and
(e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime})
in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Out}}(v,q)\cap{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}E}^{\ltimes}_{\mathrm{col}}
. Their
\varphi(e_{1},e_{2})=\varphi(e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime})
if and only if
e_{1}=e_{1}^{\prime}
. Let
a\in\Sigma
be the colour of
e_{1}
. Since
\mathcal{A}
is
deterministic
, there is at most one transition from
q
labelled by
a
, that must be
e_{2}=e_{2}^{\prime}
. We conclude that
(e_{1},e_{2})=(e_{1}^{\prime},e_{2}^{\prime})
and that
\varphi
is
locally injective
.

Let
\rho
be a
rejecting run
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\ltimes}}\mathcal{A}
(we use the notations introduced above). In that case,
c_{0}c_{1}c_{2}\dots\notin\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{A}}
, and therefore
\varphi_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho)
is a
rejecting run
over
a_{0}a_{1}a_{2}\dots
. Since
\mathcal{A}
is
deterministic
, this is the only
run over

a_{0}a_{1}a_{2}\dots
, so we conclude that it does not belong to
\mathbb{W}.
We conclude that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\varphi}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}(\rho)
is a
rejecting run
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
.

2.

Let
(r_{0},r_{\mathcal{A}})
be a
resolver
for
\mathcal{A}
. We define a
resolver

({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}},r_{\varphi})

simulating

\varphi
. First, we let
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}}(v_{0})=(v_{0},r_{0})
for all
v_{0}\in I_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}
. We define
r_{\varphi}\colon{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}E}^{\ltimes}}^{*}\times E\to{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}E}^{\ltimes}
by induction on the length of the
runs
.
Let
e_{0}=v_{0}\xrightarrow{a}v_{1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Out}}(v_{0})
be an edge in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
. If
e_{0}
is
uncoloured
(
a=\varepsilon
), we let
r_{\varphi}(\varepsilon,e_{0})=e_{0}=(v_{0},r_{0})\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}(v_{1},r_{0})
. If not, we let
r_{\varphi}(\varepsilon,e_{0})=(e_{0},e_{a})
, where
e_{a}=r(\varepsilon,a)
.
Assume that
r_{\varphi}
has been defined for sequences of edges of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\ltimes}}\mathcal{A}
of length
<n
and let
\rho=e_{0}e_{1}\dots e_{n-1}\in{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}E}^{\ltimes}}^{*}
be a sequence length
n+1
and
e_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}=v_{n}\xrightarrow{a_{n}}v_{n+1}
be an edge in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
.
If
\rho
is not a
run
or if it does not end in
\varphi^{-1}(v_{n})
, we let
r_{\varphi}(\rho,e_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}})
be any edge in
\varphi^{-1}(e_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}})
.
Assume that
\rho
is a
run
ending in
\varphi^{-1}(v_{n})
. If
a_{n}=\varepsilon
, we define
r_{\varphi}(\rho,e_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}})=e_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}
. As noted before,
\rho
induces a
run

\varphi_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho)=q_{0}\xrightarrow{a_{0}:c_{0}}q_{1}\xrightarrow{a_{1}:c_{1}}\dots\xrightarrow{}q_{n}
in
\mathcal{A}
. We let
e_{\mathcal{A}}=r_{\mathcal{A}}(\varphi_{\mathcal{A}}(\rho),a_{n})
be the transition chosen by the
resolver
of
\mathcal{A}
after this
run
, and we define
r_{\varphi}(\rho,e_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}})=(e_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}},e_{\mathcal{A}})
.

It directly follows from this definition that
({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}},r_{\varphi})
is indeed a
resolver
. The proof that if
\rho\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Run}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS})}
is an
accepting run
then
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r}_{\varphi,\mathpzc{Runs}}(\rho)
is
accepting
follows the same lines as the previous item.

3.

We prove that, if
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
is a
game

suitable for transformations
, the
resolver

({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}},r_{\varphi})
defined in the previous item is
sound for
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
. We claim that if
\rho
is a
run
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
, the only
run

consistent with

({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}},r_{\varphi})
over
\rho
is
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r}_{\varphi,\mathpzc{Runs}}(\rho)
. This follows from the fact that if
(v,q)
is a vertex in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\ltimes}}\mathcal{A}

controlled by Adam
and
e\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Out}}(v)
, then there is a unique
e^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Out}}(v,q)
such that
\varphi(e^{\prime})=e
. This is indeed the case: as
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
is
suitable for transformations
, if
v
is an
Adam’s vertex
, every
e\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Out}}(v)
is
uncoloured
, so by definition of
\varphi
we have that
\varphi(e^{\prime})=e\implies e^{\prime}=e
. (This can be seen as that
\varphi
is locally bijective in Adam’s vertices).
We conclude that if
\rho
is an
accepting run
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
and
\rho^{\ltimes}
is a
run

consistent with

({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}},r_{\varphi})
over
\rho
, then
\rho^{\ltimes}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r}_{\varphi,\mathpzc{Runs}}(\rho)
, which is
accepting
by
soundness
of the
resolver

({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}},r_{\varphi})
.

Morphisms witness equivalence of automata.

Proposition 3.20
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
be two
automata
over the same
input alphabet
such that there is an
HD mapping
of
automata

\varphi\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
. Then,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})}
, and
\mathcal{A}
is
HD
if and only if
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is
HD
.
If
\varphi
is moreover
locally bijective
and
surjective
,
\mathcal{A}
is
deterministic
(resp.
complete
) if and only if
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is.

Proof 3.21
.

Since
\varphi

preserves accepting runs
, it is clear that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})}
. Since
\varphi
admits a
sound resolver

({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}},r)
, if
\rho
is an
accepting

run over

w\in\Sigma^{\omega}
in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}(\rho)
is an
accepting

run over

w
in
\mathcal{A}
, so
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
.

Let
({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}},r_{\varphi})
be a
sound

resolver simulating

\varphi
.
Assume that
\mathcal{A}
is
HD
, admitting a
resolver

(r_{0},r)
. A
resolver

(r_{0}^{\prime},r^{\prime})
for
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
can be obtained just by composing
r_{\varphi}
and
\varphi
, that is:
r_{0}^{\prime}=\varphi(r_{0})
and for
\rho^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Run}^{\mathsf{fin}}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})}
and
a\in\Sigma
,
r^{\prime}(\rho^{\prime},a)=\varphi(r({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r}_{\varphi,\mathpzc{Runs}}(\rho^{\prime}),a)))
. That is, given a
run

\rho^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, we simulate it in
\mathcal{A}
using
r_{\varphi}
, then, we look at what is the continuation proposed by the resolver
r
when we give the letter
a
, and we transfer back this choice to
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
using
\varphi
.
Assume now that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is
HD
and that
(r_{0}^{\prime},r^{\prime})
is a
resolver
for it. We define a
resolver

(r_{0},r)
for
\mathcal{A}
. We let
r_{0}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}}(r_{0}^{\prime})
, and for
\rho\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Run}^{\mathsf{fin}}(\mathcal{A})}
and
a\in\Sigma
,
r(\rho,a)=r_{\varphi}(({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\varphi}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}(\rho),r({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\varphi}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}(\rho),a))
. That is, given a
run

\rho
in
\mathcal{A}
, we simulate it in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
using
\varphi
, then, we look at what is the continuation proposed by the resolver
r^{\prime}
when we give the letter
a
, and we transfer back this choice to
\mathcal{A}
using
r_{\varphi}
.
It is a direct check that the
resolvers
defined this way witness that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{A}
, respectively, are
HD
.

The proof that
\mathcal{A}
is
deterministic
(resp.
complete
) if and only if
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is
deterministic
(resp.
complete
), assuming
surjectivity
and
local bijectivity
of
\varphi
, follows the same lines.

A subclass of
automata
with a restrictive amount of
non-determinism
that is widely study is that of
unambiguous
automata (we refer to
[
29
,
20
]
for a detailed exposition). An automaton is
unambiguous
if for every input word
w\in\Sigma^{\omega}
there is at most one accepting
run over

w
, and it is
strongly unambiguous
if there is at most one
run over

w
. By Remark
3.4
,
locally bijective morphisms
also preserve
(strongly) unambiguity
: if
\varphi\colon\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is a
locally bijective

morphism
then
\mathcal{A}
is (
strongly
)
unambiguous
if and only if
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is.

Morphisms preserve winning regions of games.

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
be two
games
, such that there is a
weak morphism

of games

\varphi\colon\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
that is
locally surjective
and
preserves accepting runs
. If
Eve

wins
the
game

\mathcal{G}
from an initial vertex
v
, then she
wins

\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
from
\varphi(v)
.

Proof 3.23
.

Let
v^{\prime}=\varphi(v)
, and let
\mathsf{strat}_{v}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Path}^{\mathsf{fin}}_{v}(\mathcal{G})}\to E
be a
strategy
from
v
for Eve in
G
.
Intuitively, we will define a
strategy
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
as follows: for each finite run
\rho^{\prime}
from
v^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, we pick a preimage
\rho\in\varphi^{-1}(\rho^{\prime})
in
\mathcal{G}
, look at the decision made by
\mathsf{strat}_{v}
at the end of
\rho
and transfer it back to
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
via
\varphi
. In order to define a correct
strategy
, the choices of the preimages have to be made in a coherent manner. We formalise this idea next.

We will make use of a function
\mathsf{choice}_{st}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Path}^{\mathsf{fin}}_{v^{\prime}}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})}\to{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Path}^{\mathsf{fin}}_{v}(\mathcal{G})}
satisfying that for any
\rho^{\prime}=e_{0}^{\prime}e_{1}^{\prime}\dots e_{n-1}^{\prime}e_{n}^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Path}^{\mathsf{fin}}_{v^{\prime}}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})}
:

•

The run
\mathsf{choice}_{st}(\rho^{\prime})
has length
n+1
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\varphi}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}(\mathsf{choice}_{st}(\rho^{\prime}))=\rho^{\prime}
.

•

Monotonicity: if
\tilde{\rho}^{\prime}\rho^{\prime}
then
\mathsf{choice}_{st}(\tilde{\rho}^{\prime})\mathsf{choice}_{st}(\rho^{\prime})
.

•

If there exists
e_{n}\in\varphi^{-1}(e_{n}^{\prime})
such that
\mathsf{choice}_{st}(e_{0}^{\prime}e_{1}^{\prime}\dots e_{n-1}^{\prime})e_{n}
is
consistent with

\mathsf{strat}_{v}
, then
\mathsf{choice}_{st}(\rho^{\prime})
is
consistent with

\mathsf{strat}_{v}
.

Assume for now that such a function exists, and define a
strategy
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
as

\mathsf{strat}^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}(\rho^{\prime})=\varphi(\mathsf{strat}_{v}(\mathsf{choice}_{st}(\rho^{\prime}))),\;\text{ for }\rho^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Path}^{\mathsf{fin}}_{v^{\prime}}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})}.

We prove that
\mathsf{strat}^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}
is
winning
.
Let
\rho^{\prime}=e_{0}^{\prime}e_{1}^{\prime}\dots\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Run}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})}
be an infinite
play

consistent with

\mathsf{strat}^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}
. For each finite prefix
\tilde{\rho}^{\prime}\rho^{\prime}
,
\mathsf{choice}_{st}(\tilde{\rho}^{\prime})
is a finite play in
\mathcal{G}
, and by the monotonicity assumption, we can define the limit of these runs as:

\vec{\rho}=e_{0}e_{1}e_{2}\dots\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Run}(\mathcal{G})},\;\text{ where }e_{0}e_{1}\dots e_{n}=\mathsf{choice}_{st}(e_{0}^{\prime}e_{1}^{\prime}\dots e_{n}^{\prime}),

which is indeed a
run
in
\mathcal{G}
. We show that
\vec{\rho}
is
consistent with

\mathsf{strat}_{v}
by induction. Let
\rho_{n}=e_{0}e_{1}\dots e_{n-1}
be the
prefix
of size
n
of
\vec{\rho}
, and suppose that it ends in a vertex
v_{n}
controlled by
Eve
. We want to show that
e_{n}=\mathsf{strat}_{v}(\rho_{n})
. By definition of
\mathsf{strat}^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}
,
e_{n}^{\prime}=\varphi(\mathsf{strat}_{v}(\rho_{n}))=\varphi(e_{n})
, and as
v_{n}
is controlled by
Eve
,
\mathsf{strat}_{v}(\rho_{n})
is the only continuation of
\rho_{n}

consistent with

\mathsf{strat}_{v}
, so by the last property of
\mathsf{choice}_{st}
,
e_{n}
has to coincide with
\mathsf{strat}_{v}(\rho_{n})
, as we wanted. As
\vec{\rho}
is
consistent with
the
winning strategy

\mathsf{strat}_{v}
, it is an
accepting run
in
\mathcal{G}
, and since
\varphi

preserves accepting runs
,
\rho^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\varphi}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}(\vec{\rho})
is also an
accepting run
.

Finally, we show how to build a function
\mathsf{choice}_{st}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Path}^{\mathsf{fin}}_{v^{\prime}}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})}\to{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Path}^{\mathsf{fin}}_{v}(\mathcal{G})}
by induction on the length of the runs. Assume that
\mathsf{choice}_{st}
has been defined for
runs
of length
\leq n
, and let
e_{0}^{\prime}e_{1}^{\prime}\dots e_{n}^{\prime}
be a run of length
n+1
, with
\mathsf{choice}_{st}(e_{0}^{\prime}e_{1}^{\prime}\dots e_{n-1}^{\prime})=e_{0}e_{1}\dots e_{n-1}
. If
e_{0}e_{1}\dots e_{n-1}
is not
consistent with

\mathsf{strat}_{v}
, it ends in a vertex
v_{n}
controlled by
Adam
, or
\mathsf{strat}_{v}(e_{0}e_{1}\dots e_{n-1})\notin\varphi^{-1}(e_{n})
, we let
e_{n}\in\varphi^{-1}(e_{n})\cap{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Out}}(v_{n})
be any edge (one such edge exists by
local surjectivity
).
On the contrary, we let
e_{n}=\mathsf{strat}_{v}(e_{0}e_{1}\dots e_{n-1})
.
We define
\mathsf{choice}_{st}(e_{0}^{\prime}e_{1}^{\prime}\dots e_{n-1}^{\prime}e_{n}^{\prime})=e_{0}e_{1}\dots e_{n-1}e_{n}
.
By construction, the obtained function fulfils the 4 requirements.

Proposition 3.24
.

Let
\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
be two
games
such that there is an
HD-for-games mapping

\varphi\colon\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.
Eve’s

winning region
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is the projection of her
winning region
in
\mathcal{G}
:
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{Eve}}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})}=\varphi({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{Eve}}(\mathcal{G})})
.

Proof 3.25
.

If
Eve

wins

\mathcal{G}
from an
initial vertex

v
, Lemma
3.22
guarantees that she
wins

\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
from
\varphi(v)
.

Assume now that
Eve

wins

\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
from an
initial vertex

v^{\prime}
with a
strategy

\mathsf{strat}^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Path}^{\mathsf{fin}}_{v^{\prime}}(\mathcal{G}^{\prime})}\to E^{\prime}
. We need to show that she
wins

\mathcal{G}
from some
initial vertex
in
\varphi^{-1}(v^{\prime})
. Let
({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}},r)
be a
resolver simulating

\varphi

sound for
\mathcal{G}
and let
v={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}}(v^{\prime})
. We define

\mathsf{strat}_{v}(\rho)=r(\rho,\mathsf{strat}^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\varphi}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}(\rho)),\;\text{ for }\rho\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Path}^{\mathsf{fin}}_{v}(\mathcal{G})}.

That is,
\mathsf{strat}_{v}
is a
strategy
in
\mathcal{G}
from
v
that, given a finite run
\rho
, simulates
\rho
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, looks at the move done by the
strategy

\mathsf{strat}^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}
in there, and transfers this choice back to
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
by using the
resolver

r
. We prove that
\mathsf{strat}_{v}
is
winning for Eve
in
\mathcal{G}
. Let
\rho=e_{0}e_{1}\dots\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Path}_{v}(\mathcal{G})}
be a
play
consistent with
\mathsf{strat}_{v}
. We claim that
\varphi(\rho)
is
consistent with

\mathsf{strat}^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}
and that
\rho
is
consistent with

({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}},r)
over
\varphi(\rho)
. This implies the desired result;
consistency with

\mathsf{strat}^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}
implies that
\varphi(\rho)
is
accepting
, and since
({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}},r)
is
sound for
\mathcal{G}
,
\rho
would be
accepting
in
\mathcal{G}
.

We prove that
\varphi(\rho)
is
consistent with

\mathsf{strat}^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}
. Let
e_{0}^{\prime}e_{1}^{\prime}\dots e_{n-1}^{\prime}
be a subplay of
\varphi(\rho)
ending in a vertex
v_{n}

controlled by Eve
. By definition of the strategy
\mathsf{strat}_{v}
, we have that
e_{n}=r(e_{0}\dots e_{n-1},\mathsf{strat}^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}(e_{0}^{\prime}\dots e_{n-1}^{\prime}))
, and by definition of a
resolver
(item
2
), we obtain that
e_{n}^{\prime}=\varphi(e_{n})=\mathsf{strat}^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}(e_{0}^{\prime}\dots e_{n-1}^{\prime}))
, as we wanted.

The fact that
\rho
is
consistent with

({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}},r)
over
\varphi(\rho)
follows directly from the definition of
\mathsf{strat}_{v}
.

The next corollary follows from the previous proposition and Lemma
3.17
.

Corollary 3.26
.

Let
\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
be two
games
whose states are
accessible
and such that their
acceptance sets

\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{G}}
and
\mathbb{W}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}
are
prefix-independent
. If there is an
HD-for-games mapping

\varphi\colon\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, then
Eve’s

full winning region
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is the projection of her
full winning region
in
\mathcal{G}
:
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{Eve}}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{V^{\prime}}})}=\varphi({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{Eve}}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{V}})})
.

4
The Zielonka tree: An optimal approach to Muller languages

In this section, we take a close look into the
Zielonka tree
, a structure introduced (under the name of
split trees
) to study
Muller languages

[
94
]
.
We show how to use the
Zielonka tree
to construct minimal
deterministic

parity

automata
and minimal
history-deterministic

Rabin

automata

recognising

Muller languages
.
In Section
4.2
, we describe the construction of a minimal
deterministic

parity

automaton

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{parity}}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}}
for a given
Muller language

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}(\mathcal{F})}
. Theorem
4.14
, the main contribution of this section, states the minimality of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{parity}}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}}
both amongst
deterministic
and
HD

parity

automata
. Theorem
4.13
states the optimality on the number of
colours
of the
acceptance condition
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{parity}}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}}
, and implies that we can determine the
parity index
of a
Muller language
from its
Zielonka tree
.
We will use the optimality of
automaton

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{parity}}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}}
to provide a polynomial-time algorithm minimising
DPAs

recognising

Muller languages
in Section
6.3
.

In Section
4.3
, we describe the construction of a minimal
history-deterministic

Rabin

automaton

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{Rabin}}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}}
for a
Muller language

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}(\mathcal{F})}
. Its minimality amongst
HD

automata
is shown in Theorem
4.64
, by using the characterisation of the
memory requirements
of a
Muller language
in terms of its
Zielonka tree

[
34
]
.

On the other hand, it has been shown that finding a minimal
deterministic

Rabin

automaton

recognising
a given
Muller language
is
\mathsf{NP}\mathchar 45\relax\mathrm{complete}
, if the language is represented by a
parity
or
Rabin

automaton
, or even by its
Zielonka tree

[
21
]
.
Therefore, unless
\mathsf{P}=\mathsf{NP}
, there are
Muller languages
for which minimal
deterministic

Rabin
automata are strictly larger than minimal
HD

Rabin

automata
. Some explicit such languages were shown in
[
24
, Section 4]
.
A summary of the minimal automata
recognising

Muller languages
appears in Table
1
.

Type of automata

Deterministic

History-

deterministic

Parity

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{parity}}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}}

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{parity}}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}}

Rabin

No characterisation

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{Rabin}}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}}

Table 1
:
Minimal automata
recognising
a
Muller language

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}(\mathcal{F})}
, according to the type of
acceptance condition
(
parity
or
Rabin
) and the form of
determinism
.

4.1
The Zielonka tree

Definition 4.1

(
[
94
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}2^{\Sigma}_{+}}
be a family of non-empty subsets over a finite set
\Sigma
. A
Zielonka tree
for
\mathcal{F}
(over
\Sigma
),
6
6

6

The definition of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
, as well as most subsequent definitions, do not only depend on
\mathcal{F}
but also on the alphabet
\Sigma
. Although this dependence is important, we do not explicitly include it in the notations in order to lighten them, as most of the time the alphabet will be clear from the context.
denoted
\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}=(N,\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\preceq}},\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\nu}{}{}:N\to{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}2^{\Sigma}_{+}})
is a
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}2^{\Sigma}_{+}}
-labelled tree
with nodes partitioned into
round nodes
and
square nodes
,
N=\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}N_{\bigcirc}}\sqcup\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}N_{\Box}}
, such that:

•

The
root
is labelled
\Sigma
.

•

If a
node
is labelled
X\subseteq\Sigma
, with
X\in\mathcal{F}
, then it is a
round node
, and it has a child for each maximal non-empty subset
Y\subseteq X
such that
Y\not\in\mathcal{F}
, which is labelled
Y
.

•

If a
node
is labelled
X\subseteq\Sigma
, with
X\not\in\mathcal{F}
, then it is a
square node
, and it has a child for each maximal non-empty subset
Y\subseteq X
such that
Y\in\mathcal{F}
, which is labelled
Y
.

Remark 4.2
.

We note that for each family of subsets
\mathcal{F}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}2^{\Sigma}_{+}}
, there is only one
Zielonka tree
up to renaming of its nodes, so we will talk of
the

Zielonka tree
of
\mathcal{F}
.

For a family of subsets
\mathcal{F}\subseteq 2^{\Sigma}
and
\Sigma^{\prime}\subseteq\Sigma
, we write
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathcal{F}\mathchoice{\vphantom{\big|}}{}{}{}\right|_{\Sigma^{\prime}}}}=\mathcal{F}\cap 2^{\Sigma^{\prime}}
.

Remark 4.3
.

We remark that if
n
is a node of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
, then the
subtree of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
rooted at
n
is the
Zielonka tree
for the family
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathcal{F}\mathchoice{\vphantom{\big|}}{}{}{}\right|_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)}}}
over the alphabet
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)
, that is, for the restriction of
\mathcal{F}
to the subsets included in the label of
n
.

Remark 4.4
.

Let
n
be a node of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
and let
n_{1}
be a
child
of it. If
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{1})\subsetneq X\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{1})\in\mathcal{F}\iff X\notin\mathcal{F}\iff{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)\notin\mathcal{F}
. In particular, if
n_{1},n_{2}
are two different
children
of
n
, then

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{1})\in\mathcal{F}\iff{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{2})\in\mathcal{F}\iff{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{1})\cup{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{2})\notin\mathcal{F}
.

We equip
Zielonka trees
with an
order
to navigate in them. That is, we equip each set
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Children}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}}(n)
with a total order, making
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
an
ordered tree
. The precise order considered will be irrelevant for our purposes.
From now on, we will assume that all
Zielonka trees
are
ordered
, without explicitly mentioning it.

For a
leaf

l\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Leaves}}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})
and a letter
a\in\Sigma
we define
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\mathsf{Supp}}(l,a)=n
to be the
deepest

ancestor
of
l
(maximal for
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\preceq}}
) such that
a\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)
.

Example 4.5
.

We will use the
Muller language associated to
the following family of subsets as a running example throughout the paper.
Let
\Sigma=\{a,b,c\}
and let
\mathcal{F}
be:

\mathcal{F}=\{\{a,b\},\{a,c\},\{b\}\}.

In Figure
9
we show the
Zielonka tree
of
\mathcal{F}
.
We use Greek letters (in pink) to name the nodes of the tree.
Integers appearing on the right of the tree will be used in the next section.

We have that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Supp}}(\xi,c)=\lambda
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Supp}}(\xi,b)=\alpha
.
Also,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Jump}}(\xi,\lambda)=\zeta
is the
leaf
reached by going from
\xi
to
\lambda
, then changing to the next branch (in a cyclic way) and re-descend by taking the leftmost path. Similarly,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Jump}}(\xi,\alpha)=\theta
.

The
subtree rooted at

\lambda
contains the nodes
\{\lambda,\xi,\zeta\}
. We note that this is the
Zielonka tree
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathcal{F}\mathchoice{\vphantom{\big|}}{}{}{}\right|_{\{a,c\}}}}=\{\{a,c\}\}
(over the alphabet
\{a,c\}
).

Figure 9
:
Zielonka tree
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
for

\mathcal{F}=\{\{a,b\},\{a,c\},\{b\}\}
.

4.2
A minimal deterministic parity automaton

We present next the
Zielonka-tree-parity-automaton
, a minimal
deterministic

parity

automaton
for a
Muller language

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}(\mathcal{F})}
built from the
Zielonka tree

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
. Our construction will furthermore let us determine the
parity index
of the language
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}(\mathcal{F})}
from its
Zielonka tree
.

4.2.1
The Zielonka-tree-parity-automaton

We associate a non-negative integer to each level of a
Zielonka tree

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}=(N,\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\preceq}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{})
.
We let
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}p_{\mathcal{Z}}}:N\to\mathbb{N}
be the function defined as:

•

if
\Sigma\in\mathcal{F}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}p_{\mathcal{Z}}}(n)=0pt(n)
,

•

if
\Sigma\notin\mathcal{F}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}p_{\mathcal{Z}}}(n)=0pt(n)+1
.

We let
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\min_{\mathcal{F}}}
(resp.
\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\max_{\mathcal{F}}}
) be the minimum (resp. maximum) value taken by the function
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}p_{\mathcal{Z}}}
.

Remark 4.6
.

A node
n
in the
Zielonka tree

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
verifies that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}p_{\mathcal{Z}}}(n)
is even if and only if
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)\in\mathcal{F}
.
If
\Sigma\in\mathcal{F}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\min_{\mathcal{F}}}=0
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\max_{\mathcal{F}}}
equals the
height
of the
Zielonka tree
minus one. If
\Sigma\notin\mathcal{F}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\min_{\mathcal{F}}}=1
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\max_{\mathcal{F}}}
equals the
height
of the
Zielonka tree
.

Example 4.7
.

The
Muller language
from Example
4.5
satisfies
\Sigma\notin\mathcal{F}
. The values taken by the function
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}p_{\mathcal{Z}}}
are represented at the right of the
Zielonka tree
in Figure
9
. We have
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}p_{\mathcal{Z}}}(\alpha)=1
,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}p_{\mathcal{Z}}}(\beta)={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}p_{\mathcal{Z}}}(\lambda)=2
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}p_{\mathcal{Z}}}(\theta)={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}p_{\mathcal{Z}}}(\xi)={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}p_{\mathcal{Z}}}(\zeta)=3
, so
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\min_{\mathcal{F}}}=1
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\max_{\mathcal{F}}}=3
.

Definition 4.8

(Zielonka-tree-parity-automaton)
.

Given a family of non-empty subsets
\mathcal{F}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}2^{\Sigma}_{+}}
,
we define the
ZT-parity-automaton

\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{parity}}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}}=(Q,\Sigma,q_{0},[{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\min_{\mathcal{F}}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\max_{\mathcal{F}}}],{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\delta},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{parity}})
as the
deterministic

parity

automaton
given by:

•

Q={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Leaves}}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})
,

•

q_{0}
is the
leftmost

leaf
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
,
7
7

7

Any state can be chosen as
initial state
(see Lemma
2.5
).

•

The transition reading
a\in\Sigma
from
q\in Q
goes to
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Jump}}(q,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Supp}}(q,a))
and produces
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}p_{\mathcal{Z}}}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Supp}}(q,a))
as
output
, that is,

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\delta}(q,a)=\left({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Jump}}(q,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Supp}}(q,a)),{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}p_{\mathcal{Z}}}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Supp}}(q,a))\right).

Intuitively, the transitions of the automaton are determined as follows: if we are in a
leaf

l
and we read a colour
a
, then we move up in the branch of
l
until we reach a node
n
that contains the letter
a
in its label.
Then we pick the
child
of
n
just on the right of the branch that we took before (in a cyclic way), and we move to the
leftmost

leaf
below it. The colour produced as output is
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}p_{\mathcal{Z}}}(n)
, determined by the
depth
of
n
.

Example 4.9
.

In Figure
10
we show the
ZT-parity-automaton

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{parity}}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}}
of the family of subsets from Example
4.5
.

Figure 10
:
ZT-parity-automaton

recognising
the
Muller language associated to

\mathcal{F}=\{\{a,b\},\{a,c\},\{b\}\}
.

Correctness of the Zielonka-tree-parity-automaton.

Proposition 4.10

(Correctness)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}2^{\Sigma}_{+}}
be a family of non-empty subsets. Then,

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{parity}}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}})}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{F})}.

That is, a word
w\in\Sigma^{\omega}
is
accepted
by
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{parity}}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}}
if and only if
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Inf}}(w)\in\mathcal{F}
.

The following useful lemma follows directly from the definition of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Supp}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Jump}}
.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
q
be a
leaf
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
and let
n
be a node
above

q
. Then,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Supp}}(q,a)
is a
descendant
of
n
if and only if
a\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)
, and in this case,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Jump}}(q,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Supp}}(q,a))
is a
descendant
of
n
too.

Proof 4.12

(Proof of Proposition
4.10
)
.

Let
w=w_{0}w_{1}w_{2}\dots\in\Sigma^{\omega}
be an infinite word. For
i>0
, let

q_{i}
be the
leaf
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
reached after the (only)
run over

w_{0}w_{1}\dots w_{i-1}
in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{parity}}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}}
.
For
i\geq 0
let
n_{i}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Supp}}(q_{i},w_{i})
be the “intermediate node” used to determine the next state and the output colour of each transition, and let
c_{i}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}p_{\mathcal{Z}}}(n_{i})=\gamma(q_{i},w_{i})\in[{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\min_{\mathcal{F}}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\max_{\mathcal{F}}}]
be that output colour (the
output
of the
run over

w
being therefore
c_{0}c_{1}c_{2}\dots\in\mathbb{N}^{\omega}
).
Let
q_{\infty}
be a node appearing infinitely often in the sequence
q_{0}q_{1}q_{2}\dots
, and let
n_{w}
be the
deepest

ancestor
of
q_{\infty}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Inf}}(w)\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{w})
.

Claim 1
.

There is
K\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
i\geq K
,
q_{i}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\succeq}}n_{w}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Supp}}(q_{i},w_{i})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\succeq}}n_{w}
. In particular,
c_{i}\geq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}p_{\mathcal{Z}}}(n_{w})
for
i\geq K
.

{subproof}

Let
K\in\mathbb{N}
be a position such that
w_{i}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Inf}}(w)
for all
i\geq K
and
q_{K}=q_{\infty}
.
The claim follows from Lemma
4.11
and induction.

Claim 2
.

Let
n_{w,1},\dots,n_{w,s}
be an enumeration of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Children}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}}(n_{w})

from left to right
. It holds that:

1.

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Supp}}(q_{i},w_{i})=n_{w}
infinitely often. In particular,
c_{i}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}p_{\mathcal{Z}}}(n_{w})
for infinitely many
i
’s.

2.

There is no
n_{w,k}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Children}}(n_{w})
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Inf}}(w)\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{w,k})
.

{subproof}

We first remark that for all
n_{w,k}
there are arbitrarily large positions
i
such that
q_{i}
is not
below

n_{w,k}
. Suppose by contradiction that this is not the case. Then, for all
i
sufficiently large we have that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Supp}}(q_{i},w_{i})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\succeq}}n_{w,k}
, and by Lemma
4.11
,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Inf}}(w)\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{w,k})
. In particular,
q_{\infty}
is
below

n_{w,k}
, contradicting the fact that
n_{w}
is the
deepest

ancestor
of
q_{\infty}
containing
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Inf}}(w)
.

Let
K
be like in the Claim
1
.
We show that if
i\geq K
and
q_{i}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\succeq}}n_{w,k}
, then there is
j>i
such that
w_{j}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{w,k})
,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Supp}}(q_{j},w_{j})=n_{w}
and
q_{j+1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\succeq}}n_{w,k+1}
(by an abuse of notation we let
s+1=1
).
It suffices to consider the least
j\geq i
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Supp}}(q_{j},w_{j})\nsucceq n_{w,k}
(which exists by the previous remark). Since
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Inf}}(w)\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{w})
we have that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Supp}}(q_{j},w_{j})=n_{w}
, so
w_{j}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{w,j})
(by Lemma
4.11
) and by definition of the transitions of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{parity}}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}}
,
q_{j+1}
will be a
leaf

below

n_{w,k+1}
.

The fact that
q_{j+1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\succeq}}n_{w,k+1}
implies that for any child
n_{w,k^{\prime}}
, infinitely many states
q_{i}
will be below
n_{w,k^{\prime}}
(we go around the children in a round-robin fashion). Therefore, for any
k
, there are arbitrarily large
j
such that
w_{j}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{w,j})
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Supp}}(q_{j},w_{j})=n_{w}
, implying both items in the claim.

Combining both claims, we obtain that the minimum of the colours that are produced as output infinitely often is
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}p_{\mathcal{Z}}}(n_{w})
. By Remark
4.6
,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}p_{\mathcal{Z}}}(n_{w})
is even if and only if
n_{w}
is a
round node
(if
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{w})\in\mathcal{F}
). It remains to show that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Inf}}(w)\in\mathcal{F}
if and only if
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{w})\in\mathcal{F}
, which holds by the second item in Claim
2
and Remark
4.4
.

4.2.2
Optimality of the Zielonka-tree-parity-automaton

We now state and prove the main results of this section: the optimality of the
ZT-parity-automaton
in both number of states (Theorem
4.14
) and number of
colours
of the
acceptance condition
(Theorem
4.13
).
The minimality of the
ZT-parity-automaton
comes in two versions. A weaker one states its minimality only amongst
deterministic

automata
(Theorem
4.18
), and a stronger one states its minimality amongst all
history-deterministic

automata
(Theorem
4.14
). Although the weaker version is implied by the stronger one, we find it instructive to provide a proof for this easier case. The proof of the stronger statement is one of the most technical parts of the paper, but the argument used in its proof is just a careful refinement of the ideas appearing in the weaker version.

Statement of the results.

Theorem 4.13

(Optimality of the parity condition)
.

The
parity index
of a
Muller language

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{F})}
is
[{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\min_{\mathcal{F}}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\max_{\mathcal{F}}}]
. That is, the
ZT-parity-automaton
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{F})}
uses the optimal number of colours to recognise this language.

Theorem 4.14

(Minimality of the ZT-parity-automaton)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
history-deterministic

parity

automaton

recognising
a
Muller language

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{F})}
. Then,
|{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{parity}}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}}|\leq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}|\mathcal{A}|}
.

Corollary 4.15
.

For every
Muller language

L
, a minimal
deterministic

parity

automaton

recognising

L
has the same size as a minimal
HD

parity

automaton

recognising

L
.

We remark that, nonetheless, there are non-trivial
HD

parity

automata

recognising

Muller languages
. The automaton provided in Example
2.3
is an
HD

coBüchi

automaton

recognising
a
Muller language
that cannot be made
deterministic
just by removing transitions. We note that the (
deterministic
)
ZT-parity-automaton
for this
Muller language
has only
2
states.

We say that an
automaton

\mathcal{A}
is
determinisable by pruning
if there is a subset
\Delta^{\prime}\subseteq\Delta
of its transitions and an
initial state

q_{0}
such that the
subautomaton induced
by
\Delta^{\prime}
with initial state
q_{0}
is
deterministic
and
recognises

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
.

Proposition 4.16
.

There exists an
HD

parity

automaton

recognising
a
Muller language
that is not
determinisable by pruning
.

Optimality of the parity condition.

Proof 4.17

(Proof of Theorem
4.13
)
.

Let
L={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{F})}
.
The
ZT-parity-automaton
of
L
is a
parity

automaton
recognising
L
using colours in
[{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\min_{\mathcal{F}}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\max_{\mathcal{F}}}]
, therefore, the
parity index
of
L
is at most
[{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\min_{\mathcal{F}}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\max_{\mathcal{F}}}]
.

To prove that the
parity index is not less
than
[{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\min_{\mathcal{F}}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\max_{\mathcal{F}}}]
, we use the Flower Lemma
2.16
. The language
L
is trivially recognised by a
deterministic

Muller

automaton

\mathcal{A}_{L}
with just one state
q
, transitions
q\xrightarrow{a:a}q
for each
a\in\Sigma
, and
acceptance condition
given by
L
itself. Let
n_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\preceq}}n_{2}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\preceq}}\dots\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\preceq}}n_{d}
be a branch of maximal length of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
(that must verify
d={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\max_{\mathcal{F}}}-{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\min_{\mathcal{F}}}
, and that the
root

n_{1}
is a
round node
if and only if
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\min_{\mathcal{F}}}
is even). If we let
\ell_{i}
be the
cycle
in
\mathcal{A}_{L}
containing exactly the transitions corresponding to letters in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{i})
, we obtain that
\ell_{1}\supsetneq\ell_{2}\supsetneq\dots\supsetneq\ell_{d}
is a
d
-flower
over
q
, which is
positive
if and only if
n_{1}
is a
round node
. Lemma
2.16
allows us to conclude.

Minimality of the ZT-parity-automaton with respect to deterministic automata.

Before presenting the proof of Theorem
4.14
, we prove a weaker result, namely, that the
ZT-parity-automaton
is minimal amongst
deterministic
parity automata recognising a
Muller language
.

Theorem 4.18

(Minimality of the Zielonka Tree automaton with respect to deterministic automata)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
DPA

recognising
a
Muller language

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{F})}
. Then,
|{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{parity}}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}}|\leq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}|\mathcal{A}|}
.

We recall that, by Remark
2.4
and Lemma
2.5
, we can assume that all the states of
automata

recognising

Muller languages
are
accessible
, and that any of them can be chosen to be
initial
. When considering
subautomata
of these
automata
, we will sometimes not mention their
initial state
.

Let
\mathcal{A}=(Q,\Sigma,I,\Gamma,\Delta,\mathbb{W})
be an
automaton
, and let
X\subseteq\Sigma
be a subset of the
input alphabet
.
We say that a
subgraph

\mathcal{S}
