of the
underlying graph
of
\mathcal{A}
is
X
-closed
if for every state
q
in
\mathcal{S}
and every letter
a\in X
there is some transition
q\xrightarrow{a:c}q^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{S}
.
An
X
-final strongly connected component
(
X
-FSCC) of
\mathcal{A}
is an
X
-closed

final SCC
in the graph obtained by taking the restriction of the
underlying graph
of
\mathcal{A}
to the edges labelled by letters in
X
. We remark that a subset
S\subseteq Q
is the set of states of an
X
-FSCC
if and only if:

•

for any two states
q,q^{\prime}\in S
there is a finite word
w\in X^{*}
labelling a finite
path
from
q
to
q^{\prime}
, and

•

if
q\in S
and there is a finite
path
from
q
to
q^{\prime}
labelled with a word
w\in X^{*}
, then
q^{\prime}\in S
.

Lemma 4.19
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
complete

automaton
. For every subset
X\subseteq\Sigma
,
\mathcal{A}
contains an
accessible

X
-FSCC
.

Proof 4.20
.

As any
graph
without sinks contains some
final SCC
, the
accessible part
of the restriction of
\mathcal{A}
to edges labelled by letters in
X
contains one. By
completeness
of
\mathcal{A}
, one such
final SCC
has to be an
X
-closed

subgraph
, so it is an
X
-FSCC
.

Lemma 4.21
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
DMA

recognising
a
Muller language

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{F})}
, let
X\subseteq\Sigma
and let
\mathcal{S}_{X}
be an
accessible

X
-FSCC
of
\mathcal{A}
. Then, the
automaton induced by

\mathcal{S}_{X}
is a
deterministic

automaton

recognising

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{X}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathcal{F}\mathchoice{\vphantom{\big|}}{}{}{}\right|_{X}}})}=\{w\in X^{\omega}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Inf}}(w)\in\mathcal{F}\}
.

Proof 4.22
.

Let
\mathcal{A}=(Q,\Sigma,q_{0},\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\delta},\mathbb{W})
(where
\mathbb{W}
is a
Muller language
). Let
q_{S}
be the state in
\mathcal{S}_{X}
chosen to be
initial
, and let
u_{0}\in\Sigma^{*}
be a finite word such that the
run over

u_{0}
from
q_{0}
ends in
q_{S}
.
By
prefix-independence
of
Muller languages
, a word
w\in X^{\omega}
belongs to
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{F})}
if and only if
u_{0}w\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{F})}
, and therefore,
\mathcal{A}

accepts

w
if and only if it
accepts

u_{0}w
.
Since the
run
in
\mathcal{A}
over
u_{0}w
and the
run
in
\mathcal{S}_{X}
over
w
have a suffix in common, and by
prefix-independence
of
\mathbb{W}
, we have that
w\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}_{X})}
if and only if
u_{0}w\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
if and only if
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Inf}}(w)\in\mathcal{F}
.

The next lemma states that, in a
parity

automaton
, the union of two
accepting

cycles
must be
accepting
, and similarly for
rejecting cycles
.
In Section
6.1
, we will see that this property is actually a characterisation of
parity

transition systems
(Proposition
6.13
).

Lemma 4.23
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
parity

automaton
. Let
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Cycles}(\mathcal{A})}
be two
cycles
with some
state in common
. If
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
are both
accepting
(resp.
rejecting
), then
\ell_{1}\cup\ell_{2}
is also
accepting
(resp.
rejecting
).

Proof 4.24
.

Let
\gamma\colon\Delta\to\mathbb{N}
be the
colouring function
of
\mathcal{A}
. The
cycles

\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
are
accepting
if and only if
d_{i}=\min\gamma(\ell_{i})
is even, for
i=1,2
. In this case,
\min\gamma(\ell_{1}\cup\ell_{2})=\min\{d_{1},d_{2}\}
is even. The proof is symmetric if
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
are
rejecting
.

By a small abuse of notation, we will say that two
SCC

\mathcal{S}_{1}
and
\mathcal{S}_{2}
are disjoint, and write
\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{S}_{2}=\emptyset
, if their sets of states are disjoint.

Lemma 4.25
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}2^{\Sigma}_{+}}
be a family of subsets with
Zielonka tree

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}=(N,\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\preceq}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{})
, and let
\mathcal{A}
be a
DPA

recognising

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{F})}
. Let
n\in N
be a node of the
Zielonka tree
of
\mathcal{F}
, and let
n_{1},n_{2}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Children}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}}(n)
be two different
children
of
n
. If
\mathcal{S}_{1}
and
\mathcal{S}_{2}
are two
accessible

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{1})
-FSCC
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{2})
-FSCC
in
\mathcal{A}
, respectively, then
\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{S}_{2}=\emptyset
.

Proof 4.26
.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that all states in
\mathcal{A}
are
accessible
, and since the language that
\mathcal{A}
recognises is
prefix-independent
, we can also suppose that
\mathcal{A}
is
complete
.
Let
l_{\Sigma}\colon\Delta\to\Sigma
be the labelling of the transitions of
\mathcal{A}
with
input letters
.
Let
\mathcal{S}_{i}
be a
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{i})
-FSCC
in
\mathcal{A}
, for
i=1,2
, and let
\ell_{i}
be its set of edges, which form a
cycle
satisfying
l_{\Sigma}(\ell_{i})={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{i})
.
Suppose by contradiction that
\mathcal{S}_{1}\cap\mathcal{S}_{2}\neq\emptyset
. Then
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
have some state in common, and their union is also a
cycle
satisfying
l_{\Sigma}(\ell_{1}\cup\ell_{2})={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{1})\cup{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{2})
. By Lemma
4.23
, we must have

\ell_{1}\text{ {\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}accepting} }\iff\ell_{1}\cup\ell_{2}\text{ {\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}accepting}},

contradicting the fact that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{1})\in\mathcal{F}
if and only if
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{1})\cup{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{2})\notin\mathcal{F}
(Remark
4.4
).

Proof 4.27

(Proof of Theorem
4.18
)
.

We proceed by induction in the
height
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
. For
height

1
, the result is trivial, since
|{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{parity}}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}}|=1
.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
DPA

recognising

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{F})}
. Let
n_{0}
be the
root
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
and
n_{1},n_{2},\dots,n_{k}
be an enumeration of the
children
of
n_{0}
in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
.
By Lemma
4.19
, for each
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
,
\mathcal{A}
contains some
accessible

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{i})
-FSCC

\mathcal{S}_{i}
, and by Lemma
4.25
these must be pairwise disjoint. By Lemma
4.21
, each
\mathcal{S}_{i}
induces a
deterministic

subautomaton

recognising

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{i})}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathcal{F}\mathchoice{\vphantom{\big|}}{}{}{}\right|_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{i})}}})}
. Let
\mathcal{Z}_{i}
by the
subtree of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
rooted at

n_{i}
, which we recall that is the
Zielonka tree
for
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathcal{F}\mathchoice{\vphantom{\big|}}{}{}{}\right|_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{i})}}}
. By induction hypothesis, it must be the case that
|{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Leaves}}(\mathcal{Z}_{i})|\leq|\mathcal{S}_{i}|
, so we can conclude:

|{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{parity}}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}}|=|{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Leaves}}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})|=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}|{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Leaves}}(\mathcal{Z}_{i})|\leq\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}|\mathcal{S}_{i}|\leq|\mathcal{A}|.

Minimality of the ZT-parity-automaton with respect to HD automata.

We intend to prove Theorem
4.14
, that is, that for any
\mathcal{F}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}2^{\Sigma}_{+}}
, the automaton
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{parity}}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}}
is minimal amongst
HD

parity

automata

recognising

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}(\mathcal{F})}
.
We will follow the same proof scheme than in the
deterministic
case, performing an induction over the
height
of the
Zielonka tree
. Assume that
\mathcal{A}
is an
HD

parity

automaton
for
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}(\mathcal{F})}
and that
n_{0}
is the
root
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
having
n_{1},\dots,n_{k}
as
children
. For each
child

n_{i}
we want to find an
HD

subautomaton

\mathcal{A}_{i}

recognising
the
language associated
to
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathcal{F}\mathchoice{\vphantom{\big|}}{}{}{}\right|_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{i})}}}
in such a way that the automata
\mathcal{A}_{i}
are pairwise disjoint, which would allow us to carry out the induction and obtain that
|\mathcal{A}|\geq|{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Leaves}}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})|=|{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{parity}}_{\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}}|
. Our objective will be therefore to prove:

Proposition 4.28
.

Let
n_{0}
be the
root
of the
Zielonka tree
of
\mathcal{F}
, and let
n_{1},n_{2},\dots,n_{k}
be an enumeration of the
children
of
n_{0}
. If
\mathcal{A}
is an
HD automaton

recognising

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{F})}
, then,
\mathcal{A}
contains
k
pairwise disjoint
subautomata

\mathcal{A}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{A}_{k}
that are
history-deterministic
and such that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{i})}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{i})}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathcal{F}\mathchoice{\vphantom{\big|}}{}{}{}\right|_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{i})}}})}
.

The
non-determinism
of
\mathcal{A}
will make this task considerably more laborious than in the previous paragraph, and we will have to thoroughly examine the strategies used by the
resolvers
for
\mathcal{A}
.
By the inherently asymmetric semantics of
non-deterministic

automata
, there are two well-differentiated cases to consider, depending on whether the
root
of the
Zielonka tree
is
round
(
\Sigma\in\mathcal{F}
) or
square
(
\Sigma\notin\mathcal{F}
).

In order to simplify the proof, we will assume that all states are
reachable using a sound resolver
and that all automata have a single
initial state
, which can be done without loss of generality since a
resolver
for an
HD automaton
fixes such initial state in advance.

Case 1: The root of the Zielonka tree is a
square node
:
\Sigma\notin\mathcal{F}
.

Let
\mathcal{A}=(Q,\Sigma,q_{0},\Gamma,\Delta,\mathbb{W})
be a
non-deterministic automaton
. A
memory structure
for
\mathcal{A}
is a
memory skeleton

\mathcal{M}
over
\Delta
together with a function
\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\sigma}\colon Q\times M\times\Sigma\to\Delta
, where
M
is the set of states of
\mathcal{M}
.
We say that
(\mathcal{M},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\sigma})

implements
a
resolver

(q_{0},r)
if for all
a\in\Sigma
,
r(\varepsilon,a)={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\sigma}(q_{0},m_{0},a)
and for all
\rho\in\Delta^{+}
,
r(\rho,a)={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\sigma}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Target}}(\rho),{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mu}(m_{0},\rho),a)
, where
m_{0}
is the initial state of
\mathcal{M}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mu}\colon M\times\Delta^{*}\to M
is its
update function
.

Lemma 4.29

(
[
9
]
)
.

Every
HD

parity

automaton
admits a
sound resolver

implemented by
a
finite memory structure
.

As
\mathcal{M}
is a
pointed graph
labelled with the transitions of
\mathcal{A}
, we could consider the
product automaton

\mathcal{A}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\ltimes}}\mathcal{M}
. We want to furthermore restrict the transitions of this automaton to those that are indicated by the next-move function
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\sigma}
.
Given an
automaton

\mathcal{A}
and a
memory structure

(\mathcal{M},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\sigma})
, we define their
composition
, which we write
\mathcal{A}\mathbin{\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\lhd_{\sigma}}}\mathcal{M}=(Q\times M,\Sigma,(q_{0},m_{0}),\Gamma,\Delta^{\prime},\mathbb{W})
as the
automaton
having transitions
(q,m)\xrightarrow{a:c}(q^{\prime},m^{\prime})
if
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\sigma}(q,m,a)=e=q\xrightarrow{a:c}q^{\prime}
and
\mu(m,e)=m^{\prime}
(formally,
\Delta^{\prime}
is a subset of
\Delta\times E_{\mathcal{M}}
, where
E_{\mathcal{M}}
are the edges of the
memory skeleton
). We note that
\mathcal{A}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\lhd_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\sigma}}}}\mathcal{M}
is
deterministic
, and it is
complete
if
\mathcal{A}
is.

The following lemma follows directly from the definition of
soundness
of a
resolver
and the definition of
composition
of an
automaton
and a
memory structure
.

Lemma 4.30
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an
automaton
and
(\mathcal{M},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\sigma})
a
memory structure
for
\mathcal{A}
. The
resolver implemented by

(\mathcal{M},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\sigma})
is
sound
if and only if
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{A}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\lhd_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\sigma}}}}\mathcal{M}

recognise
the same language.

For the rest of the paragraph, we let
\mathcal{A}=(Q,\Sigma,q_{0},\mathbb{N},\Delta,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{parity}})
be a
complete

history-deterministic

automaton

recognising
the
Muller language

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{\mathcal{F}}(\Sigma)}
admitting a
sound resolver

(q_{0},r)
implemented by a
memory structure

(\mathcal{M},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\sigma})
.
We let
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\pi}_{\mathcal{A}}\colon\mathcal{A}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\lhd_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\sigma}}}}\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{A}
be the
morphism of automata
given by the projection into the first component:
\pi_{\mathcal{A},V}(q,m)=q
and
\pi_{\mathcal{A},E}(e_{1},e_{2})=e_{1}
.

Remark 4.31
.

If
\rho
is a path in
\mathcal{A}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\lhd_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\sigma}}}}\mathcal{M}
that is labelled by
input letters

a_{0}a_{1}\dots\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\Sigma^{\infty}}
and producing
output

c_{0}c_{1}\dots\in\mathbb{N}^{\infty}
, then the
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\pi}_{\mathcal{A}}
-projection of
\rho
is a path in
\mathcal{A}
labelled by
a_{0}a_{1}\dots\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\Sigma^{\infty}}
and producing
c_{0}c_{1}\dots\in\mathbb{N}^{\infty}
as
output
.

Lemma 4.32
.

Let
X\subseteq\Sigma
and let
\mathcal{S}_{X}
be an
accessible

X
-FSCC
of
\mathcal{A}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\lhd_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\sigma}}}}\mathcal{M}
. Then,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\pi}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{S}_{X})

induces
an
HD

subautomaton
of
\mathcal{A}
recognising
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{X}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathcal{F}\mathchoice{\vphantom{\big|}}{}{}{}\right|_{X}}})}=\{w\in X^{\omega}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Inf}}(w)\in\mathcal{F}\}
.

Proof 4.33
.

Let
q_{S}
be a state in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\pi}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{S}_{X})
chosen to be initial. Let
m_{S}
be a state in
\mathcal{M}
such that
(q_{S},m_{S})\in\mathcal{S}_{X}
. By Lemma
4.21
,
\mathcal{S}_{X}
induces a
deterministic

subautomaton
with initial state
(q_{S},m_{S})

recognising

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{X}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathcal{F}\mathchoice{\vphantom{\big|}}{}{}{}\right|_{X}}})}
.
On the one hand, since
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\pi}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{S}_{X})
is an
accessible

subautomaton
of
\mathcal{A}
having only transitions labelled by
X
and by
prefix-independence
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
, we have that

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\pi}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{S}_{X}))}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}\cap X^{\omega}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{X}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathcal{F}\mathchoice{\vphantom{\big|}}{}{}{}\right|_{X}}})}.

On the other hand, the projection of any
accepting run
in
\mathcal{S}_{X}
provides an
accepting run
in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\pi}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{S}_{X})
(by Remark
4.31
), so

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{S}_{X})}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{X}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathcal{F}\mathchoice{\vphantom{\big|}}{}{}{}\right|_{X}}})}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\pi}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{S}_{X}))}.

Moreover, a
sound resolver
for
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\pi}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{S}_{X})
is
implemented by

({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{M}_{m_{S}}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\sigma})
(the
memory structure
with initial state set to
m_{S}
).

Lemma 4.34
.

Let
n\in N
be a
square node
of the
Zielonka tree
of
\mathcal{F}
(
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)\notin\mathcal{F}
), and let
n_{1},n_{2}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Children}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}}(n)
be two different
children
of
n
. If
\mathcal{S}_{1}
and
\mathcal{S}_{2}
are two
accessible

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{1})
-FSCC
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{2})
-FSCC
in
\mathcal{A}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\lhd_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\sigma}}}}\mathcal{M}
, respectively, then
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\pi}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{S}_{1})\cap{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\pi}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{S}_{2})=\emptyset
.

Proof 4.35
.

Suppose by contradiction that there is some state
q
in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\pi}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{S}_{1})\cap{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\pi}_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{S}_{2})
, and let
m_{1},m_{2}\in M
be such that
(q,m_{1})
and
(q,m_{2})
are states in
\mathcal{S}_{1}
and
\mathcal{S}_{2}
, respectively. For
i=1,2
, let
\ell_{i}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Cycles}_{(q,m_{i})}(\mathcal{A}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\lhd_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\sigma}}}}\mathcal{M})}
be the
cycle over
(q,m_{i})
containing all edges in
\mathcal{S}_{i}
. We note that
l_{\Sigma}(\ell_{i})={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{i})
and therefore
\min\gamma(\ell_{i})
has to be even (as
\mathcal{A}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\lhd_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\sigma}}}}\mathcal{M}
is
deterministic
), where
l_{\Sigma}
and
\gamma
are the labellings of
\mathcal{A}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\lhd_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\sigma}}}}\mathcal{M}
with
input letters
and
output colours
, respectively. By Remark
4.31
, the
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\pi}_{\mathcal{A}}
-projections of
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
are
cycles over
q
in
\mathcal{A}
labelled with
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{1})
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{2})
and in which the minimal colour appearing is even.
By alternating these two
cycles
, we can build an
accepting run
in
\mathcal{A}

over
a word
w\in\Sigma^{\omega}
with
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Inf}}(w)={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{1})\cup{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{2})
, contradicting the fact that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{1})\cup{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{2})\notin\mathcal{F}
(Remark
4.4
).

Lemmas
4.19
,
4.32
and
4.34
imply Proposition
4.28
in the case in which the
root
of the
Zielonka tree
is a
square node
.

Case 2: The root of the Zielonka tree is a
round node
:
\Sigma\in\mathcal{F}
.

Before presenting the formal proof, let us discuss why considering these two cases separately is necessary. A first idea to obtain the desired result would be to follow the same steps as in Case 1. However, this approach encounters a major difficulty: the argument used in the proof of Lemma
4.34
is not valid if
\Sigma\in\mathcal{F}
. Indeed, even if we can find two
rejecting

cycles

\ell_{1}
,
\ell_{2}
such that
l_{\Sigma}(\ell_{i})={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{i})
, their
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\pi}_{\mathcal{A}}
-projections could a priori have a
state in common
; this would imply the
existence
of a
rejecting run
over the set of letters
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{1})\cup{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{2})\in\mathcal{F}
, which is not enough to conclude, as the non-determinism of
\mathcal{A}
leaves room for the existence of other
accepting runs
over this set of letters.
To circumvent this difficulty, we need to take a closer look at the strategies used by the
resolver
. Rather than considering any
finite memory strategy
resolving the
non-determinism
of
\mathcal{A}
, we will show that we can choose a specific
resolver
for which we will be able to obtain a result analogous to Lemma
4.34
.
To do this, we first construct the
letter game
of
\mathcal{A}
, as introduced in
[
44
]
, which is a
Muller

game
satisfying that a
strategy
for it yields a
resolver
for
\mathcal{A}
.
The
strategy
that we will use in this
game
is the one obtained by applying McNaughton’s algorithm to solve
Muller

games

[
66
]
guided by the
Zielonka tree
, as presented in
[
34
]
.

Let
\mathcal{A}=(Q,\Sigma,q_{0},[d_{\min},d_{\max}],\Delta,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{parity}})
be a
parity

automaton

recognising

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}(\mathcal{F})}
, and assume that
\Sigma\cap[d_{\min},d_{\max}]=\emptyset
.
The
letter game
for
\mathcal{A}
is the
game

\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}}
defined as follows:

•

The set of vertices is
V=Q\sqcup(Q\times\Sigma)
.
Adam

controls
vertices in
Q
, and
Eve

controls
vertices in
Q\times\Sigma
.

•

For each letter
a\in\Sigma
and each
q\in Q
, there is an edge
q\xrightarrow{a}(q,a)
.

•

For each position
(q,a)\in Q\times\Sigma
, and for each transition
q\xrightarrow{a:c}q^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{A}
, there is an edge
(q,a)\xrightarrow{c}q^{\prime}
.

•

The set of colours is
\Gamma=\Sigma\sqcup[d_{\min},d_{\max}]
, and the
acceptance set
is the
Muller language associated to

\mathbin{\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\mathcal{F}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{parity}}}}=\{C\subseteq\Gamma\mid[C\cap\Sigma\in\mathcal{F}]\implies[\min(C\cap[d_{\min},d_{\max}])\text{ is even}]\}.

That is, in the
letter game
,
Adam
provides
input letters
one by one, and
Eve
chooses transitions corresponding to those letters in the
automaton

\mathcal{A}
.
Eve

wins
this
game
if she manages to build an
accepting run
every time that
Adam
gives as input an infinite word in the language
recognised
by
\mathcal{A}
.

Figure 11
:
Letter game
for the
HD automaton
from Figure
1
,
recognising
the
Muller language associated to

\mathcal{F}=\{\{a\},\{b\},\{c\},\{a,b\},\{b,c\}\}
. Squares represent
Adam’s
vertices (the states of the automaton) and circles
Eve’s
ones.
Blue edges correspond to input-letters, and orange edges to the choices of transitions in the automaton after each input letter.
The only vertex where
Eve
has a non-trivial choice to make in order to resolve the
non-determinism
of the automaton is
(q_{1},b)
. In this example,
Eve
has a
winning strategy
corresponding to the
resolver
described in Example
2.3
.

We remark that a
subgraph
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}}
induces a
subautomaton
of
\mathcal{A}
via the (partial) mapping
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\mathsf{aut}_{\mathcal{A}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\rightharpoonup}\mathcal{A}
that sends states of the form
q\in Q
to
q
and edges of the form
(q,a)\xrightarrow{c}q^{\prime}
to
q\xrightarrow{a:c}q^{\prime}
.

Remark 4.36
.

A
strategy
for
Eve
in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}}
induces a
resolver
in
\mathcal{A}
, which is
sound
if and only if the
strategy
is
winning
.

Remark 4.37
.

If two subsets of vertices of the
letter game

S_{1},S_{2}\subseteq V
are disjoint, then
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{aut}_{\mathcal{A}}}(S_{1})\cap{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{aut}_{\mathcal{A}}}(S_{2})=\emptyset
.

Remark 4.38
.

If
\rho
is a
play
in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}}
, labelled
a_{0}c_{0}a_{1}c_{1}\dots\in(\Sigma\cdot[d_{\min},d_{\max}])^{\infty}
, the
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{aut}_{\mathcal{A}}}
-projection of
\rho
is a
run
in
\mathcal{A}
over
a_{0}a_{1}\dots\in\Sigma^{\infty}
producing
c_{0}c_{1}\dots\in[d_{\min},d_{\max}]^{\infty}
as
output
.

Lemma 4.39

(
[
44
]
)
.

A
parity

automaton

\mathcal{A}
is
HD
if and only if
Eve

wins
the
letter game

from
some
initial state
of
\mathcal{A}
.

For a subset
X
of vertices or edges of a
game

\mathcal{G}
, we define Eve’s
attractor to
X
as:

\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathcal{G}}(X)}=\{v\in V\mid\text{ there is a strategy for Eve ensuring to eventually visit }X\text{ from }v\}.

For a colour
c\in\Gamma
we note
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathcal{G}}(c)}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathcal{G}}(E_{c})}
, where
E_{c}
is the set of edges coloured
c
.

For the rest of the paragraph, let
\mathcal{A}=(Q,\Sigma,q_{0},[0,d],\Delta,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{parity}})
be a
complete

history-deterministic

parity

automaton

recognising

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}(\mathcal{F})}
. We can assume without loss of generality that the minimal colour that it uses is
0
.
We let
V
and
E
denote the sets of vertices and edges, respectively, of the
letter game
and
\Gamma=\Sigma\sqcup[0,d]
its set of colours. Whenever we use expressions like “the minimal colour appearing in a play”, it will refer to the restriction of
\Gamma
to
[0,d]
.
From the
prefix-independence
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}(\mathcal{F})}
we can moreover assume that
Eve

wins
the
letter game
from any vertex (see Lemma
2.5
).
We let
n_{0}
be the
root
of the
Zielonka tree
of
\mathcal{F}
(assumed to be
round
, that is
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{0})\in\mathcal{F}
), let
n_{1},\dots,n_{k}
be its
children
, and let
\Sigma_{i}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{i})\subseteq\Sigma
(note that
\Sigma_{i}\notin\mathcal{F}
for
i\geq 1
).

Let us examine the condition
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{F}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{parity}}}}
used in the
letter game
a bit closer. The first levels of the
Zielonka tree
of this condition are depicted in Figure
12
. It is clear that a
strategy
in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}}
ensuring to produce colour
0
infinitely often is
winning
. It might be the case that
Adam
can prevent
Eve
from doing this, however, since
Eve

wins

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}}
, in that case she could ensure to produce infinitely often a set of colours included in some of the
round nodes
below the
root
, that is, to either avoid colour
1
, or to produce letters included in some
\Sigma_{i}
. We use this idea to define next
attractor decompositions
for
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}}
.

Figure 12
:
First levels of the
Zielonka tree
of the Muller condition
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{F}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{parity}}}}
, which is the
winning condition
of the
letter game

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}}
.

Given a subset of vertices
V^{\prime}\subseteq V
we write
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}(V^{\prime})}
to denote the subgame of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}}
containing the vertices of
V^{\prime}
and the edges between them.

Let
x
be an even integer. For a subgame
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}(V^{\prime})}
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}}
with no colour strictly smaller than
x
, we define an
x
-attractor decomposition
of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
as a partition of
V^{\prime}
into

V^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}(x)}\sqcup V_{1}\sqcup A_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup V_{l}\sqcup A_{l},

satisfying:

•

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}(x)}
is Eve’s
attractor to
x
in
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.

•

For each
V_{j}
, either (1) there is some
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
such that no colour of
\Sigma\setminus\Sigma_{i}
appears in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}(V_{j})}
, or (2)
Eve
has a
winning strategy
for
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}(V_{j})}
(from any vertex) avoiding colour
x+1
; and in both cases, if
Adam
can leave
V_{j}
taking an edge
v\xrightarrow{a}v^{\prime}
(
v\in V_{j}
,
v^{\prime}\notin V_{j}
), then
v^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}(x)}\sqcup V_{1}\sqcup A_{1}\sqcup\dots V_{j-1}\sqcup A_{j-1}
.
In case (1) we say that
V_{j}
is a
\Sigma_{i}
-region of the attractor decomposition
and in case (2) that
V_{j}
is an
x+1
-avoiding region
.

•

Eve

wins

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}(V_{j})}
from every vertex for all
j
.

•

A_{j}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathcal{G}_{j}}(V_{j})}
, where
\mathcal{G}_{j}
is the subgame induced by the subset of vertices given by
V\setminus\left({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}(x)}\sqcup V_{1}\allowbreak\sqcup\dots\allowbreak\sqcup\allowbreak V_{j-1}\allowbreak\sqcup A_{j-1}\right)
(we note that this game does not contain edges coloured with
x
).

If
V_{j}
is an
x+1
-avoiding region
, we let
\mathcal{G}_{j}^{\prime}
be the subgame obtained from
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}(V_{j})}
by removing the transitions labelled
x+1
.

An
x
-recursive attractor decomposition
of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is:

\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}}=\langle{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}(x)},(V_{1},A_{1},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{G}_{1}^{\prime}}}),(V_{2},A_{2},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{G}_{2}^{\prime}}}),\dots,(V_{l},A_{l},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{G}_{l}^{\prime}}})\rangle,

where
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}(x)}\sqcup V_{1}\sqcup A_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup V_{l}\sqcup A_{l}
is an
x
-attractor decomposition
of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
, and, if
V_{j}
is an
x+1
-avoiding region
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{G}_{j}^{\prime}}}
is an
x+2
-recursive attractor decomposition
of
\mathcal{G}_{j}^{\prime}
. (If
V_{j}
is an
\Sigma_{i}
-region
,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{G}_{j}^{\prime}}}
can be disregarded).

A representation of an attractor decomposition appears in Figure
13
.

Figure 13
:
On the left, a
0
-attractor decomposition
of a game
\mathcal{G}
. On the right, the coloured part represents a
2
-attractor decomposition
of the subgame
\mathcal{G}_{2}^{\prime}
induced by the
1
-avoiding region

V_{2}
. Since no
3
-avoiding region
appears on it, this is a
full attractor decomposition
of the game
\mathcal{G}
, inducing a partition into three different kinds of regions.
The order over the
\Sigma_{i}
-regions
is given by
V_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}<_{\mathcal{D}}}}V_{2,1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}<_{\mathcal{D}}}}V_{2,2}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}<_{\mathcal{D}}}}V_{3}
.
Adam can only force to decrease with respect to this order, that is, at each sublevel of the decomposition,
Adam
cannot force to go to the right.

We say that a subgame
\mathcal{S}
of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is a
\Sigma_{i}
-region of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}}
if it is a
\Sigma_{i}
-region
of some of the recursively defined
attractor decompositions
. Similarly, for
y>x
an odd integer, we say that
\mathcal{S}
is a
y
-avoiding region of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}}
if it is a
y
-avoiding region
of some of the recursively defined
attractor decompositions
. We say that the full game
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is an
x-1
-avoiding region (note that
x
might take the value
0
).
We remark that for any subset
S
of vertices of
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
there is one and only one minimal
y
-avoiding region of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}}
containing
S
(note that
y
might equal
-1
).

Remark 4.40
.

A
0
-recursive attractor decomposition

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{D}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}}}}
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}}
induces a partition of the vertices into

V=S_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup S_{r}\;\sqcup\;A_{1}\sqcup\dots A_{r}\;\sqcup\;B_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup B_{s},

such that:

•

S_{j}
is a
\Sigma_{i}
-region of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{D}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}}}}
, for some
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
,

•

A_{j}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathcal{G}_{j}}(S_{j})}
for some subgame
\mathcal{G}_{j}
appearing at some level of the
decomposition
,

•

B_{j}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathcal{G}_{j}^{\prime}}(x)}
for some even integer
x
and some
x-1
-avoiding region

\mathcal{G}_{j}^{\prime}
appearing at some level of the
decomposition
.

Moreover, such a
decomposition
induces a total order over the
\Sigma_{i}
-regions
: for two sets
S_{t}
,
S_{t^{\prime}}
, we write
S_{t}\mathrel{\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}<_{\mathcal{D}}}}S_{t^{\prime}}
if there are two regions
V_{j}
,
V_{j^{\prime}}
belonging to the same
attractor decomposition
in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{D}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}}}}
such that
j<j^{\prime}
,
S_{t}\subseteq V_{j}
and
S_{t^{\prime}}\subseteq V_{k^{\prime}}
.

We call such a partition a
full attractor decomposition
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}}
. We remark that, by definition of an
attractor decomposition
,
Eve

wins

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{j})}
from every vertex for every
S_{j}
.
See Figure
13
for an illustration.

The proof that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}}
admits a
full attractor decomposition
uses the ideas appearing in
[
34
, Section 3]
.

Lemma 4.41
.

Let
x
be an even integer. If
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is a subgame of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}}
with no colour smaller than
x
and such that
Eve
can
win
from every vertex, then it admits an
x
-attractor decomposition
.
In particular,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}}
admits a
full attractor decomposition
.

Proof 4.42
.

We assume without loss of generality that
x=0
.
Suppose that
V_{1},A_{1},\dots V_{j-1},A_{j-1}
have already been defined and that they verify the desired properties. Suppose that the game
\mathcal{G}_{j}
with vertices
V\setminus\left({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}(0)}\sqcup V_{1}\sqcup\dots V_{j-1}\sqcup A_{j-1}\right)
is non-empty. First, note that
Eve

wins

\mathcal{G}_{j}
from any position. Indeed,
Eve

wins

\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
from any vertex
v
in
\mathcal{G}_{j}
(as we suppose that she can
win

\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
starting anywhere); moreover, since
v\notin A_{j^{\prime}}
for any
j^{\prime}<j
,
Adam
has a
strategy
from
v
forcing to remain in
\mathcal{G}_{j}
, and
Eve
has to be able to
win
against any such
strategy
.

We prove that either (1) there is some
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
and
v
vertex in
\mathcal{G}_{j}
such that
Eve
has a
winning strategy
from
v
forcing to produce no colour in
\Sigma\setminus\Sigma_{i}
, or (2) there is some vertex
v
in
\mathcal{G}_{j}
such that
Eve
has a
winning strategy
from
v
avoiding colour
1
. Suppose by contradiction that this was not the case. Then, Adam can use the following strategy: first, he forces producing colour
1
, then, a colour not in
\Sigma_{1}
, followed by a colour not in
\Sigma_{2}
, and continues this pattern until a colour not in
\Sigma_{l}
is produced (and this without producing colour
0
, since no
0
-edge appears in
\mathcal{G}_{j}
). Afterward, he continues repeating these steps in a round-robin fashion. This allows him to produce a play winning for him (the word produced is in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}(\mathcal{F})}
while the minimal number produced is
1
), contradicting the fact that
Eve

wins

\mathcal{G}_{j}
from
v
.

We assume that we are in the case (1) (case (2) is identical), so from some vertices
Eve
can
win
producing no colour in
\Sigma\setminus\Sigma_{i}
. We let
V_{j}
be the set of such vertices, and for each of them we fix a
strategy

\mathsf{strat}_{v}
that is
winning
in
\mathcal{G}_{j}
and avoids colours in
\Sigma\setminus\Sigma_{i}
. By definition of
V_{j}
, if
\rho=v\mathrel{\hbox to13.34pt{\vbox to5.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.67142pt\lower-3.25276pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.11865pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{70}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}
}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}
}
{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.57 -3.26 L 3.42 -3.26 L 2.24 -4.22 L -0.12 -2.29 L -2.49 -4.22 L -4.85 -2.29 L -7.21 -4.22 L -8.95 -3.26 L -8.95 -3.26}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.47144pt}{-2.35277pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.11 0 C -0.28 0.28 -1.66 0.83 -3.32 1.8 C -1.66 0.55 -1.66 -0.55 -3.32 -1.8 C -1.66 -0.83 -0.28 -0.28 1.11 0 Z}{} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

is a finite play
consistent with

\mathsf{strat}_{v}
in
\mathcal{G}_{j}
, then
v^{\prime}\in V_{j}
(Eve can still win without producing colours in
\Sigma\setminus\Sigma_{i}
), so
Adam
cannot force leaving
V_{j}
. This proves that:

1.

\mathsf{strat}_{v}
is
winning
in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}(V_{j})}
from
v
,

2.

if
v\in V_{j}
is controlled by
Adam
and
v\xrightarrow{}v^{\prime}
is an edge in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}}
, then
v^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Attr}_{\mathcal{G}^{\prime}}(0)}\sqcup V_{1}\sqcup A_{1}\sqcup\dots V_{j-1}\sqcup A_{j-1}\sqcup V_{j}
.

Also, if a vertex
v
controlled by Adam is in
V_{j}
, no edge
v\xrightarrow{a\notin\Sigma_{i}}v^{\prime}
appears in
\mathcal{G}_{j}
, so no colour of
\Sigma\setminus\Sigma_{i}
appears in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}(V_{j})}
.

To finish the proof, we define
A_{j}
to be the attractor of
V_{j}
in
\mathcal{G}_{j}
.

The existence of a
full attractor decomposition
for
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}}
follows from the fact that any
x+1
-avoiding region
of an
x
-attractor decomposition
verifies the hypothesis of the lemma.

Lemma 4.43

(
[
66
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a
game
using a
Muller

acceptance condition
such that
Eve

wins

\mathcal{G}
from every vertex. Then, there is a finite
memory structure

(\mathcal{M},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\sigma})
over
\mathcal{G}

implementing
a
winning strategy

uniformly
, that is, for every vertex
v
of
\mathcal{G}
there is a memory state
m_{v}
in
\mathcal{M}
such that the
memory structure

({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{M}_{m_{v}}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\sigma})

implements
a
winning strategy
from
v
.

For the rest of the paragraph, we fix a
0
-recursive attractor decomposition

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{D}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}}}}
for
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}}
and let
S_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}<_{\mathcal{D}}}}S_{2}\dots\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}<_{\mathcal{D}}}}S_{r}
be the
\Sigma_{i}
-regions
of the induced
full attractor decomposition
.
For each region
S_{j}
we fix a
memory structure

(\mathcal{M}_{j},\sigma_{j})

uniformly

implementing
a
winning strategy
for
Eve
in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{j})}
(as given by Lemma
4.43
).
As in the previous paragraph, we can consider the
composition

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{j})}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\lhd_{\sigma_{j}}}}\mathcal{M}_{j}
consisting of the
product game
in which the choices for
Eve
are restricted to those of the form

(v,m)\xrightarrow{c}(v^{\prime},m^{\prime})
if
\sigma_{j}(v,m)=e=v\xrightarrow{c}v^{\prime}
and
\mu_{j}(m,e)=m^{\prime}
.
By definition,
Eve
does not have any choice in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{j})}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\lhd_{\sigma_{j}}}}\mathcal{M}_{j}
, and since
(\mathcal{M}_{j},\sigma_{j})

implements
a
winning strategy
, any infinite
path
in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{j})}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\lhd_{\sigma_{j}}}}\mathcal{M}_{j}
produces a set of colours in
\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{F}\rightarrow{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{parity}}}}
.
We let
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{j})}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\lhd_{\sigma_{j}}}}\mathcal{M}_{j}\to{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{j})}
be the projection into
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{j})}
.

A
subgraph

G_{j}
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{j})}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\lhd_{\sigma_{j}}}}\mathcal{M}_{j}
is
X
-Adam-closed
, for a subset
X\subseteq\Sigma
, if for every vertex
(q,m)
controlled by Adam and every
a\in X
, the transition
(q,m)\xrightarrow{a}((q,a),m^{\prime})
remains in
G_{j}
. We say that
G_{j}
is an
X
-FSCC
if it is a
final SCC
of the restriction of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{j})}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\lhd_{\sigma_{j}}}}\mathcal{M}_{j}
to the graph where Adam’s choices are restricted to letters in
X
that is moreover
X
-Adam-closed
.
We say that a
subgraph

\mathcal{G}
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}}
is an
X
-closed subgame
(with respect to the
attractor decomposition

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{D}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}}}}
and a family of
finite memory

strategies
) if
\mathcal{G}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\pi_{\mathcal{G}}}(G_{j})
for
G_{j}
some
X
-Adam-closed

SCC
of some product
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{j})}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\lhd_{\sigma_{j}}}}\mathcal{M}_{j}
.

Intuitively, an
X
-closed subgame

\mathcal{G}
of the
letter game
is a subgame included in a region
S_{j}
of the
full attractor decomposition
such that, if
Adam
only provides letters in
X
and
Eve
plays according to the strategy defined by the
memory structures

\mathcal{M}_{j}
, the play will never leave
\mathcal{G}
.

Lemma 4.44
.

Eve

wins
any
X
-closed subgame
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}}
(from any vertex).

Proof 4.45
.

In an
X
-closed subgame
included in a region
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{j})}
,
Adam’s
moves have been restricted; however, all Eve’s moves coming from the
strategy

implemented by

(M_{j},\sigma_{j})
are available. Therefore, this
strategy
is also
winning
in such a subgame, since it is
winning
in the full
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{j})}
.

Putting this lemma together with Remark
4.36
we obtain:

Lemma 4.46
.

Let
X\subseteq\Sigma
, and let
\mathcal{G}_{X}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}}
be an
X
-closed subgame
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}}
. The
subautomaton
of
\mathcal{A}

induced by

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{aut}_{\mathcal{A}}}(\mathcal{G}_{X})
is
HD
and
recognises

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{X}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathcal{F}\mathchoice{\vphantom{\big|}}{}{}{}\right|_{X}}})}
.

Lemma 4.47
.

If a product
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{A}}(S_{j})}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\lhd_{\sigma_{j}}}}\mathcal{M}_{j}
does not contain any
X
-Adam-closed subgraph
, for
X\subseteq\Sigma
, then from any vertex
(q,m)

Adam
can force leaving
S_{j}
while playing only letters in
X
.
That is, there is a path
(q,m)\mathrel{\hbox to13.34pt{\vbox to5.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.67142pt\lower-3.25276pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
