is the finite run corresponding to
\ell^{\prime}
fixed in the definition of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{A}_{(\varphi^{-1},v^{\prime})}}
.
By definition, the obtained resolver satisfies the following property:

\displaystyle\text{If }e_{1}e_{2}\dots\in\Delta^{\omega}\text{ is the {\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}run induced by} }r_{\mathcal{A}}\text{ over }\ell_{1}^{\prime}\ell_{2}^{\prime}\dots\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Cycles}_{v^{\prime}}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime})}^{\omega},

\displaystyle\text{then }\rho_{0}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Unfold}}(e_{1}e_{2}\dots)={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r}_{\varphi,\mathpzc{Runs}}(\rho_{0}^{\prime}\rho_{1}^{\prime}\rho_{2}^{\prime}\dots).

This gives us:

\bigcup{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Inf}}(\ell_{1}^{\prime},\ell_{2}^{\prime},\dots)\text{ is {\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}accepting cycle} in }\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}\iff\rho_{0}^{\prime}\rho_{1}^{\prime}\rho_{2}^{\prime}\dots\text{ is {\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}accepting run} in }\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}\implies\\
\implies\rho_{0}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Unfold}}(e_{1}e_{2}\dots)\text{ {\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}accepting run} in }\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}\iff e_{1}e_{2}\dots\text{ {\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}accepting run} in }{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{A}_{(\varphi^{-1},v^{\prime})}}.

(1)

Which allows us to conclude that the
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{A}_{(\varphi^{-1},v^{\prime})}}

recognises

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{LocalMuller}_{v^{\prime}}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS})}
and that
r_{\mathcal{A}}
is a
sound

resolver
.

Corollary 5.55
.

Let
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}
be a
Muller
and a
parity

transition system
, respectively, and let
\varphi\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}\to\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
be an
HD mapping
. Let
v
be an
accessible

recurrent
state of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
. Then,

|\varphi^{-1}(v)|\geq|{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Leaves}}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{LocalMuller}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}(v)}}})|=|{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Leaves}}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{T}_{v}})|.

Proof 5.56
.

By Lemma
5.53
, the
automaton

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{A}_{(\varphi^{-1},v)}}
is a
history-deterministic
automaton
recognising

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{LocalMuller}_{v}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS})}
of size
|\varphi^{-1}(v)|
, and by Lemma
5.51
, it is a
parity

automaton
. The optimality of the
ZT-parity-automaton
(Theorem
4.14
) gives us the first inequality. The second equality follows from the fact that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{T}_{v}}
is the
Zielonka tree
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{LocalMuller}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}(v)}
(Lemma
5.9
).

The next corollary admits an identical proof, using the optimality of the
ZT-HD-Rabin-automaton
(Theorem
4.64
).

Corollary 5.57
.

Let
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}
be a
Muller
and a
Rabin

transition system
, respectively, and let
\varphi\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}\to\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
be an
HD mapping
. Let
v
be an
accessible

recurrent
state of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
. Then,

|\varphi^{-1}(v)|\geq|{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{rbw}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{LocalMuller}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}(v)}}})}|=|{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{rbw}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{T}_{v}})}|.

Theorems
5.35
and
5.36
follow from these two corollaries, the formulas for the size of the
ACD-transforms
(Remarks
5.15
and
5.26
) and the fact that a
locally surjective morphism

\varphi:\widetilde{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}\to\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
is
surjective
if all vertices of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
are
accessible
(Lemma
3.5
).

6
Corollaries

In this section, we discuss some further applications of the
Zielonka tree
and the
alternating cycle decomposition
.
In Section
6.1
, we use the insights gained from the
ACD
to conduct a comprehensive study of
typeness
results for
deterministic

Muller

automata
(that is, when can we relabel a
DMA
with an
equivalent
and simpler
acceptance condition
).
In Section
6.2
we present a
normal form
for
parity

transition systems
and prove the main properties exhibited by
TS
in this form.
In Section
6.3
, we provide a polynomial-time algorithm minimising
DPA

recognising

Muller languages
.

6.1
Typeness results

As we have seen, there are many different types of
acceptance conditions
for
\omega
-regular automata. An important question is the following:

Question: Given a
Muller

automaton

\mathcal{A}
, can we define a simpler
acceptance condition
over the
underlying graph
of
\mathcal{A}
obtaining an
equivalent
automaton
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
?

This question was first studied (in the context of
automata
using state-based acceptance) by Krishnan, Puri and Brayton
[
55
,
56
]
, who showed how to determine if a
DMA
can be relabelled with an
equivalent

Büchi

condition
. Their work was generalised to
parity

automata
by Boker, Kupferman and Steinitz
[
11
]
, and related questions about
typeness
were studied for
non-deterministic

automata
by Kupferman, Morgenstern and Murano
[
59
]
, and for
history-deterministic
automata by Boker, Kupferman and Skrzypczak
[
10
]
.

In this section, we provide new general characterisations of
typeness
for
Muller

transition systems
. The main contributions of this section appear in Propositions
6.9
,
6.11
and
6.13
, which characterise when a
Muller

TS
can be relabelled with
equivalent

parity
,
Rabin
, or
Streett

conditions
in terms of properties of the
cycles
of the
TS
. For instance, Proposition
6.9
states that a
Muller

TS
can be relabelled with an
equivalent

Rabin

condition
if and only if its
rejecting

cycles
are closed under union. The “only if” part of these results was already known
[
63
]
, but the fact that this is indeed a characterisation is a novel result, for which the use of the
ACD
is essential.
These characterisations directly imply the results from
[
11
,
55
,
56
]
.
We also show how to use the
ACD
to determine the
parity index
of the
language recognised
by a
DMA
(Proposition
6.16
), which can be seen as a simplification of the results from
[
56
, Section 3.2]
.
Further results concerning
generalised Büchi languages
and
weak automata
can be found in Appendix
A
.

6.1.1
Typeness for Muller languages

We first present some results proven by Zielonka
[
94
, Section 5]
that show how we can use the
Zielonka tree
to deduce if a
Muller language
is a
Rabin
, a
Streett
or a
parity language
. These results are generalised to
transition systems
in the next subsection. A study of further types of
Muller languages
can be found in Appendix
A
.

We do not include the proofs of the results of this section in the main body of the paper, as they are known results
[
94
, Section 5]
and they are special cases of the proofs in Section
6.1.2
. Nevertheless, we include them in Appendix
E
.

We first introduce some definitions. The terminology will be justified by the upcoming results.

Definition 6.1
.

Let
T
be a
tree
with nodes partitioned into
round
nodes and
square
nodes. We say that
T
has:

•

Rabin shape
if every
round node
has at most one
child
.

•

Streett shape
if every
square node
has at most one
child
.

•

Parity shape
if every node has at most one
child
.

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}2^{\Gamma}_{+}}
be a family of non-empty subsets. The following conditions are equivalent:

1.

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{\Gamma}(\mathcal{F})}
is a
Rabin language
.

2.

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}2^{\Gamma}_{+}}\setminus\mathcal{F}
is closed under union: If
C_{1}\notin\mathcal{F}
and
C_{2}\notin\mathcal{F}
, then
C_{1}\cup C_{2}\notin\mathcal{F}
.

3.

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
has
Rabin shape
.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}2^{\Gamma}_{+}}
be a family of non-empty subsets. The following conditions are equivalent:

1.

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{\Gamma}(\mathcal{F})}
is a
Streett language
.

2.

The family
\mathcal{F}
is closed under union.

3.

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
has
Streett shape
.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}2^{\Gamma}_{+}}
be a family of non-empty subsets. The following conditions are equivalent:

1.

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{\Gamma}(\mathcal{F})}
is a
parity language
.

2.

Both
\mathcal{F}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}2^{\Gamma}_{+}}\setminus\mathcal{F}
are closed under union: If
C_{1}\in\mathcal{F}\iff C_{2}\in\mathcal{F}
, then,
C_{1}\cup C_{2}\in\mathcal{F}\iff C_{1}\in\mathcal{F}
.

3.

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
has
parity shape
.

Moreover, if some of these conditions is satisfied,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{\Gamma}(\mathcal{F})}
is a
[{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\min_{\mathcal{F}}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\max_{\mathcal{F}}}]
-parity language
.

Corollary 6.5
.

A
Muller language

L\subseteq\Gamma^{\omega}
is a
parity language
if and only if it is both a
Rabin
and a
Streett language
.

6.1.2
Typeness for Muller transition systems and deterministic automata

We start this subsection by introducing the necessary definitions about
equivalence of acceptance conditions
and
typeness
. Then, we state and prove our main contributions concerning
typeness
of
transition systems
.

Equivalence of acceptance conditions and typeness.

Let
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}_{1}=(G,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{1})
and
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}_{2}=(G,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{2})
be two
transitions systems
over the same
underlying graph

G
, with
acceptance conditions

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{i}=(\gamma_{i},\Gamma_{i},\mathbb{W}_{i})
, for
i\in\{1,2\}
.
We say that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{1}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{2}
are
equivalent over
G
, written
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{1}\mathrel{\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\simeq}_{G}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{2}
, if for all
runs

\rho\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Run}(G)}
,
\rho
is
accepting
for
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}_{1}
if and only if it is
accepting
for
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}_{2}
; that is,
\gamma_{1}(\rho)\in\mathbb{W}_{1}\iff\gamma_{2}(\rho)\in\mathbb{W}_{2}
.

We write
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}_{1}\mathrel{\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\simeq}}\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}_{2}
if
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}_{1}
and
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}_{2}
are
isomorphic
. We recall that two
transition systems
are
isomorphic
if there is a
morphism of transition systems

\varphi\colon\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}_{1}\to\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}_{2}
whose inverse is also a
morphism
, that is,
\varphi
and
\varphi^{-1}

preserve the acceptance of runs
.

Remark 6.6
.

If
\varphi\colon\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}_{1}\to\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}_{2}
is an
isomorphism
, then
(\gamma_{2}\circ\varphi,\Gamma_{2},\mathbb{W}_{2})
is an
acceptance condition
over the
underlying graph
of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}_{1}
that is equivalent to
(\gamma_{1},\Gamma_{1},\mathbb{W}_{1})
over this graph.

Conversely, if two
acceptance conditions

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{1}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{2}
are
equivalent over a same graph
G
, then the identity function is an
isomorphism
between
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}_{1}=(G,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{1})
and
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}_{2}=(G,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{2})
.

For
X
one of types of languages defined in Section
2.2
(Büchi, parity, Muller, etc…), we say that a
transition system

\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
is
X
type
if there exists an
isomorphic

transition system

\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\simeq}}\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
using an
X
acceptance condition
. We note that, by the previous remark, in that case an
X
acceptance condition
can be defined directly over the
underlying graph
of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
.

We remark that, given a
pointed graph

G
(whose states are
accessible
), the equivalence classes of
Muller

acceptance conditions
for the relation
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\simeq}_{G}}
are given exactly by the mappings
f\colon{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Cycles}(G)}\to\{\mathrm{Accept},\mathrm{Reject}\}
.

The ACD determines the type of transition systems.

Definition 6.7
.

Let
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
be a
Muller

transition system
with a set of states
V
. We say that its
alternating cycle decomposition

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}
is a:

•

Rabin ACD
if for every state
v\in V
, the tree
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{T}_{v}}
has
Rabin shape
.

•

Streett ACD
if for every state
v\in V
, the tree
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{T}_{v}}
has
Streett shape
.

•

Parity ACD
if for every state
v\in V
, the tree
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{T}_{v}}
has
parity shape
.

•

[0,d-1]
-parity ACD
(resp.
[1,d]
-parity ACD
) if it is a
parity ACD
,
trees
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}
have
height
at most
d
and
trees
of
height

d
are
positive
(resp.
negative
).

Remark 6.8
.

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}
is a
parity ACD
if and only if it is both a
Rabin
and a
Streett ACD
.

Proposition 6.9
.

Let
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}=({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}})
be a
Muller

transition system
whose states are
accessible
. The following conditions are equivalent:

1.

\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
is
Rabin type
.

2.

For every pair of
rejecting cycles

\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Cycles}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS})}
with some
state in common
,
\ell_{1}\cup\ell_{2}
is a
rejecting cycle
.

3.

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}
is a
Rabin ACD
.

Proof 6.10
.

(
1\Rightarrow 2
)

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{R}=(\gamma,\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Rabin}(R)}
be the
Rabin

acceptance condition

equivalent to

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}
, and let
R=(G_{1},R_{1}),\dots,(G_{r},R_{r})
be its
Rabin pairs
. Let
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
be two
cycles
with a state in common, and suppose that
\ell_{1}\cup\ell_{2}
is
accepting
; we show that either
\ell_{1}
or
\ell_{2}
is
accepting
. The
cycle

\ell_{1}\cup\ell_{2}
is
accepted by
some
Rabin pair

(G_{j},R_{j})
, so for all edges
e\in\ell_{1}\cup\ell_{2}
,
\gamma(e)\notin R_{j}
, and there is some
e_{0}\in\ell_{1}\cup\ell_{2}
such that
\gamma(e_{0})\in G_{j}
. If
e_{0}
belongs to
\ell_{1}
, then
\ell_{1}
is
accepted by
the
Rabin pair

(G_{j},R_{j})
, and if
e_{0}\in\ell_{2}
, then
\ell_{2}
is
accepted
by it.

(
2\Rightarrow 3
)

Let
v
be a vertex of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{T}_{v}}
the
local subtree at
v
. Suppose that there is a
round node

n\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{T}_{v}}
with two different children
n_{1}
and
n_{2}
. The
cycles

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{1})
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{2})
are
rejecting cycles
over
v
, but their union is an
accepting cycle
(by Remark
5.2
).

(
3\Rightarrow 1
)

We observe that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}
is a
Rabin ACD
if and only if
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{rbw}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{T}_{v}})}=1
for all vertices
v
of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
. In particular, the
ACD-HD-Rabin-transform
of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
does not add any state to
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
. It is immediate to check that the
morphism

\varphi\colon{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{Rabin}}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS})}\to\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
given by
\varphi_{V}(v,x)=v
,
\varphi_{E}(e,l)=e
defined in the proof of Proposition
5.27
is an
isomorphism
, and
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
uses a
Rabin

acceptance condition
.

Proposition 6.11
.

Let
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}=({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}})
be a
Muller

transition system
. The following conditions are equivalent:

1.

\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
is
Streett type
.

2.

For every pair of
accepting cycles

\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Cycles}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS})}
with some
state in common
,
\ell_{1}\cup\ell_{2}
is an
accepting cycle
.
13
13

13

This property was introduced by Le Saëc under the name
cyclically closed automata

[
82
]
. We point out that the “if” direction of the result stated in
[
82
, Theorem 5.2]
does not hold. That statement can be rephrased as: If a
DMA

\mathcal{A}
is cyclically closed, then the
parity index
of
\mathcal{A}
is
[0,1]
. We refer to Proposition
6.16
for a correct characterisation.

3.

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}
is a
Streett ACD
.

Proof 6.12
.

Implications (
1\Rightarrow 2
) and (
2\Rightarrow 3
) are analogous to those from Proposition
6.9
.

(
3\Rightarrow 1
)

We consider the
transition system

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\overline{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}
obtained by complementing the
acceptance set
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}
. By Remark
5.6
, the
ACD
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\overline{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}
is obtained from
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}
by turning
round nodes
into
square nodes
and vice-versa. Thus, the
ACD
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\overline{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}
is a
Rabin ACD
, and by applying the previous proposition we can define a
Rabin condition

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{R}=(\gamma,\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Rabin}_{\Gamma}(R)})
such that the
transition system

({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{R})
is
isomorphic
to
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\overline{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}
. Since
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Streett}_{\Gamma}(R)}
is the complement language of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Rabin}_{\Gamma}(R)}
, we obtain that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{S}=(\gamma,\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Streett}_{\Gamma}(R)})
is a
Streett

acceptance condition

equivalent to

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}
over
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}
.

Proposition 6.13
.

Let
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}=({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}})
be a
Muller

transition system
. The following conditions are equivalent:

1.

\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
is
parity type
.

2.

For every pair of
accepting
(resp.
rejecting
)
cycles

\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Cycles}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS})}
with some
state in common
,
\ell_{1}\cup\ell_{2}
is an
accepting
(resp.
rejecting
)
cycle
.

3.

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}
is a
parity ACD
.

Moreover, if some condition is satisfied,
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
is
[0,d-1]
(resp.
[1,d]
)-parity type
if and only if
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}
is a
[0,d-1]
(resp.
[1,d]
)-parity ACD
.

Proof 6.14
.

(
1\Rightarrow 2
)

Proven in Lemma
4.23
.

(
2\Rightarrow 3
)

Admits an analogous proof to the corresponding implication in Proposition
6.9
.

(
3\Rightarrow 1
)

By definition,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}
is a
parity ACD
if and only if
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Leaves}}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{T}_{v}})
is a singleton for each vertex
v
of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
. In particular, the
ACD-parity-transform
of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
does not add any state to
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
. It is immediate to check that the
morphism

\varphi\colon{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{parity}}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS})}\to\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
defined in the proof of Proposition
5.19
is an
isomorphism
. Therefore,
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{parity}}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS})}
are
isomorphic

transition systems
, and the latter uses a
parity

acceptance condition
that is a
[0,d-1]
(resp.
[1,d]
)-parity condition if
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}
is a
[0,d-1]
(resp.
[1,d]
)-parity ACD
. If
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}
is not a
[0,d-1]
(resp.
[1,d]
)-parity ACD
, then the number of colours cannot be reduced by the optimality of the number of colours of the
ACD-parity-transform
(Theorem
5.34
).

Corollary 6.15
.

A
Muller

transition system
is
parity type
if and only if it is both
Rabin
and
Streett type
.

The ACD and the parity index of
\omega
-regular languages.

Proposition 6.16
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
deterministic

Muller

automaton
whose states are
accessible
. Then, the
parity index
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
is:

•

[0,d-1]
(resp.
[1,d]
) if and only if:

–

trees
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{A}}}
have
height
at most
d
,

–

there is at least one
tree
of
height

d
, and

–

trees
of
height

d
are
positive
(resp.
negative
).

•

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{d}}
if and only if:

–

trees
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{A}}}
have
height
at most
d
,

–

there is at least one
positive

tree
of
height

d
, and

–

there is at least one
negative

tree
of
height

d
.

Proof 6.17
.

We prove the right-to-left implication for the case
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{d}}
.
Assume that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{A}}}
verifies the previous list of conditions (in particular, it is
equidistant
). Then, the
ACD-parity-transform

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{parity}}(\mathcal{A})}
is a
DPA

recognising

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
using colours in
[0,d]
. In order to obtain a
DPA
for
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
with colours in
[1,d+1]
we need to introduce a small modification to the function
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}p}_{\mathcal{ACD}}
. For
\ell_{i}
a maximal
cycle
of
\mathcal{A}
and
n\in N_{\ell_{i}}
we define:

•

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}p}_{\mathcal{ACD}}^{\prime}(n)=0pt(n)+2
, if
\ell_{i}
is
accepting
,

•

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}p}_{\mathcal{ACD}}^{\prime}(n)=0pt(n)+1
, if
\ell_{i}
is
rejecting
.

It is a routine check to see that the version of the
ACD-parity-transform
using
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}p}_{\mathcal{ACD}}^{\prime}
is indeed a correct
parity

automaton
using colours in
[1,d+1]
.

To prove that no
DPA

recognising

L
uses less than
d
colours, it suffices to use the Flower Lemma
2.16
and the fact that a branch of length
d
in a
tree
of the
ACD
induces a
d
-flower
in
\mathcal{A}
, which is
positive
if and only if the corresponding tree is
positive
(Lemma
5.43
).

This is indeed a complete characterisation, since for any
ACD
there is a minimal
d
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{A}}}
lies in one and only one of the classes specified in the statement of the proposition.

Proposition 6.18
.

Let
L\subseteq\Sigma^{\omega}
be an
\omega
-regular language
of
parity index at least

[0,d-1]
(resp.
[1,d]
). Any
history-deterministic

Muller

automaton

recognising

L
uses an
acceptance condition
with at least
d
different
output colours
.

Proof 6.19
.

We first prove the result for
deterministic
automata. Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
DMA

recognising

L
using the
acceptance condition

(\gamma,\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{\Gamma}(\mathcal{F})})
. By Proposition
6.16
, there is a
tree

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{AltTree}(\ell_{i})}
in the
ACD
of
\mathcal{A}
of
height
at least
d
.
We define
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\gamma}_{\mathcal{ACD}}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Nodes}(\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}})}\to\Gamma
to be the function that assigns to each node of the
ACD
the colours appearing in it, that is:
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\gamma}_{\mathcal{ACD}}(n)=\gamma({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n))
. We remark that if
n^{\prime}
is a
descendant
of
n
then
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\gamma}_{\mathcal{ACD}}(n^{\prime})\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\gamma}_{\mathcal{ACD}}(n)
, and that a node
n
is
round
if and only if
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\gamma}_{\mathcal{ACD}}(n)\in\mathcal{F}
. Therefore, by the alternation of
round
and
square
nodes, if
n^{\prime}
is a strict
descendent
of
n
,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\gamma}_{\mathcal{ACD}}(n^{\prime})\subsetneq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\gamma}_{\mathcal{ACD}}(n)
. We conclude that the root of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{AltTree}(\ell_{i})}
must contain at least
d
different colours.

In order to obtain the result for
history-deterministic
automata we use
finite-memory resolvers
as defined in Section
4.2
. If
\mathcal{A}
is a
history-deterministic

Muller

automaton
, it admits a
sound

resolver

implemented by
a finite
memory structure

(\mathcal{M},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\sigma})
(Lemma
4.29
). Then, the
composition

\mathcal{A}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\lhd_{\sigma}}}\mathcal{M}
is a
DMA
using the same number of colours, that has to be at least
d
.

The following result (which was already known, as it is a consequence of the construction by Carton and Maceiras
[
19
]
), is refined and proven in Appendix
A
(Corollary
A.25
).

Proposition 6.20
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
deterministic

parity

automaton
such that all its states are
accessible
and the
parity index
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
is
[0,d-1]
(resp.
[1,d]
).
Then,
\mathcal{A}
is
[0,d-1]
(resp.
[1,d]
)-
parity type
.

The previous result does not hold for
history-deterministic
automata, as we could artificially add transitions augmenting the complexity of the structure of the automaton (enlarging the
flowers
of the automaton) without modifying the language it
recognises
. Nevertheless, some analogous results applying to
HD automata
can be obtained. Boker, Kupferman and Skrzypczak proved that any
HD

parity

automaton

recognising
a language of
parity index

[0,1]
(resp.
[1,2]
) admits an
equivalent

HD

subautomaton
using a
Büchi
(resp.
coBüchi
)
condition

[
10
, Theorems 10 and 13]
. We do not know whether the result holds for languages of arbitrary
parity index
.

Typeness for deterministic automata.

Two
automata

\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
such that
\mathcal{A}_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\simeq}}\mathcal{A}_{2}
recognise the same language:
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{1})}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{2})}
. However, the converse only holds for
deterministic
automata.

Lemma 6.21
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
be two
deterministic automata
over the same
underlying graph
and with the same
labelling
by
input letters
. Then,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{1})}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{2})}
if and only if
\mathcal{A}_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\simeq}}\mathcal{A}_{2}
.

Proof 6.22
.

The implication from right to left is trivial. For the other implication, suppose that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{1})}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{2})}
, and let
\rho\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Run}(\mathcal{A}_{1})}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Run}(\mathcal{A}_{2})}
be an infinite
run
over the
underlying graph
of
\mathcal{A}_{1}
. Let
w\in\Sigma^{\omega}
be the word over the
input alphabet

\Sigma
labelling the run
\rho
. Since
\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
are
deterministic
,
\rho
is the only
run over

w
, and therefore:

\rho\text{ is {\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}accepting} for }\mathcal{A}_{1}\iff w\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{1})}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}_{2})}\iff\rho\text{ is {\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}accepting} for }\mathcal{A}_{2}.

Corollary 6.23

(First proven in
[
11
, Theorem 7]
)
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}_{\mathcal{A}}
be the
underlying graph
of a
deterministic automaton
. Then, there are
Rabin
and
Streett
conditions
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{R}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{S}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}_{\mathcal{A}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{R})}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}_{\mathcal{A}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{S})}
if and only if there is a
parity condition

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{p}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}_{\mathcal{A}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{p})}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}_{\mathcal{A}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{R})}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}_{\mathcal{A}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{S})}
.

We remark that the hypothesis of
determinism
in the previous corollary is necessary, as it has been shown that an analogous result does not hold for
non-deterministic

automata

[
11
]
.

Proposition 6.24

(First proven in
[
55
, Theorem 15]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
deterministic

Rabin
(resp. Streett)
automaton
, and assume that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
can be
recognised
by a
deterministic

Büchi
(resp.
coBüchi
)
automaton
; that is, the
parity index
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
is at most
[0,1]
(resp. at most
[1,2]
). Then,
\mathcal{A}
is
Büchi type
(resp.
coBüchi type
).

Proof 6.25
.

We do the proof for the case Rabin-Büchi. We can assume that all the states of
\mathcal{A}
are
accessible
, as we can define a trivial acceptance condition in the part of
\mathcal{A}
that is not accessible. Since
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
has
parity index
at most
[0,1]
, the trees of the
ACD
of
\mathcal{A}
have
height
at most
2
, and trees of
height

2
are
positive
(the
root
is a
round node
), by Proposition
6.16
. As
\mathcal{A}
is a
Rabin

automaton
, its
ACD
has
Rabin shape
(Proposition
6.9
), so
round nodes
have at most one
child
. We conclude that the trees of the
ACD
of
\mathcal{A}
have a single branch, so it is a
[0,1]
-parity ACD
, and by Proposition
6.13
,
\mathcal{A}
is
Büchi type
.

6.2
A normal form for parity transition systems

In this section, we propose a definition of a
normal form
of
parity

automata
. This is exactly the form of
automata
resulting by applying the procedure defined by Carton and Maceiras
[
19
]
, or, equivalently, of
automata
resulting from the
ACD-parity-transform
(Corollary
6.33
). These automata satisfy that they are
parity-index-tight
, that is, their
acceptance condition
uses the minimal possible number of
colours
. But they offer some further convenient properties, stated in Propositions
6.34
and
6.36
, which make them particularly well-suited for reasoning about
deterministic

parity

automata
.

This
normal form
, or partial versions of it, have already been used in the literature to prove results about
parity
automata in different contexts, such as
history-deterministic

coBüchi

automata

[
1
,
35
,
57
]
, positionality of languages defined by
deterministic

Büchi

automata

[
15
]
or learning of
DPAs

[
6
]
. The
normalisation
of
transition systems
also facilitates solving
parity

games
in practice
[
41
]
.
However, the application of this
normal form
in the literature is limited to specific cases, and no prior works have provided a formal and systematic study of it.

From our results we obtain three equivalent ways of defining the
normal form
of a
parity

transition system

\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
. Informally, they can be stated as:

1.

\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
use the smallest possible
colour
in each of its transitions (Definition
6.27
).

2.

\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{parity}}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS})}
(Corollary
6.33
).

3.

Paths in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
producing
colour

d>0
can be closed into a
cycle
producing
d^{\prime}
as minimal
colour
, for all
d^{\prime}\leq d
(Theorem
6.38
).

Remark 6.26
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}=(\gamma,[d_{\min},d_{\max}],{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{parity}})
is a
parity

acceptance condition
over a
pointed graph

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}
, we can always assume that
d_{\min}
is
0
or
1
. Indeed, define
\chi=d_{\min}
if
d_{\min}
is even, and
\chi=d_{\min}-1
if
d_{\min}
is odd. The
parity

acceptance condition

(\gamma^{\prime},[d_{\min}-\chi,d_{\max}-\chi],{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{parity}})
defined as
\gamma^{\prime}(e)=\gamma(e)-\chi
is
equivalent
to
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}
over
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}
.

Definition of the normal form.

Just as in the definition of the
ACD-parity-transform
we had to define
positive
and
negative

ACDs
to obtain an accurate optimality result in the number of
colours
, we need now to take care of a small technical detail so that
TS
in
normal form
are
parity-index-tight
.

We say that a
transition system

\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
is
negative
if
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}
is
negative
, that is, if for some
d

\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
contains a
negative

d
-flower
but contains no
positive

d
-flower
. Intuitively, a
parity

TS
is
negative
if and only if the minimal colour used by a
parity

acceptance condition
using an optimal number of
colours
is
1
.

Definition 6.27

(Normal form)
.

Let
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}=({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}})
be a
parity

transition system
using a
colouring function

\gamma
. If
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
is
not negative
, we say that
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
is in
normal form
if any other
parity

acceptance condition

equivalent to

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}
over
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}
using a
colouring function

\gamma^{\prime}
satisfies that for every edge
e
:

\gamma(e)\leq\gamma^{\prime}(e).

If
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
is
negative
, we say that it is in
normal form
if any other
equivalent

colouring

\gamma^{\prime}
not using colour
0
satisfies that for any edge
e
:

1\leq\gamma(e)\leq\gamma^{\prime}(e).

If
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
is in
normal form
, we will also say that its
acceptance condition
or the
colouring function
it uses are in normal form.

Example 6.28
.

Parity

transition systems
from Figures
3
,
10
,
17
and
18
are all in
normal form
. Parity automata appearing in Figures
10
and
17
are
negative
(the minimal colour used by an optimal
acceptance condition
is odd), whereas
parity

automata
in Figure
18
are not.

On the other hand, the
automaton
from Figure
1
is not in
normal form
(even if it uses an optimal number of colours). We can put it in
normal form
by assigning colour
1
to transitions
q_{1}\xrightarrow{a,b}q_{0}
and
q_{1}\xrightarrow{b,c}q_{2}
. The
automaton
obtained in this way
recognises
the same language.

Proposition 6.29
.

Let
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}=({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}})
be a
parity

transition system
with a
colouring function

\gamma
.
There is a unique
parity

acceptance condition

equivalent
to
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}
over
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}
in
normal form
.
Moreover, this
acceptance condition
is exactly the
parity condition
of the
ACD-parity-transform
of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
.

Before showing the proof of Proposition
6.29
, we prove a useful technical lemma.

Lemma 6.30
.

Let
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
be a
parity

transition system
with
colouring function

\gamma
. If
\ell_{1}\supsetneq\ell_{2}\supsetneq\dots\supsetneq\ell_{k}
is a
positive
(resp.
negative
)
k
-flower
of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
, then
\min\gamma(\ell_{k})\geq k-1
(resp.
\min\gamma(\ell_{k})\geq k
).

Proof 6.31
.

We show the result for
negative flowers
. Let
d_{i}=\min\gamma(\ell_{i})
. We show that
d_{i}\geq i
by induction. Since
\ell_{i}
is an
accepting cycle
if and only if
i
is even, we have that
d_{i}
is even if and only if
i
is even. Clearly,
d_{1}\geq 1
, as
1
is the least odd number. Also,
d_{i+1}\geq d_{i}
, since
\ell_{i+1}\subseteq\ell_{i}
, and the inequality is strict by the alternation of the parity, concluding the proof.

Proof 6.32

(Proof of Proposition
6.29
)
.

We first remark that the uniqueness is directly implied by the definition of
normal form
.

We prove that the
acceptance condition
of the
ACD-parity-transform
is in
normal form
. We note its
colouring function
by
\gamma_{\mathcal{ACD}}
.
The transitions not belonging to any
SCC
are coloured
0
if
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
is
not negative
and
1
if
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
is
negative
, as desired. It suffices to prove the result for edges in
SCCs
.

We assume that
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
is
not negative
and
we let
\mathcal{S}
be an
accepting SCC
of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
(the proof is similar for
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}

negative
and a
rejecting SCC
). Let
e=v\xrightarrow{}v^{\prime}
be an edge in
\mathcal{S}
, and let
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{T}_{v}}
be the
local subtree at
v
, which is composed of a single
branch
(see Proposition
6.13
). We let
n_{0}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\preceq}}n_{1}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\preceq}}\dots\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\preceq}}n_{r}
be that
branch
, where
n_{0}
is the
root
and
n_{r}
the
leaf
. Let
n_{k}
be the
deepest
node of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{T}_{v}}
such that
e\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{k})
. By definition of the
ACD-parity-transform
,
\gamma_{\mathcal{ACD}}(e)={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}p}_{\mathcal{ACD}}(e)=k
. Also,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{0})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\preceq}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{1})\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\preceq}}\dots\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\preceq}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{k})
is a
positive

k+1
-flower
(by Lemma
5.43
).
Lemma
6.30
implies then that any
equivalent

parity

condition
using a
colouring function

\gamma^{\prime}
verifies
\gamma^{\prime}(e)\geq\gamma_{\mathcal{ACD}}(e)=k
.

Corollary 6.33
.

The
ACD-parity-transform

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{parity}}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS})}
of any
Muller

transition system

\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
is in
normal form
.

Fundamental properties of the normal form.

We now state what we consider to be the two fundamental properties of
parity

transition systems
in
normal form
. Theorem
6.38
states that these properties characterise the
normal form
.

.

\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}

producing no colour strictly smaller than

.

Proof 6.35
.

By Proposition
6.29
, we know that the
colouring
of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
is the one given by its
ACD-transform
, that we note
\gamma_{\mathcal{ACD}}
.
If
v
and
v^{\prime}
are in different
SCCs
the result is trivial.
Let
v
and
v^{\prime}
be in the same
SCC
, that we suppose to be an
accepting SCC
without loss of generality. Let
\rho=v\xrightarrow{e_{1}}\dots\xrightarrow{e_{k}}v^{\prime}
be a path from
v
to
v^{\prime}
producing
\min\gamma_{\mathcal{ACD}}(\rho)=d
as minimal colour.
We remark that, as
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}
is a
parity ACD
, each edge
e
appears in one and only one branch of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}
, and that
\gamma_{\mathcal{ACD}}(e)
equals the
depth
of the
deepest
node containing
e
. In particular, if
e\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)
for some node
e
,
\gamma_{\mathcal{ACD}}(e)\geq 0pt(n)
. Our objective is to show that a similar result holds for the path
\rho
as a set of edges:

Claim 6
.

Let
N_{\rho}
be the set of nodes of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}
containing the edges of the path
\rho
in their label, that is,
N_{\rho}=\{n\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Nodes}(\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}})}\mid\{e_{1},\dots,e_{k}\}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)\}
.
Then,
\min(\gamma_{\mathcal{ACD}}(\rho))
equals the
depth
of a node of maximal
depth
of
N_{\rho}
.
14
14

14

In fact, the nodes of
N_{\rho}
are totally ordered by the
ancestor
relation, so there is a unique node of maximal
depth
in
N_{\rho}
. This fact is not used in our proof.

This claim allows us to conclude. Indeed, let
n
be a node of maximal
depth
of
N_{\rho}
, verifying
0pt(n)=d
. Then,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)
is a
cycle
containing the vertices
v
and
v^{\prime}
, and for all the edges
e\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)
,
\gamma_{\mathcal{ACD}}(e)\geq 0pt(n)=d
. This provides the desired path from
v^{\prime}
to
v
.

{subproof}

[Proof of Claim
6
]
First, we remark that if
\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\dots,\ell_{k}
are
cycles
such that
\ell_{i}
and
\ell_{i+1}
have some state in common, then
\cup_{i=1}^{k}\ell_{i}
is a
cycle
. Let
n
be a node of maximal
depth
in
N_{\rho}
. By the previous remarks,
\gamma_{\mathcal{ACD}}(e)\geq 0pt(n)
. Suppose by contradiction that
\gamma_{\mathcal{ACD}}(\rho)>0pt(n)
. Then, each edge
e_{i}
of
\rho
would appear in some strict descendant
n_{i}
of
n
(we can assume that
n_{i}
is a
child
of
n
). Then,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{1}),\dots,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{k})
would be
cycles
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{i})
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{i+1})
have some state in common (namely,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Target}}(e_{i})={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Source}}(e_{i+1})
), so their union is a
cycle
. However, this is not possible in a
parity

transition system
, as
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)
is
accepting
if and only if each of the
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{i})
is
rejecting
(see Lemma
4.23
).

This completes the proof of Proposition
6.34
.

Proposition 6.36

(Normal flowers do not lack petals)
.

Let
v
be a state of a
parity

transition system
in
normal form
belonging to an
accepting
(resp.
rejecting
)
SCC
. Let
\ell\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Cycles}_{v}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS})}
be a
cycle
over
v
and let
d_{\ell}
be the minimal colour appearing in it.

•

If
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
is
not negative
, for each
x\in[0,d_{\ell}]
(resp.
x\in[1,d_{\ell}]
) there is a cycle
\ell_{x}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Cycles}_{v}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS})}
producing
x
as minimal colour.

•

If
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
is
negative
, for each
x\in[2,d_{\ell}]
(resp.
x\in[1,d_{\ell}]
) there is a cycle
\ell_{x}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Cycles}_{v}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS})}
producing
x
as minimal colour.

Proof 6.37
.

We do the proof for the case in which
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
is
not negative
and
v
belongs to an
accepting SCC
.
By Proposition
6.29
, the
colouring
of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
is the one given by its
ACD-transform
, noted
\gamma_{\mathcal{ACD}}
. Consider the
local subtree at
v
,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{T}_{v}}
, consisting in a single
branch
, as it has
parity shape
(Proposition
6.13
). Let
n_{0}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\preceq}}\dots\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\preceq}}n_{k}
be that branch, and let
n_{i}
be the
deepest
node such that
\ell\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{i})
. We remark that, by definition of
\gamma_{\mathcal{ACD}}
,
d_{\ell}=0pt(n_{i})=i
. The desired
cycles
are obtained by taking
\ell_{x}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{x})
, for
x\in[0,d_{\ell}]
.

The next theorem states a simple characterisation of
transition systems
in
normal form
. It provides a useful tool to show
normality
of
parity

TS
in many proofs. In essence, it shows that the two previous propositions characterise the
normal form
. We state it for
non-negative

transition systems
for simplicity; a similar characterisation for
negative

transition systems
is immediate.

We say that an
SCC
of a parity
TS
is
positive
if the minimal
colour
appearing on it is even, and that it is
negative
if this minimal
colour
is odd.

Theorem 6.38
.

non-negative

parity

•
