Parity index from automata in normal form.

The next definition constitutes a syntactic version of the
parity index
, defined at the level of
parity

transition systems
.
The following results establish the tight relation between the semantic notion of
parity index
and its syntactic counterpart, and state that the
parity index
of a language can be directly read from a
DPA
in
normal form
.

Definition 6.40
.

We say that a
parity

transition system

\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}=({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}})
is
parity-index-tight
if any other
parity condition

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}^{\prime}
over
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\simeq}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}}{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}
uses at least as many
colours
as
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}
.

We have shown in Corollary
6.33
that the
ACD-parity-transform
is always in
normal form
. Therefore, the optimality properties of the
colouring
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{parity}}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS})}
(Theorem
5.34
) transfer to
parity

transition systems
in
normal form
.

Corollary 6.41
.

A
parity

transition system
in
normal form
is
parity-index-tight
.

Moreover, the
parity index
of an
\omega
-regular language
can be read from any
DPA
in
normal form

recognising
it.

Corollary 6.42
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
deterministic

parity

automaton
in
normal form
such that all its states are
accessible
.
If
\mathcal{A}
uses
colours
in
[0,d-1]
(resp.
[1,d]
), then the
parity index
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
is
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{d-1}}
or
[0,d-1]
(resp.
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{d-1}}
or
[1,d]
).

We prove this result in Appendix
A
(Corollary
A.23
), and we provide there a refined characterisation by using
generalised weak automata
.

6.3
Minimisation of deterministic parity automata recognising Muller languages

The minimisation of
\omega
-automata is a fundamental problem of an intriguing complexity. In 2010, Schewe showed that the minimisation of
deterministic

Büchi
and
parity

automata
is
\mathsf{NP}\mathchar 45\relax\mathrm{complete}
, if the
acceptance condition
is defined over the states
[
86
]
. However, the reduction of
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness does not generalise to automata with edge-based acceptance. A surprising positive result was obtained in 2019 by Abu Radi and Kupferman: we can minimise in polynomial time
HD

coBüchi
automata using transition-based acceptance
[
1
]
. Schewe showed that the minimisation was again
\mathsf{NP}
-hard for
HD
automata with state-based acceptance
[
87
]
. To the best of our knowledge, the only existing hardness result applying to transition-based automata is Casares’ result about the
\mathsf{NP}
-completeness of the minimisation of
deterministic

Rabin

automata

[
21
]
. In fact, in
[
21
]
a stronger result is proven: it is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard to minimise
deterministic

Rabin

automata

recognising

Muller languages
.

In this section, we provide a polynomial-time algorithm for the minimisation of
DPA

recognising

Muller languages
(with
acceptance condition
over transitions). By Proposition
4.10
and Theorem
4.14
, we know that a minimal
(history-)deterministic

parity

automaton

recognising
a
Muller language

L={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{F})}
can be constructed in linear time from the
Zielonka tree

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
. We will therefore provide a polynomial-time algorithm computing this
Zielonka tree
from a
DPA

recognising

L
.

Theorem 6.43
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
DPA

recognising
a
Muller language

L={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{F})}
. We can find a minimal
deterministic
(resp.
history-deterministic
)
parity

automaton
recognising
L
in polynomial time in the size of the representation of
\mathcal{A}
.
15
15

15

We can assume that the representation of
\mathcal{A}
has size polynomial in
|Q|+|\Sigma|
, where
Q
and
\Sigma
are the set of states and the
input alphabet
of
\mathcal{A}
. Indeed, as
\mathcal{A}
is
deterministic
the number of transitions is at most
|Q|\cdot|\Sigma|
, and we can assume that
\mathcal{A}
has no more
output colours
than transitions.

Description of the algorithm.

Let
\mathcal{A}=(Q,\Sigma,q_{0},\Gamma,\Delta,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{parity}})
be a
DPA

recognising

L={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{F})}
. We outline a recursive algorithm building
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}=(N,\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\preceq}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{})
in a top-down fashion; it starts from the
root
of the
tree
(which is always labelled
\Sigma
), and each time that some node is added to
N
, we compute its
children
.
If we have built
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
up to a node
n
, we compute the
children
of
n
by using the procedure
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathtt{AlternatingSets}}
described in Algorithm
1
, which we disclose next.

We assume without loss of generality that
n
is
round
, that is,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)\in\mathcal{F}
. First, we take the restriction of
\mathcal{A}
to transitions labelled with letters in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)
and pick a
final SCC
on it. Such
final SCC
induces a
subautomaton

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{A}

recognising

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathcal{F}\mathchoice{\vphantom{\big|}}{}{}{}\right|_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)}}})}
(see also Lemma
4.21
).
Our objective is to find the maximal
subautomata
of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
using as
input letters
sets
X\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)
such that
X\notin\mathcal{F}
. We will keep all such subsets
X
in a list
\mathsf{altSets}
. The labels of the
children
of
n
will then correspond to the maximal sets appearing in this list, which are returned by the algorithm
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathtt{AlternatingSets}}
(Line
1
).
In order to find them, we remove the transitions using the minimal colour in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
(that is even, since
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)\in\mathcal{F}
) and compute a decomposition in
strongly connected components
of the obtained graph.
Let
\mathcal{S}
be a component of this decomposition and let
\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)
be the
input letters
appearing in it. Then,
\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}\notin\mathcal{F}
if and only if the minimal
output colour
in
\mathcal{S}
is odd (see Lemma
6.44
below). In this case, we add
\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}
to
\mathsf{altSets}
. On the contrary, we remove the minimal (even) colour from
\mathcal{S}
, and we start again finding a decomposition in
SCCs
of the obtained graph.

We include the pseudocode for the procedure
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathtt{AlternatingSets}}
in Algorithm
1
. We use the following notations:

•

\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\mathtt{Letters}}(\mathcal{S})
is the set of
input letters
appearing in
\mathcal{S}
,

•

\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\mathtt{MinColour}}(\mathcal{S})
is the minimal
output colour
appearing in
\mathcal{S}
(which determines whether
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathtt{Letters}}(\mathcal{S})\in\mathcal{F}
, if
\mathcal{S}
is
strongly connected
),

•

\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\mathtt{SCC\mathchar 45\relax Decomposition}}(\mathcal{A})
outputs a list of the
strongly connected components
of
\mathcal{A}
. If
\mathcal{A}
is empty, it outputs an empty list.

•

\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\mathtt{MaxInclusion}}(\mathsf{lst})
returns the list of the maximal subsets in
\mathsf{lst}
.

Input:

A
strongly connected

automaton

\mathcal{A}
over
\Sigma
;
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}(\mathcal{F})}

Output:

Maximal subsets
\Sigma_{1},\dots,\Sigma_{k}\subseteq\Sigma
such that
\Sigma_{i}\in\mathcal{F}\iff\Sigma\notin\mathcal{F}

d\leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathtt{MinColour}}(\mathcal{A})
;

1

\mathcal{A}_{>d}\leftarrow
restriction of
\mathcal{A}
to transitions
\Delta_{>d}=\{q\xrightarrow{a:x}q^{\prime}\in\Delta\mid x>d\}
;

2

\langle\mathcal{S}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{S}_{r}\rangle\leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathtt{SCC\mathchar 45\relax Decomposition}}(\mathcal{A}_{>d})
;

3

\mathsf{altSets}\leftarrow\{\}
;

4

for

i=1,\dots,r

do

5

if

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathtt{MinColour}}(\mathcal{S}_{i})
is odd if and only if
d
is even

then

6

\mathsf{altSets}\leftarrow\mathsf{altSets}\,\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathtt{Letters}}(\mathcal{S}_{i})\}
;

7

else

8

\mathsf{altSets}\leftarrow\mathsf{altSets}\,\cup\,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathtt{AlternatingSets}}(\mathcal{S}_{i})
;

9

\mathsf{maxAltSets}\leftarrow{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathtt{MaxInclusion}}(\mathsf{altSets})
;

10

return

\mathsf{maxAltSets}
;

11

Algorithm 1

\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\mathtt{AlternatingSets}}(\mathcal{A})
: Computing the children of a node.

Correctness of the algorithm.

Let
n
be a node of the
Zielonka tree
of
\mathcal{F}
labelled with
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)
, and let
\mathcal{A}_{n}
be an
accessible

subautomaton
of
\mathcal{A}
over
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)

recognising

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathcal{F}\mathchoice{\vphantom{\big|}}{}{}{}\right|_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)}}})}
.
We prove that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathtt{AlternatingSets}}(\mathcal{A}_{n})
returns a list of sets corresponding to the labels of the
children
of
n
in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
. We assume without loss of generality that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)\in\mathcal{F}
and therefore the minimal colour
d
in
\mathcal{A}_{n}
is even.

First, we observe that if
X\subseteq\Sigma
is added to
\mathsf{altSets}
during the execution of the procedure
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathtt{AlternatingSets}}
, then
X
is the set of input letters appearing in a
cycle
whose minimal colour is odd.
Next lemma implies that in this case,
X\notin\mathcal{F}
.
In particular, no subset is added if
n
is a
leaf
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
.

Lemma 6.44
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
DPA
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{F})}
.
Let
\ell\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Cycles}(\mathcal{A})}
be an
accessible

cycle
of
\mathcal{A}
. Let
\Sigma_{\ell}\subseteq\Sigma
be the
input letters
appearing in
\ell
, and let
d_{\ell}
be the minimal colour on
\ell
. Then,
\Sigma_{\ell}\in\mathcal{F}
if and only if
d_{\ell}
is even.

Proof 6.45
.

Since
\ell
is an
accessible

cycle
, there is a word
w\in\Sigma^{\omega}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Inf}}(w)=\Sigma_{\ell}
and verifying that the edges visited infinitely infinitely often by the (only)
run over

w
in
\mathcal{A}
are the edges of
\ell
. Therefore
w\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
if and only if
d_{\ell}
is even, and since
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
is a
Muller language
,
w\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
if and only if
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Inf}}(w)=\Sigma_{\ell}\in\mathcal{F}
.

As the final output of the algorithm consists solely on the maximal subsets in
\mathsf{altSets}
, and no accepting set is added to this list, it suffices to show that each maximal rejecting subset
\Sigma_{\max}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)
is added to
\mathsf{altSets}
at some point.

Let
\Sigma_{\max}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)
be one of the maximal rejecting subsets of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)
. Let
\mathcal{S}
be a
final SCC
of the restriction of
\mathcal{A}_{n}
to transitions labelled with letters in
\Sigma_{\max}
(by the previous lemma,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathtt{MinColour}}(\mathcal{S})
is odd). We show that
\Sigma_{\max}
will eventually be considered by the recursive procedure
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathtt{AlternatingSets}}
, and therefore
\Sigma_{\max}
will be added to
\mathsf{altSets}

We use of the following remark:

Claim 7
.

If
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is a
strongly connected

subautomaton
of
\mathcal{A}_{n}
such that
\mathcal{S}\subsetneq\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{n}
, then the minimal colour in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is even.

{subproof}

Let
\Sigma^{\prime}
be the
input letters
appearing in
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.
As
\mathcal{S}\subsetneq\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
and no transition labelled with a letter in
\Sigma_{\max}
leaves
\mathcal{S}
, we must have
\Sigma_{\max}\subsetneq\Sigma^{\prime}
. The claim follows from Lemma
6.44
.

Therefore, either
\mathcal{S}
is one of the
SCCs
of
\mathcal{A}_{>d}
(in this case,
\Sigma_{\max}
is added to
\mathsf{altSets}
in Line
1
), or it is contained in one
SCC
of
\mathcal{A}_{>d}
whose minimal colour is even, and we can conclude by induction.

Complexity analysis.

We will show that the proposed algorithm works in time
\mathcal{O}(|Q|^{3}|\Sigma|^{2}|\Gamma|)
, where
Q
,
\Sigma
and
\Gamma
are the states, set of
input letters
and set of
output colours
of the automaton, respectively.
We remark that, since
\mathcal{A}
is
deterministic
,
|\Delta|\leq|Q||\Sigma|
.

First, we study the complexity of the procedure
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathtt{AlternatingSets}}(\mathcal{A})
. At each recursive call, at least one edge is removed from
\Delta
, and a decomposition in
strongly connected components
of the
automaton
is performed, which can be done in
\mathcal{O}(|Q||\Sigma|)

[
91
]
. Therefore, the
children
of a node of the
Zielonka tree
can be computed in
\mathcal{O}(|Q|^{2}|\Sigma|^{2})
.

We perform this operation for each node of the
Zielonka tree
. By the optimality of the
ZT-parity-automaton
(Theorems
4.13
and
4.14
), we know that
|Q|\geq|{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Leaves}}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}})|
and that the
height
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
is at most
|\Gamma|
. Therefore,
|{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}|\leq|Q||\Gamma|
, and the procedure
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathtt{AlternatingSets}}
is called at most
|Q||\Gamma|
times. We conclude that the proposed algorithm works in time
\mathcal{O}(|Q|^{3}|\Sigma|^{2}|\Gamma|)
.

Remark 6.46

(State-based automata)
.

The acceptance condition of the parity automaton obtained from the
Zielonka tree
appears naturally over the transitions of the automaton. In order to make it a state-based automaton, we would need to add one state per
colour
it uses. It turns out that, in this specific case, this is optimal, and the state-based parity automaton we obtain is minimal. Therefore, we can also minimise in polynomial time state-based parity automata
recognising

Muller languages
.
However, it is no longer possible to obtain optimal transformations towards state-based parity automata based on the
ACD
(see
[
25
, Section 5.3]
and
[
22
, Section I.8]
for further details).

7
Conclusion

In this work, we have carried out an extensive study of transformations of
automata
and
games
that use
Muller

acceptance conditions
. We have proposed different types of
morphisms
to formalise the idea of valid transformations of
transition systems
, which distil the central features of existing transformations. Our main contribution resides in the introduction of a new structure, the
alternating cycle decomposition
, which is a succinct representation of the
alternating chains of loops
of a
Muller

automaton
– in the sense of Wagner
[
93
]
– and provides the necessary information to understand the interplay between its
acceptance condition
and its
underlying graph
.

Optimal and practical transformations of automata.

We have presented a transformation that, given a
deterministic

Muller

automaton
, provides an equivalent
deterministic

parity

automaton
, and another that provides an equivalent
history-deterministic

Rabin

automaton
. These transformations are optimal in a strong sense; the obtained automata have a minimal number of states amongst those which accept a
history-deterministic mapping
to the original
Muller

automaton
.
The first of these transformations has been implemented in the open-source tools Spot 2.10
[
2
]
and Owl 21.0
[
52
]
, and it has been shown to perform extremely well in practice
[
25
]
, as the natural definition of the
ACD
provides a fairly efficient way to compute the transformation, while its optimality guarantees to produce
automata
as small as possible.

Understanding the limitations of history-deterministic automata.

As a corollary of our results, we have obtained that minimal
deterministic
and
history-deterministic

parity

automata

recognising

Muller languages
have the same size (Corollary
4.15
). Moreover, we have shown that
HD

parity

automata
that are strictly smaller than equivalent
deterministic
ones cannot come from a
deterministic

Muller

automaton
(Corollary
5.39
). This provides a partial explanation on the difficulty to find succinct
HD

parity

automata
, as we could argue that a simple way to conceptualise
\omega
-regular languages
is through
deterministic

Muller

automata
.
Maybe most importantly, this sets a limitation in the usefulness of
history-determinism
in practice, as procedures that use a
DMA
as an intermediate step – as the ones from the tools Strix
[
65
]
and
ltlsynt

[
70
]
, or automata determinisation
[
78
,
88
,
64
]
– cannot benefit from the succinctness of
HD automata
.

On the other hand, we have shown that, if our objective is to obtain
Rabin

automata
as output, the
ACD-HD-Rabin-transform
allows us to benefit from succinct
HD automata
. In this case, it has been shown that these automata can be exponentially smaller than equivalent
deterministic
ones
[
24
, Theorem 21]
.

Disclosing the structure of
\omega
-automata.

As an application of the insights gained from the
alternating cycle decomposition
, we have derived results concerning
typeness
of
automata
. In particular, we have characterised when we can define a
parity
,
Rabin
or
Streett

condition
on top of a
Muller

automaton
, obtaining an
equivalent

automaton
(Propositions
6.9
,
6.11
and
6.13
). These characterisations have already been proven instrumental in works about the
memory
for
games

[
21
]
, and to obtain lower bounds on the size of
deterministic

Rabin
automata
[
24
]
.

We have also employed the
ACD
to present a
normal form
for
parity

transition systems
and systematically proved the most important properties that make this form a valuable tool for manipulating
parity

automata
. We believe that this
normal form
will be useful to extend existing results about
Büchi
and
coBüchi
automata (as the ones in
[
1
,
10
,
15
]
) to
parity
automata.

Appendix A
Generalised classes of acceptance conditions

Further acceptance conditions.

Generalised Büchi.

Given
k
non-empty subsets
B_{1},\dots,B_{k}\subseteq\Gamma
, we define the
generalised Büchi language associated to

B=\{B_{1},\dots,B_{k}\}
as

\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\textsf{genB\"{u}chi}_{\Gamma}(B)}=\{w\in\Gamma^{\omega}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Inf}}(w)\cap B_{i}\neq\emptyset\text{ for all }i\in\{1,\dots,k\}\}.

We say that a language
L\subseteq\Gamma^{\omega}
is a
generalised Büchi language
if there is a family of sets
B=\{B_{1},\dots,B_{k}\}
such that
L={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{genB\"{u}chi}_{\Gamma}(B)}
.

Generalised coBüchi.

Given
k
non-empty subsets
B_{1},\dots,B_{k}\subseteq\Gamma
, we define the
generalised coBüchi language associated to

B=\{B_{1},\dots,B_{k}\}
as

\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\textsf{genCoB\"{u}chi}_{\Gamma}(B)}=\{w\in\Gamma^{\omega}\mid{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Inf}}(w)\cap B_{i}=\emptyset\text{ for some }i\in\{1,\dots,k\}\}.

We say that a language
L\subseteq\Gamma^{\omega}
is a
generalised coBüchi language
if there is a family of sets
B=\{B_{1},\dots,B_{k}\}
such that
L={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{genCoB\"{u}chi}_{\Gamma}(B)}
.

Remark A.1
.

Deterministic

generalised Büchi
(resp.
generalised coBüchi
)
automata
have the same expressive power than
deterministic

Büchi
(resp.
coBüchi
)
automata
: they
recognise
languages of
parity index
at most
[0,1]
(resp.
[1,2]
).

We will also define
conditions that depend on the structure
of the
transition system
and not only on the set of colours.

Generalised weak transition systems.

Let
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}=({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}})
be a
transition system
using a
parity condition

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}=(\gamma,[d_{\min},d_{\max}],{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{parity}})
. We say that
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
is
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\textsf{Weak}_{d}}
if in each
strongly connected component

\mathcal{S}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}
there are at most
d
different colours that appear, that is,
|\gamma(E_{\mathcal{S}})|\leq d
, where
E_{\mathcal{S}}
is the set of edges of
\mathcal{S}
.

As for the rest of conditions, we say that a
transition system

\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
is
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{d}}
type
if there exists an
isomorphic

parity

transition system

\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\simeq}}\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
that is
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{d}}
.

The adjective
Weak
has typically been used to refer to the condition corresponding to a partition of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
into accepting and rejecting
SCC
. A
run
will be
accepting
if the component it finally stays in is accepting. It corresponds to
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{1}}
with our notation.

As we will show (Corollary
A.17
), the notation is justified by the fact that an
\omega
-regular language
of
parity index

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{d}}
can be
recognised
by a
deterministic

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{d}}
automaton.

The Zielonka tree of generalised acceptance conditions.

Definition A.2
.

Let
T
be a
tree

T
with nodes partitioned into
round
nodes and
square
nodes. We say that
T
has:

•

Büchi shape
if it has a single branch,
height
at most
2
, and if it has
height

2
its
root
is
round
.

•

coBüchi shape
if it has a single branch,
height
at most
2
, and if it has
height

2
its
root
is
square
.

•

Generalised Büchi shape
if it has
height
at most
2
, and if it has
height

2
its
root
is
round
.

•

Generalised coBüchi shape
if it has
height
at most
2
, and if it has
height

2
its
root
is
square
.

Proposition A.3
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}2^{\Gamma}_{+}}
be a family of non-empty subsets. Then
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{\Gamma}(\mathcal{F})}
is a
Büchi

(resp. coBüchi) language
if and only if
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
has
Büchi

(resp. coBüchi) shape
.

Proof A.4
.

This is just a special case of Proposition
6.4
.

Proposition A.5
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}2^{\Gamma}_{+}}
be a family of non-empty subsets. Then
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{\Gamma}(\mathcal{F})}
is a
generalised Büchi

(resp. coBüchi) language
if and only if
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
has
generalised Büchi

(resp. generalised coBüchi) shape
.

Proof A.6
.

We do the proof for the case
generalised Büchi
(symmetric for generalised coBüchi).
Assume that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{\Gamma}(\mathcal{F})}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{genB\"{u}chi}_{\Gamma}(B)}
for some family
B=\{B_{1},\dots,B_{k}\}
. Then,
\Gamma\in\mathcal{F}
, as
\Gamma\cap B_{i}\neq\emptyset
, so the
root
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
is
round
. If
C\subseteq\Gamma
is rejecting,
C\cap B_{i}=\emptyset
for all
i
, then it is the same for any subset
C^{\prime}\subseteq C
, so
square
nodes of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
are
leaves
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
has
height
at most
2
.

Conversely, assume that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
has
height

2
and that its
root
is
round
(
\Gamma\in\mathcal{F}
). Let
A_{1},\dots,A_{k}
be the labels of the
k

leaves
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
and define
B_{i}=A_{i}
. We claim that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{\Gamma}(\mathcal{F})}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{genB\"{u}chi}_{\Gamma}(B)}
, for
B=\{B_{1},\dots,B_{k}\}
. Indeed, if
C\in\mathcal{F}
if and only if
C\nsubseteq A_{i}
for any
i
if and only if
C\cap B_{i}\neq\emptyset
for all
i
.

Corollary A.7
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
deterministic

generalised Büchi
(resp.
generalised coBüchi
)
automaton

recognising
a
Muller language

L={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}_{\Sigma}(\mathcal{F})}
. There is a
deterministicgeneralised Büchi
(resp.
generalised coBüchi
)
automaton
recognising
L
with just one state, that can be computed in polynomial time in the size of the representation of
\mathcal{A}
.

Proof A.8
.

We do the proof for the case
generalised Büchi
.
By Remark
A.1
, the
parity index
of
L
is at most
[0,1]
, so by Proposition
6.16
, the
Zielonka tree
of
\mathcal{F}
has
generalised Büchi shape
. Therefore, by Proposition
A.5
,
L
is a
generalised Büchi
that can be trivially
recognised
by a
generalised Büchi

automaton
with just one state.

The acceptance condition of such automaton can be deduced in linear time from the
Zielonka tree

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
, as indicated in the proof of Proposition
A.5
. The
Zielonka tree

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
can be computed from the original automaton
\mathcal{A}
using a similar argument than in the proof of Theorem
6.43
. Suppose that the
generalised Büchi
condition used by
\mathcal{A}
is given by the sets
B_{1},\dots,B_{k}\subseteq\Gamma
. Then, for each
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
we compute the restriction of
\mathcal{A}
to the transitions using colours in
\Gamma\setminus B_{i}
, and perform a decomposition in
SCCs
of the obtained
graph
. If
\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}\subseteq\Sigma
is the set of
input letters
appearing in one of those
SCC
, then
\Sigma_{\mathcal{S}}\notin\mathcal{F}
. We put all the subsets of letters obtained in that way in a list
\mathit{altSets}
. The
leaves
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
correspond then to the maximal subsets of
\mathit{altSets}
.

ACD and typeness for generalised acceptance conditions.

Definition A.9
.

Let
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
be a
Muller

transition system
with a set of states
V
. We say that its
alternating cycle decomposition

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}
is a:

•

Büchi ACD
if it is a
[0,1]
-parity ACD
.

•

coBüchi ACD
if it is a
[1,2]
-parity ACD
.

•

Generalised Büchi ACD
if for every state
v\in V
, the tree
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{T}_{v}}
has
generalised Büchi shape
.

•

Generalised coBüchi ACD
if for every state
v\in V
, the tree
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{T}_{v}}
has
generalised coBüchi shape
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{d}}
ACD
if it is a
parity ACD
and
trees
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}
have
height
at most
d
.

Remark A.10
.

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}
is a
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{d}}
ACD
if and only if it is a
[0,d]
-parity ACD
and a
[1,d+1]
-parity ACD
.

Proposition A.11
.

A
transition system

\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
is
Büchi

(resp. coBüchi) type
if and only if
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}
is a
Büchi ACD
(resp.
coBüchi ACD
).

Proof A.12
.

This is a special case of Proposition
6.13
.

Proposition A.13
.

A
transition system

\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
is
generalised Büchi

(resp. generalised coBüchi) type
if and only if
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}
is a
generalised Büchi ACD
(resp.
generalised coBüchi ACD
).

Proof A.14
.

The result follows by applying the same argument and construction than in Proposition
A.5
, using as set of
output colours
the set of edges of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
.

Proposition A.15
.

A
transition system

\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
is
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{d}}
type
if and only if
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}
is a
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{d}}
ACD
.

Proof A.16
.

Proposition
6.13
already provides that
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
is
parity type
if and only if
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}
is a
parity ACD
. As in the proof of the aforementioned proposition, we observe that
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{parity}}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS})}
are
isomorphic
. If
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}
is a
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{d}}
ACD
, then
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{parity}}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS})}
is
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{d}}
. Conversely, if
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}}}
is not a
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{d}}
ACD
, then
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
contains a
(d+1)
-flower
, so the number of colours cannot be reduced (using the same argument as in the proof of Theorem
5.34
).

Corollary A.17
.

If
L\subseteq\Sigma^{\omega}
is an
\omega
-regular language
of
parity index

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{d}}
, then
L
can be
recognised
by a
deterministic

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{d}}
automaton.

Proof A.18
.

Let
L
be of
parity index

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{d}}
. By definition,
L
is
recognised
by
parity

automaton

\mathcal{A}
using colours in
[0,d]
(it is also recognised by an automaton using colours in
[1,d+1]
; we make an arbitrary choice). We will prove that
\mathcal{A}
is in fact
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{d}}
. We will show that its
ACD

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{A}}}
is
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{d}}
type
, which allows to conclude by Proposition
A.15
.
Suppose that this was not the case, that is, that some
tree
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{A}}}
has
height
at least
d+1
. In this case,
\mathcal{A}
would contain a
(d+1)
-flower
(Lemma
5.43
), so by the Flower Lemma
2.16
,
L
has
parity index at least

[0,d]
or
[1,d+1]
, a contradiction.

The following result generalises
[
7
, Theorem 2]
.

Corollary A.19
.

A
Muller

transition system
is
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{d}}
type
if and only if it is both
[0,d]
and
[1,d+1]
-parity type
.

Deterministic automata using generalised acceptance conditions.

Corollary A.20
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}_{\mathcal{A}}
be the
underlying graph
of a
deterministic automaton
. There are
[0,d-1]
and
[1,d]
-parity
conditions
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{p,0}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{p,1}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}_{\mathcal{A}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{p,0})}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}_{\mathcal{A}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{p,1})}
if and only if there is a
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{p}}
condition
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{W}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}_{\mathcal{A}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{W})}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}_{\mathcal{A}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{p,0})}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}G}_{\mathcal{A}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathrm{Acc}}_{p,1})}
.

Proposition A.21
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
deterministic

Muller

automaton
, and assume that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
can be
recognised
by a
deterministic

Büchi
(resp.
coBüchi
)
automaton
; that is, the
parity index
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
is at most
[0,1]
(resp. at most
[1,2]
). Then,
\mathcal{A}
is
generalised Büchi type
(resp.
generalised coBüchi type
).

Proof A.22
.

We prove the result for the case generalised Büchi (analogous for coBüchi). We can assume that all the states of
\mathcal{A}
are
accessible
, as we can define a trivial acceptance condition in the part of
\mathcal{A}
that is not accessible. Since
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
has
parity index
at most
[0,1]
, the trees of the
ACD
of
\mathcal{A}
have
height
at most
2
, and trees of
height

2
are
positive
(the
root
is a
round node
), by Proposition
6.16
, so it is a
generalised Büchi ACD
, and by Proposition
A.13
,
\mathcal{A}
is
generalised Büchi type
.

Parity index from automata in normal form.

Corollary A.23
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
deterministic

parity

automaton
in
normal form
using
colours
in
[0,d-1]
(resp.
[1,d]
) such that all its states are
accessible
. If
\mathcal{A}
is
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{d-1}}
, then the
parity index
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
is
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{d-1}}
. If not, the
parity index
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
is
[0,d-1]
(resp.
[1,d]
).

Proof A.24
.

We assume that
\mathcal{A}
uses colours in
[0,d-1]
(in particular, it is
not negative
).
If
\mathcal{A}
is not
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{d-1}}
, there is an
SCC
containing all the colours
[0,d-1]
. By Proposition
6.36
, such
SCC
contains a
positive

d
-flower
, so by the Flower Lemma
2.16
, the
parity index
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
is
[0,d-1]
.

Suppose now that
\mathcal{A}
is
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{d-1}}
. Let
\ell
be a
cycle
of
\mathcal{A}
in which the colour
d-1
occurs. By Proposition
6.36
,
\ell
contains a
negative

(d-1)
-flower
. As
\mathcal{A}
is
not negative
, it also contains a
positive

(d-1)
-flower
. By the Flower Lemma
2.16
, the
parity index
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
is
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{d-1}}
.

Corollary A.25
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
deterministic

parity

automaton
such that all its states are
accessible
and the
parity index
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
is
[0,d-1]
(resp.
[1,d]
/
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{d}}
).
Then,
\mathcal{A}
is
[0,d-1]
(resp.
[1,d]
/
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Weak}_{d}}
)-
parity type
.

Proof A.26
.

We assume that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
has
parity index

[0,d-1]
(the other cases are similar). By the Flower Lemma
2.16
,
\mathcal{A}
does not contain any
negative

d
-flower
.
By Proposition
6.16
, the trees of the
ACD
of
\mathcal{A}
have
height
at most
d
, and trees of
height

d
are
positive
. That is,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{A}}}
is a
[0,d-1]
-parity ACD
, and we conclude by applying Proposition
6.13
.

Appendix B
Transformations for games

Games suitable for transformations.

As we have indicated throughout the paper, defining transformations not preserving determinism in the case of
games
poses certain formal challenges. This difficulties appear both when such transformations arise as the
product

\mathcal{G}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\ltimes}}\mathcal{A}
of a
game

\mathcal{G}
by an
non-deterministic

automaton

\mathcal{A}
, or when they are witnessed by an
HD mapping

\varphi\colon\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
. The problem comes from the fact that the semantics of
non-determinism
in
automata
(or
history-determinism
of
morphisms
) are inherently asymmetric, and this asymmetry needs to be made compatible with the semantics of
games
.
The choices we have made to overcome this technical difficulty are:

•

Restrict transformations of
games
to
games
in a standard form, which we have called
games
suitable for transformations
.

•

Add a restriction to
HD mappings
in the case of
games
, introducing the notion of
HD-for-games
mapping.

The main motivation for the standard form of
games
that we propose comes from viewing games as originating from logical formulas. Indeed, an equivalent model for
games
can be given as follows: vertices in the game graph are not partitioned into
Eve’s
and
Adam’s
nodes, instead, we assign a boolean formula to each transition that determines an interaction between the two players. The outcome of this interaction is (1) the next vertex, and (2) the
output colour
of the
acceptance condition
. We can obtain a
game
of the kind we have defined in this paper by unfolding the boolean formulas of the transitions. There is a natural way to standardize such games: putting the boolean formulas in disjunctive normal form (DNF). Then, the unfolding of a game with formulas in DNF yields a
game
in which the partition into
Eve
-
Adam
nodes induces a bipartite graph with a particular structure: first,
Adam
chooses an
uncoloured
transition leading to a vertex controlled by
Eve
(with only one ingoing transition), and then
Eve
picks a transition producing some
output colour
.

We recall that a
game
is
suitable for transformations
if it verifies that for every edge
e=v\xrightarrow{}v^{\prime}
, if
v
is controlled by
Adam
, then
e
is
uncoloured
(
\gamma(e)=\varepsilon
),
v^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V_{\mathrm{Eve}}}
, and
e
is the only incoming edge to
v^{\prime}
(
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{In}}(v^{\prime})=\{e\}
).

Games in this form have an asymmetric structure that makes them suitable for any type of transformation. As any pair of consecutive transitions are of the form
v\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}\tilde{v}\xrightarrow{c}v^{\prime}
, with
\tilde{v}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V_{\mathrm{Eve}}}
, we can force it so that if a decision needs to be made in a product, Eve is the one who makes it.

Lemma B.1
.

For every game
\mathcal{G}
with vertices
V
and edges
E
, there exists a
game

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}
that is
suitable for transformations
, of size
|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}|=\mathcal{O}(|E|)
, and equivalent to
\mathcal{G}
in the following sense: there is an injective function
f\colon V\to\widetilde{V}
such that
Eve

wins

\mathcal{G}
from
v
if and only if she
wins

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}
from
f(v)
.

Proof B.2
.

We define
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}
as follows. We let its set of vertices be
\widetilde{V}=V\cup E
. Vertices of the form
v\in V
will correspond to vertices coming from
\mathcal{G}
, and vertices
e\in E
will be intermediate vertices added to force the
suitability for transformations
property. We let
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\widetilde{V}_{\mathrm{Adam}}}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V_{\mathrm{Adam}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\widetilde{V}_{\mathrm{Eve}}}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V_{\mathrm{Eve}}}\cup E
. If
e=v\xrightarrow{c}v^{\prime}
is an edge in
\mathcal{G}
, we add the edges
v\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}e
and
e\xrightarrow{c}v^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
.
It is clear that
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}
is
suitable for transformations
and that
Eve

wins

\mathcal{G}
from
v
if and only if she
wins

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}
from
v
.

ACD-HD-Rabin-transform-for-games.

As discussed in Section
5.3
, the
ACD-HD-Rabin-transform
of a
game

\mathcal{G}
does not always induce an
HD-for-games mapping

\varphi\colon{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{Rabin}}(\mathcal{G})}\to\mathcal{G}
, and
\mathcal{G}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{Rabin}}(\mathcal{G})}
do not necessarily have the same
winner
. This is to be expected, as the
ACD-HD-Rabin-transform
does not take into account the partition into
Eve
and
Adam
nodes. In this paragraph we propose a small modification on the transformation to obtain a correct transformation for games.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a
game
. If there is an edge
v\xrightarrow{}v^{\prime}
with
v\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V_{\mathrm{Adam}}}
, we say that
v^{\prime}
is an
A-successor
. We remark that if
\mathcal{G}
is
suitable for transformations
, an
A-successor
is controlled by
Eve
and has a unique predecessor.
We let
\immediate\immediate\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}V_{\mathrm{A}\mathchar 45\relax\mathrm{succ}}}
be the set of
A-successor
of
\mathcal{G}
and
\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}V_{\mathrm{normal}}}=V\setminus{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V_{\mathrm{A}\mathchar 45\relax\mathrm{succ}}}
. If
\mathcal{G}
is
suitable for transformations
, for each
v\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V_{\mathrm{A}\mathchar 45\relax\mathrm{succ}}}
we let
\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\mathsf{pred}}(v)
be its unique predecessor.

The idea to define the
ACD-HD-Rabin-transform-for-games

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{parity}}^{\mathsf{game}}(\mathcal{G})}
is the following: starting from the regular
ACD-HD-Rabin-transform

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{Rabin}}(\mathcal{G})}
, we make some local changes to vertices that are
A-successors
. First, if
v\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V_{\mathrm{Adam}}}
, we replace edges of the form
(v,x)\xrightarrow{n}(v^{\prime},x^{\prime})
in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{Rabin}}(\mathcal{G})}
by
(v,x)\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}(v^{\prime},x)
(we forbid Adam to choose how to update the
ACD
-component). If such an edge is followed by
(v^{\prime},x^{\prime})\xrightarrow{n^{\prime}}(v^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime\prime})
in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{Rabin}}(\mathcal{G})}
, then we add
(v^{\prime},x)\xrightarrow{n}(v^{\prime\prime},x^{\prime\prime})
to
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{parity}}^{\mathsf{game}}(\mathcal{G})}
(we note that
v^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V_{\mathrm{Eve}}}
). That is, Eve chooses retroactively how to update the
ACD
-components performing two consecutive updates. We note that the node
n^{\prime}
is not output in the new
game
; this is not a problem, since
n
must be an
ancestor
of
n^{\prime}
(we could say that
n
contains more information regarding the
acceptance condition
).

Remark B.3
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a
game

suitable for transformations
, let
v\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V_{\mathrm{Adam}}}
and
v\xrightarrow{e_{1}}v^{\prime}\xrightarrow{e_{2}}v^{\prime\prime}
be a path of size
2
in
\mathcal{G}
from
v
. It holds:

•

If some
cycle

\ell
contains
e_{2}
, it also contains
e_{1}
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{T}_{v^{\prime}}}
is a
subtree
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{T}_{v}}
.

•

Let
n_{1}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Leaves}}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{T}_{v}})
and
n_{2}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Jump}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{T}_{v^{\prime}}}}(n_{1},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Supp}}(n_{1},e_{1}))
. Then,
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Supp}}(n_{2},e_{2}))
is a
descendant
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Supp}}(n_{1},e_{1}))
in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{T}_{v^{\prime}}}
.

Definition B.4

(ACD-HD-Rabin-transform-for-games)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a
Muller

game

suitable for transformations
.
For each vertex
v\in V
we let
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\eta}{}{}_{v}\colon{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Leaves}}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{T}_{v}})\to\{1,\dots,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{rbw}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{T}_{v}})}\}
be a mapping satisfying Property (
4.52
) from Lemma
4.52
. We define the
ACD-HD-Rabin-transform-for-games
of
\mathcal{G}
to be the
Rabin

transition system

\immediate{\color[rgb]{0.3438,0.0195,0.0273}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{parity}}^{\mathsf{game}}(\mathcal{G})}
defined as follows.

Vertices.

The set of vertices is

\widetilde{V}=\bigcup_{v\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V_{\mathrm{normal}}}}\{v\}\times[1,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{rbw}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{T}_{v}})}]\;\cup\,\bigcup_{v\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V_{\mathrm{A}\mathchar 45\relax\mathrm{succ}}}}\{v\}\times[1,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{rbw}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{T}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{pred}}(v)}})}].

Players partition.

A vertex
(v,x)
belongs to
Eve
if and only if
v
belongs to
Eve
in
\mathcal{G}
.

Initial vertices.

\widetilde{I}=\{(v_{0},x)\mid v_{0}\in I\text{ and }x\in\{1,\dots,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{rbw}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{T}_{v_{0}}})}\}\}
.

Edges and output colours.

Let
e=v\xrightarrow{}v^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{G}
.

•

If
v\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V_{\mathrm{Eve}}}\cap{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V_{\mathrm{normal}}}
, we add
(v,x)\xrightarrow{n}(v^{\prime},x^{\prime})
to
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{parity}}^{\mathsf{game}}(\mathcal{G})}
exactly in the same cases as in the regular
ACD-HD-Rabin-transform
.

•

If
v\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V_{\mathrm{Adam}}}
, we let
(v,x)\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}(v^{\prime},x)
in
\widetilde{E}
for each
x\in\{1,\dots,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{rbw}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{T}_{v}})}\}
.

•

If
v\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V_{\mathrm{A}\mathchar 45\relax\mathrm{succ}}}
, we add
(v,x)\xrightarrow{n}(v^{\prime},x^{\prime})
to
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{parity}}^{\mathsf{game}}(\mathcal{G})}
if in the regular
ACD-HD-Rabin-transform
there is a path of size
2
of the form

({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{pred}}(v),x)\xrightarrow{n}(v,\tilde{x})\xrightarrow{\tilde{n}}(v^{\prime},x^{\prime}).

Formally,

\widetilde{E}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}e=v\xrightarrow{}v^{\prime}\in E\\
v\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V_{\mathrm{normal}}}\end{subarray}}\{e\}\times{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Leaves}}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{T}_{v}})\;\cup\,\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}e=v\xrightarrow{}v^{\prime}\in E\\
v\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V_{\mathrm{A}\mathchar 45\relax\mathrm{succ}}}\end{subarray}}\{e\}\times{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Leaves}}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{T}_{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{pred}}(v)}}).

Rabin condition.

R=\{(G_{n},R_{n})\}_{n\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Nodes}_{\bigcirc}(\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}})}}
, where
G_{n}
and
R_{n}
are defined as follows: Let
n
be a
round
node, and let
n^{\prime}
be any node in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Nodes}(\mathcal{ACD}_{\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}})}
,

\begin{cases}n^{\prime}\in G_{n}&\text{ if }n^{\prime}=n,\\
n^{\prime}\in R_{n}&\text{ if }n^{\prime}\neq n\text{ and }n\text{ is not an {\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}ancestor} of }n^{\prime}.\end{cases}

Correctness of the ACD-HD-Rabin-transform-for-games

See
5.32

Proof B.5
.

The proof is analogous to that of the correctness of the usual
ACD-HD-Rabin-transform
(Proposition
5.27
). We define the mapping
\varphi\colon{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{parity}}^{\mathsf{game}}(\mathcal{G})}\to\mathcal{G}
as
\varphi_{V}(v,x)=v
and
\varphi_{E}(e,l)=e
. It is clear that it is a
weak morphism
, and it
preserve accepting runs
by Lemma
5.28
and Remark
B.3
.

We define a
resolver

(r_{0},r)
simulating
\varphi
similarly to the proof of Proposition
5.27
: We use
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{parity}}(\mathcal{G})}
to guide the resolver. Let
\rho=v_{0}\xrightarrow{}v_{1}re{}\dots
be a run in
\mathcal{G}
, and let
(v_{0},l_{0})\xrightarrow{}(v_{1},l_{1})\xrightarrow{}\dots
be the preimage of this run in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{parity}}(\mathcal{G})}
. We simulate
\rho
in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{parity}}^{\mathsf{game}}(\mathcal{G})}
as follows: We ensure that at every moment
i
, if
v_{i}\notin{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V_{\mathrm{A}\mathchar 45\relax\mathrm{succ}}}
, the current vertex
(v_{i},x_{i})
is such that
x_{i}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\eta}{}{}_{v_{i}}(l_{i})
. There distinguish two cases to simulate the edge
e_{i}=v_{i}\xrightarrow{}v_{i+1}
:

•

If
v_{i}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V_{\mathrm{Adam}}}
, there is a single outgoing edge from
(v_{i},x_{i})
mapped to the edge
v_{i}\xrightarrow{}v_{i+1}
in
\rho
:
(v_{i},x_{i})\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}(v_{i+1},x_{i})
. This must be the edge picked by the
resolver

•

If
v_{i}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V_{\mathrm{Eve}}}
, we pick the edge
(v_{i},x_{i})\xrightarrow{n_{i}}(v_{i+1},x_{x+1})
such that
x_{i+1}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\eta}{}{}_{l_{i+1}}
and
n_{i}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Supp}}(l_{i},e_{i})
.

If
v_{i}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V_{\mathrm{A}\mathchar 45\relax\mathrm{succ}}}
, the vertex
(v_{i},x_{i})
will verify
x_{i}=x_{i-1}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\eta}{}{}_{v_{i}}(l_{i})
. In this case, we pick the edge
(v_{i},x_{i})\xrightarrow{n_{i}}(v_{i+1},x_{x+1})
such that
x_{i+1}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\eta}{}{}_{l_{i+1}}
and
n_{i}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Supp}}(l_{i},e_{i})
. This is indeed an edge appearing in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{parity}}^{\mathsf{game}}(\mathcal{G})}
, as the path
(v_{i-1},x_{i-1})\xrightarrow{n_{i-1}^{\prime}}(v_{i},x_{i}^{\prime})\xrightarrow{n_{i}}(v_{i+1},x_{x+1})
exists in the regular
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{Rabin}}(\mathcal{G})}
, with
x_{i}^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\eta}{}{}_{v_{i}}(l_{i})
.

The
resolver
obtained in this way is
sound for
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{parity}}^{\mathsf{game}}(\mathcal{G})}
, as there is a unique way to simulate edges issued from
Adam
vertices, and the rest of the edges are simulated in the same way as the resolver defined for the regular
ACD-HD-Rabin-transform
, which we proved to be
sound
.

Optimality of the ACD-HD-Rabin-transform-for-games

See
5.37

Proof B.6
.

The vertices of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{parity}}^{\mathsf{game}}(\mathcal{G})}
corresponding to vertices in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V_{\mathrm{normal}}}
are exactly the same that those in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{Rabin}}(\mathcal{G})}
:

\{(v,x)\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{parity}}^{\mathsf{game}}(\mathcal{G})}\mid v\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V_{\mathrm{normal}}}\}=\{(v,x)\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{Rabin}}(\mathcal{G})}\mid v\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V_{\mathrm{normal}}}\}.

Moreover, for
v\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V_{\mathrm{A}\mathchar 45\relax\mathrm{succ}}}
, there is one vertex of the form
(v,x)
for each vertex
({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{pred}}(v),x)
, and each
v\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V_{\mathrm{A}\mathchar 45\relax\mathrm{succ}}}
has exactly one predecessor in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V_{\mathrm{normal}}}
, so we conclude that:

|{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{parity}}^{\mathsf{game}}(\mathcal{G})}|\leq 2\cdot|\{(v,x)\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{parity}}^{\mathsf{game}}(\mathcal{G})}\mid v\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}V_{\mathrm{normal}}}\}|\leq 2\cdot|{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{ACD}_{\mathsf{Rabin}}(\mathcal{G})}|\leq 2\cdot\mathcal{G}^{\prime},

where the last inequality follows from Theorem
5.36
.

Appendix C
Simplifications for prefix-independent conditions

We prove in this appendix results applying to
automata

recognising

prefix-independent
languages and
games
using
prefix-independent
winning conditions. We recall that a language
L\subseteq\Sigma^{\omega}
is
prefix-independent
if for all
w\in\Sigma^{\omega}
and
u\in\Sigma^{*}
,
uw\in L
if and only if
w\in L
.

See
2.5

Proof C.1
.

(r_{0},r)
be a
sound

resolver
for
\mathcal{A}
such that

is

(r_{0},r)

induced by

over

We first show that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{A}_{q}})}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
. Let
w\in\Sigma^{\omega}
be a word accepted from
q
. Then,
w_{0}w
admits an
accepting run
from the original
initial state
(by
prefix-independence
of the
acceptance set
), so
w_{0}w\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
, and by the
prefix-independence
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
,
w\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
too.

For the converse direction, we define a
sound

resolver

(r_{0}^{\prime},r^{\prime})
for
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{A}_{q}}
. We let
r_{0}^{\prime}=q
, and
r^{\prime}(\rho,a)=r(\rho_{0}\rho,a)
be the strategy that acts as the resolver
r
assuming that
\rho_{0}
has happened in the past. It is clear that for every word
w\in\Sigma^{\omega}
, the
run induced by

(r_{0}^{\prime},r^{\prime})
over
w
has a common suffix with the
run induced by

(r_{0},r)
over
w_{0}w
. Therefore, by the
prefix-independence
assumptions:

w\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}\iff w_{0}w\text{ is accepted using }(r_{0},r)\iff w_{0}w\text{ is accepted using }(r_{0}^{\prime},r^{\prime}).

See
3.17

Proof C.2
.

First,
\varphi\colon{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}_{V}}\to{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}_{V^{\prime}}}
is trivially a
weak morphism
. We claim that it
preserves accepting runs
. Let
v\in V
be a state in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
and let

be an

from

. Since all the states are

, there is some

and

. Since

is a
weak morphism
we have that

. It holds that:

w\in\mathbb{W}\overset{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{W}\\
\text{ pref-indep. }\end{subarray}}{\implies}uw\in\mathbb{W}\implies u^{\prime}w^{\prime}\in\mathbb{W}^{\prime}\overset{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbb{W}^{\prime}\\
\text{ pref-indep. }\end{subarray}}{\implies}w^{\prime}\in\mathbb{W}^{\prime},

where the central implication follows from the fact that
\varphi

preserves accepting runs
between
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
and
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
. Therefore
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\varphi}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}(\rho)
is also an
accepting run
.

In the rest of the proof we assume that
\varphi
is an
HD-for-games mapping
, (which covers the
HD
case).
Let
({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}},r)
be a
resolver

sound for
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}

simulating

\varphi\colon\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}\to\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
. We define a
resolver

({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\tilde{r}_{\mathsf{Init}}},\tilde{r})
for the new mapping.
For every state
v
of
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
, we fix a
finite run

\rho_{v}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Path}^{\mathsf{fin}}_{I}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS})}
ending in
v
that is
consistent with

({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}},r)
over some
\rho^{\prime}
, if such a run exists. We let
V_{\mathrm{Reach}}\subseteq V
be the set of vertices for which
\rho_{v}
is well-defined. We note that for each
v^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}
there exists at least one
v\in\varphi^{-1}(v^{\prime})
such that
v\in V_{\mathrm{Reach}}
; indeed, if
\rho^{\prime}_{v^{\prime}}
is a finite run reaching
v^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
, one such
v
is
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Target}}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r}_{\mathpzc{Runs}}(\rho_{v^{\prime}}^{\prime}))
(that is, the vertex to which we arrive in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
when simulating
\rho_{v^{\prime}}^{\prime}
via the original
resolver
). We let
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\tilde{r}_{\mathsf{Init}}}(v^{\prime})
be this vertex.
If
e^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Out}}(v^{\prime})
is an edge in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime}
, we let
\tilde{r}(\varepsilon,e^{\prime})=r(\rho_{v},e^{\prime})
, for
v={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\tilde{r}_{\mathsf{Init}}}(v^{\prime})
.
For
\rho
a non-empty
finite run
starting in
v\in V_{\mathrm{Reach}}
and
e^{\prime}\in E^{\prime}
, we define
\tilde{r}(\rho,e^{\prime})=r(\rho_{v}\rho,e^{\prime})
. If
\rho
starts in
v\notin V_{\mathrm{Reach}}
we let
\tilde{r}(\rho,e^{\prime})
be any edge in
\varphi^{-1}(e^{\prime})
(if
e^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Out}}(\varphi({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Target}}(\rho)))
we pick it in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Out}}({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Target}}(\rho))
). We check that
({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\tilde{r}_{\mathsf{Init}}},\tilde{r})
satisfies the four requirements to be a
resolver
:

1.

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\tilde{r}_{\mathsf{Init}}}(v^{\prime})
has been chosen in
\varphi^{-1}(v^{\prime})
.

2.

\tilde{r}(e^{\prime})
is chosen in
\varphi^{-1}(e^{\prime})
.

3.

Let
e^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Out}}(v^{\prime})
. We have defined
\tilde{r}(\varepsilon,e^{\prime})=r(\rho_{v},e^{\prime})
, where
\rho_{v}
is a finite run
consistent with

r
ending in
v={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}}(v^{\prime})
. By Property
4
of a
resolver
,
r(\rho_{v},e^{\prime})\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Out}}(v)
.

. Let

be a finite run in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathpzc{Run}^{\mathsf{fin}}(\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}^{\prime})}
such that
\rho_{v}
is
consistent with

({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}},r)
over
\rho_{v}^{\prime}
. It is immediate to check that
\rho_{v}\rho
is
consistent with

({\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}r_{\mathsf{Init}}},r)
over
\rho_{v}^{\prime}\rho^{\prime}
. Since
\rho^{\prime}
is
accepting
, we have that
w^{\prime}\in\mathbb{W}^{\prime}
, and by
prefix-independence
of the
acceptance sets
and the fact that
\rho_{v}\rho
is
accepting
if
\rho_{v}^{\prime}\rho^{\prime}
is, we have:

w^{\prime}\in\mathbb{W}^{\prime}\implies u^{\prime}w^{\prime}\in\mathbb{W}^{\prime}\implies uw\in\mathbb{W}\implies w\in\mathbb{W},

so we conclude that
\rho
is
accepting
in
\mathcal{T\hskip-3.1298ptS}
, as we wanted to show.

Appendix D
Simplifying automata with duplicated edges

Given an automaton
\mathcal{A}=(Q,\Sigma,I,\Gamma,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\delta},\mathbb{W})
we say that it has
duplicated edges
if there is some pair of states
q,q^{\prime}\in Q
and two different transitions between them labelled with the same input letter:
q\xrightarrow{a:\alpha}q^{\prime}
,
q\xrightarrow{a:\beta}q^{\prime}
.

As commented in Remark
4.57
, the construction of the
ZT-HD-Rabin-automaton
we have presented potentially introduces
duplicated edges
, which can be seen as an undesirable property (even if some automata models such as the HOA format
[
4
]
allow them). We show next that we can always derive an equivalent automaton without
duplicated edges
. Intuitively, in the Rabin case, if we want to merge two transitions having as output letters
\alpha
and
\beta
, we add a fresh letter
(\alpha\beta)
to label the new transition. For each
Rabin pair
, this new letter will simulate the best of either
\alpha
or
\beta
depending upon the situation.

Proposition D.1

(Simplification of automata)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
Muller
(resp.
Rabin
)
automaton
presenting
duplicated edges
. There exists a
Muller
(resp.
Rabin
) automaton
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
on the same set of states without
duplicated edges
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})}
. Moreover, if
\mathcal{A}
is
history-deterministic
,
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
can be chosen
history-deterministic
. In the Rabin case, the number of
Rabin pairs
is also preserved.

Proof D.2
.

For the Rabin case, let
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
be an automaton that is otherwise as
\mathcal{A}
except that instead of the transitions
\Delta
of
\mathcal{A}
it only has one
a
-transition
q\xrightarrow{a:x}q^{\prime}\in\Delta^{\prime}
(with a fresh colour
x
per transition) per state-pair
q,q^{\prime}
and letter
a\in\Sigma
. That is,
\Delta^{\prime}=\{(q,a,x_{j},q^{\prime})\>:\>(q,a,y,q^{\prime})\in\Delta\text{ for some }y\}
. The new
Rabin condition

\{(G_{1}^{\prime},R_{1}^{\prime}),\ldots,(G_{r}^{\prime},R_{r}^{\prime})\}
is defined as follows. For each transition
q\xrightarrow{a:x}q^{\prime}
:

•

x\in G_{i}^{\prime}
if
q\xrightarrow{a:y}q^{\prime}\in\Delta
for some
y\in G_{i}
,

•

x\in R^{\prime}_{i}
if for all
q\xrightarrow{a:y}q^{\prime}\in\Delta
,
y\in R_{i}
.

We claim that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
. Indeed, if
u\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
, as witnessed by some
run

\rho
and a
Rabin pair

(G_{i},R_{i})
, then the corresponding run
\rho^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
over
u
is also
accepted by
the
Rabin pair

(G_{i}^{\prime},R_{i}^{\prime})
: the transitions of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Inf}}(\rho)\cap G_{i}
induce transitions of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Inf}}(\rho^{\prime})\cap G_{i}^{\prime}
and the fact that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Inf}}(\rho)\cap R_{i}=\emptyset
guarantees that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Inf}}(\rho^{\prime})\cap R_{i}^{\prime}=\emptyset
.

Conversely, if
u\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})}
as witnessed by a run
\rho^{\prime}
and
Rabin pair

(G_{i}^{\prime},R_{i}^{\prime})
,
then there is an accepting run
\rho
over
u
in
\mathcal{A}
: such a run can be obtained by choosing for each transition
q\xrightarrow{a:x}q^{\prime}
of
\rho^{\prime}
where
x\in G_{i}^{\prime}
a transition
q\xrightarrow{a:y}q^{\prime}\in\Delta
such that
y\in G_{i}
, which exists by definition of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, for each transition
q\xrightarrow{a:x}q^{\prime}
where
x\notin G_{i}\cup R_{i}
a transition
q\xrightarrow{q,y}q^{\prime}\in\Delta
such that
y\notin R_{i}
, which also exists by definition of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, and for other transitions
q\xrightarrow{a:x}q^{\prime}
(that is, those for which
x\in R_{i}^{\prime}
) an arbitrary transition
q\xrightarrow{a:y}q^{\prime}\in\Delta
. Since
\rho^{\prime}
is accepting, we have
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Inf}}(\rho^{\prime})\cap G_{i}\neq\emptyset
and
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Inf}}(\rho)\cap R_{i}=\emptyset
, that is,
\rho
is also accepting.

For the
Muller
case, the argument is even simpler. As above, we consider
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
that is otherwise like
\mathcal{A}
except that instead of the transitions
\Delta
of
\mathcal{A}
, it only has one
a
-transition
q\xrightarrow{a:x}q^{\prime}\in\Delta^{\prime}
(with a fresh colour per transition) per state-pair
q,q^{\prime}
and the accepting condition is defined as follows. A set of transitions
T
is accepting if and only if for each
t=q\xrightarrow{a:x}q^{\prime}\in T
there is a non-empty set
S_{t}\subseteq\{q\xrightarrow{a:y}q^{\prime}\in\Delta\}
such that
\bigcup_{t\in T}S_{t}
is accepting in
\mathcal{A}
. In other words, a set of transitions in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is accepting if for each transition we can choose a non-empty subset of the original transitions in
\mathcal{A}
that form an accepting run in
\mathcal{A}
.

We claim that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
. Indeed if
u\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A})}
, as witnessed by some run
\rho
, the run
\rho^{\prime}
that visits the same sequence of states in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is accepting as witnessed by the transitions that occur infinitely often in
\rho
.

Conversely, assume
u\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})}
, as witnessed by a run
\rho^{\prime}
and a non-empty subset
S_{t}
for each transition
t
that occurs infinitely often in
\rho^{\prime}
such that
\bigcup_{t\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Inf}}(\rho)}S_{t}
is accepting in
\mathcal{A}
. Then there is an accepting run
\rho
over
u
in
\mathcal{A}
that visits the same sequence of states as
\rho^{\prime}
and chooses instead of a transition
t\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Inf}}(\rho)
each transition in
S_{t}
infinitely often, and otherwise takes an arbitrary transition. The set of transitions
\rho
visits infinitely often is exactly
\bigcup_{t\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathsf{Inf}}(\rho)}S_{t}
, and is therefore accepting.

Finally, observe that in both cases, if
\mathcal{A}
is
HD
, then the automaton
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
without duplicate edges is also
HD
since
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
is obtained from
\mathcal{A}
by merging transitions. Indeed, the
resolver

r
of
\mathcal{A}
induces a
resolver

r^{\prime}
for
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
by outputting the unique transition with the same letter and state-pair as
r
. By the same argument as above, the run induced by
r^{\prime}
is accepting if and only if the run induced by
r
is.

Example D.3
.

The
ZT-HD-Rabin-automaton
from Figure
14
has
duplicated transitions
. In Figure
19
we present an equivalent
HD

Rabin

automaton
without duplicates. For this, we have merged the self-loops in state
1
labelled with
a
and
b
respectively. We have added the
output colours

(\alpha\beta)
and
(\theta\xi)
.
The new
Rabin pairs
are given by:

G_{\beta}^{\prime}=\{\beta,(\alpha\beta)\}
,

R_{\beta}^{\prime}=\{\alpha,\lambda,\xi,\zeta\}
,

G_{\lambda}^{\prime}=\{\lambda\}
,

R_{\lambda}^{\prime}=\{\alpha,\beta,(\alpha\beta),\theta\}
.

1
2
a:(\theta\xi)

b:(\alpha\beta)

c:\alpha

c:\lambda

a:\lambda

b:\alpha

c:\zeta

Figure 19
:
The simplified
ZT-HD-Rabin-automaton
.

Appendix E
Proofs for Section
6.1.1

Proof E.1

(Proof of Propositions
6.2
and
6.3
)
.

We prove it for the
Rabin
case,
Streett
conditions being the dual notion.

If all
round nodes
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}=(N={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}N_{\bigcirc}}\sqcup{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}N_{\Box}},\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\preceq}},{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}:N\to{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}2^{\Gamma}_{+}})
have at most one
child
, we define a family of
Rabin pairs

R=\{(G_{n},R_{n})\mid n\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}N_{\bigcirc}}\}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Rabin}(R)}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}(\mathcal{F})}
as follows: for each
round node

n\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}N_{\bigcirc}}
, we add a
Rabin pair

(G_{n},R_{n})
. We let
G_{n}=\Gamma\setminus{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)
. In order to define
R_{n}
, we observe that
n
has at most one child
n^{\prime}
, and we define
R_{n}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)\setminus{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n^{\prime})
, for
n^{\prime}
the only
child
of
n
, if it exists, or
R_{n}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)
if
n
has no
children
at all. This is, the pair
(G_{n},R_{n})

accepts
the sets of colours
A\subseteq\Gamma
that contain some of the colours that disappear in the step
n\rightarrow n^{\prime}
and none of the colours appearing up in the
tree
. We show that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Rabin}(R)}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}(\mathcal{F})}
. Let
A
be a set of colours. If
A\in\mathcal{F}
, let
n
be a maximal node (for
\mathrel{{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\preceq}}
) containing
A
. It is a
round node
and there is some colour
c\in A
not appearing in the only child of
n
. Therefore,
c\in G_{n}
and
A\cap R_{n}=\emptyset
. Conversely, if
A\notin\mathcal{F}
, then for every
round node

n
with a child
n^{\prime}
, either
A\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n^{\prime})
(and therefore
A\cap G_{n}=\emptyset
) or
A\nsubseteq{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)
(and in that case
A\cap R_{n}\neq\emptyset
).

We remark that this construction uses more
Rabin pairs
than necessary, since we could reuse Rabin pairs for nodes that are in the same level and that are not siblings.

Conversely, suppose that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}(\mathcal{F})}={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Rabin}(R)}
for the
Rabin language associated to

R=\{(G_{1},R_{1}),\dots,(G_{r},R_{r})\}
. If
n\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
is a
round node
(
A={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)\in\mathcal{F}
), then its label
A
contains some colours that belongs to
G_{i_{1}},\dots,G_{i_{k}}
and none belonging to
R_{i_{1}},\dots,R_{i_{k}}
for some
i_{1},\dots,i_{k}
,
k\geq 1
. A child of
n
must not have these colours, so the only maximal subset of
A
that is not in
\mathcal{F}
is
A\setminus(G_{i_{1}}\cup\dots\cup G_{i_{k}})
.

Proof E.2

(Proof of Proposition
6.4
)
.

We assume
\Gamma\in\mathcal{F}
(
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\min_{\mathcal{F}}}=0
), the other case is symmetric.

Assume that
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
has a single branch of length
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\max_{\mathcal{F}}}+1
. We define a mapping
\phi\colon\Gamma\to[0,{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\max_{\mathcal{F}}}]
as follows: for each colour
c\in\Gamma
we let
n_{c}
be the
deepest
node in
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
containing
c
, and we define
\phi(c)={\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n_{c})
. It is easy to check that for all
w\in\Gamma^{\omega}
,
w\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}(\mathcal{F})}
if and only if
\phi(w)\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{parity}}
.

Conversely, assume that we can assign colours to the elements of
\Gamma
by
\phi:\Gamma\rightarrow[0,d]
, whose corresponding parity language is
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\textsf{Muller}(\mathcal{F})}
. We show that any node of the
Zielonka tree

{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
has at most one child. Indeed, let
n\in N
and let
c\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)
such that
\phi(c)=\min\{\phi(c)\mid c\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)\}
. We suppose that
\phi(c)
is odd (the proof is symmetric for
\phi(c)
even). Let
p=\min\{\phi(c^{\prime})\mid c^{\prime}\in{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)\text{ and }\phi(c^{\prime})\text{ even}\}
. In every child of
n
the elements with a smaller colour than
p
must disappear, so the set of elements
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)\cap\{c\in\Gamma\mid\phi(c)\geq p\}
is the only maximal subset of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\nu}{}{}(n)
belonging to
\mathcal{F}
. Moreover, in the label of the child of
n
there is at least one colour less, so the
height
of
{\color[rgb]{0.0195,0.2109,0.2539}\mathcal{Z}_{\mathcal{F}}}
will be at most
d+1
.
